# Advances in Systems Analysis, Software Engineering, and High Performance Computing (ASASEHPC) Book Series

Vijayan Sugumaran (Oakland University, Rochester, USA)ISSN: 2327-3453 |
| --- | --- |

### Mission

The theory and practice of computing applications and distributed systems has emerged as one of the key areas of research driving innovations in business, engineering, and science. The fields of software engineering, systems analysis, and high performance computing offer a wide range of applications and solutions in solving computational problems for any modern organization.

The Advances in Systems Analysis, Software Engineering, and High Performance Computing (ASASEHPC) Book Series brings together research in the areas of distributed computing, systems and software engineering, high performance computing, and service science. This collection of publications is useful for academics, researchers, and practitioners seeking the latest practices and knowledge in this field.

 
| 

### Coverage

*   Enterprise Information Systems
*   Software Engineering
*   Engineering Environments
*   Storage Systems
*   Performance Modelling
*   Distributed Cloud Computing
*   Computer Graphics
*   Computer System Analysis
*   Human-Computer Interaction
*   Metadata and Semantic Web

*   Chao Zhang  
    Shanxi University, China
*   Wentao Li  
    Southwest University, China

In an era where data reigns supreme, the imperative for decision-makers to grapple with the vast and intricate landscape of information has become an unparalleled challenge. Big Data Quantification for Complex Decision-Making emerges as a beacon in this data-centric age, unraveling the methodologies, techniques, and frameworks essential for distilling profound insights from colossal datasets. As editors, we embark on this intellectual journey with the conviction that understanding and harnessing big data is not just a technological evolution but a fundamental transformation in the way decisions are made.

The core aspiration of this book is to illuminate the dynamic interplay between big data and the nuanced process of decision-making, particularly in uncertain contexts. Our scope encompasses a spectrum of objectives, each contributing synergistically to the advancement of research and knowledge in this field. We delve into the significance of big data in decision-making, unravel methodological intricacies, adeptly handle uncertainty, merge theoretical underpinnings with real-world applications, contribute meaningfully to ongoing research, provide pragmatic guidance for practitioners, and shed light on prospective directions and implications. At its essence, this book seeks to endow readers with a comprehensive toolbox, empowering effective navigation through the data-rich landscape of modern decision-making.

The unique blend of theoretical frameworks, practical applications, and forward-thinking perspectives forms the cornerstone of our approach. By seamlessly integrating these elements, our goal is to deepen comprehension of how big data can be strategically leveraged to address multifaceted challenges and facilitate well-considered decisions in a world characterized by pervasive uncertainty.

Our intended audience is diverse, reflecting the broad impact of big data on decision-making across various domains. Researchers and academics in data science, decision science, machine learning, artificial intelligence, and related fields will find this book to be a valuable reference, offering insights into challenges, techniques, and prospects inherent in harnessing big data. Practitioners and professionals engaged in data analytics, business intelligence, risk management, and strategic decision-making stand to gain pragmatic insights and methodologies for crafting well-informed decisions within volatile contexts. Lastly, graduate students exploring data science, decision analysis, and related realms will discover in this book a foundational resource that equips them with the knowledge and tools to address intricate real-world challenges.

The thematic breadth of this book is vast, covering topics such as big data analytics, uncertainty modeling, decision-making under uncertainty, fuzzy decision-making, formal concept analysis, rough set theory, deep learning models, neural network models, natural language processing, three-way decisions, multi-granularity analysis, and intelligent control systems. Each chapter contributes a unique perspective, enriching the collective understanding of how big data can be quantified for complex decision-making.

As editors, we express our sincere appreciation to the contributors who have brought their expertise to this endeavor. We believe that “Big Data Quantification for Complex Decision-Making” will serve as a catalyst for advancing both scholarly inquiry and practical applications in this dynamic intersection of big data and decision-making.

Chapter 1: Group-oriented Multi-attribute Decision-Making Method based on Dominance Rough Set Theory

Authored by Bin Yu, Zeyu Xiao, and Yinglong Dai from China, this chapter introduces the Group-oriented Multi-attribute Decision-Making based on mixed advantage-disadvantage degree (GOMADMMADD) method. The GOMADMMADD method addresses the challenge of group-oriented decision analysis by proposing concepts such as “local advantage-disadvantage degrees” and “advantage-disadvantage degrees.” The authors go on to enhance this method, introducing the decision-making method based on dominance-based rough sets (GMADMDRS), which optimizes the GMADMDRS method using “advantage” and “disadvantage” neighborhood operators. This optimization involves defining the “advantage-disadvantage neighborhood degree” (ADND), providing a solution for group-oriented multi-attribute decision-making with enhanced efficiency.

Chapter 2: A Multi-Granularity Triangular Fuzzy Approach for Diabetes Blood Glucometer Selection using PROMETHEE and Three-Way Decision

This chapter, authored by Liyin Wang, Yuting Cheng, Xueqing Fan, Anna Wang, and Hansen Zhao address the selection of a blood glucose meter for diabetic patients. Combining triangular fuzzy (TF) logic, multi-granularity (MG) three-way decision (TWD), and rough set theory, the authors develop a comprehensive evaluation model. TF logic models indicators and determines weights, while TWD handles incomplete and uncertain information. The PROMETHEE method is then introduced to evaluate and rank alternatives, providing personalized recommendations for diabetic patients based on the model's effectiveness in resolving the blood glucose meter selection problem.

Chapter 3: Prediction of Parkinson's Disease Severity Based on Feature Optimization

Authored by Tao Zhang, Zaifa Xue, and Zesheng Huo from Yanshan University, China, this chapter focuses on predicting the severity of Parkinson's disease (PD) using voice features. The authors propose the LDFSF and GWT-RF-Att methods for feature selection and transformation, respectively. The LDFSF method uses a dynamic feature selection strategy based on SOM clustering, while the GWT-RF-Att method leverages graph wavelet transform and random forest with an attention mechanism. These methods outperform existing comparison methods, demonstrating their effectiveness in improving prediction performance on the Parkinson's telemonitoring dataset.

Chapter 4: Creating a Data Lakehouse for a South African Government-Sector Learning Control Enforcing Quality Control for Incremental Extract-Load-Transform Pipe

In this chapter, authored by Dharmesh Dhabliya, Vivek Veeraiah, Sukhvinder Singh Dari, Jambi Ratna Raja Kumar, Ritika Dhabliya, Sabyasachi Pramanik, and Ankur Gupta from India, the focus is on creating a data lakehouse system for a South African Government sector. The Durban University of Technology is engaged in this project, emphasizing the importance of data quality in the efficiency of the lakehouse system. The chapter explores quality control methods for ingestion-layer pipelines, assessing metrics such as completeness, accuracy, integrity, correctness, and timeliness. The framework's efficiency is demonstrated through implementation on a sample semi-structured dataset, with suggestions for future development and integration of data from diverse sources.

Chapter 5: Decision Making based on Partition Order Product Space

Authored by Yi Xu from Anhui University, China, this chapter introduces the concept of partition order product space, combining multilevel granular structure with multiview granular structure. Two search algorithms, depth-first searching and breadth-first searching, are proposed to find solutions in this space. The chapter also explores three-way decisions based on partition order product space, presenting four multiview sequential three-way decisions. The approach represents an effective and reasonable way to describe and solve problems through granular computing principles.

Chapter 6: Strategy Selection and Outcome Evaluation of Change-Based Three-Way Decisions Based on Reinforcement Learning

Chunmao Jiang from Fujian University of Technology, China, enhances the trisecting-acting-outcome (TAO) model of three-way decision-making (3WD) in this chapter. The novel approach introduces Q-learning in reinforcement learning for strategy selection and outcome prediction. Changes in tripartition and actions are reinterpreted as states and actions, with cumulative prospect theory used to quantify rewards. The Q-learning algorithm iteratively determines action sets to achieve target rewards efficiently, offering a cost-effective and psychologically attuned action set for predicting utility in change-based 3WD.

Chapter 7: The Extension of MAIRCA Based on Fuzzy Number: Smart Supplier Selection

Hatice Özensel and Burak Efe from Necmettin Erbakan University, Turkey, address the supplier selection phase in supply chain management. They propose an interval-valued q-rung orthopair fuzzy (IVqROF) number-based MAIRCA method for ranking alternative suppliers. This extension of the MAIRCA method utilizes fuzzy numbers but introduces IVqROF numbers, enhancing the evaluation of expert opinions. The study examines smart supplier selection under Industry 4.0 criteria, providing insights into the supplier selection problem faced by an automotive sub-industry company.

Chapter 8: Review of Chinese Text Mining in Agriculture

Xinyue Zhao and Yunsheng Song, from China, conduct a comprehensive review of text mining in Chinese agriculture. The chapter analyzes the characteristics of agricultural text, covers preprocessing of Chinese agricultural text, text representation, agricultural text classification, and text matching. Additionally, the authors delve into the application scenarios of agricultural text mining, agricultural intelligent service, and the current research status in this field.

Chapter 9: A Snapshot Survey of Data Acquisition Forms in Multi-Attribute Decision-Making Studies

Authored by Yuge Niu, Kexin Liu, Fanghui Lu, and Jiayi Zhang, this chapter addresses the need for large-scale data collection in Multi-Attribute Decision-Making (MADM) analysis. The authors provide a snapshot survey, summarizing classic papers, open datasets, remote sensing data, sentiment analysis, and questionnaire survey data collection forms in MADM research. By evaluating classic papers, open datasets, and various data collection methods, the chapter aids researchers and professionals in selecting and designing effective data collection approaches, ensuring reliable and impactful decision-making.

Chapter 10: Research on Knowledge Representation and Reasoning Based on Decision Implication

Shaoxia Zhang from Shanxi University of Finance and Economics, China, explores knowledge representation and reasoning based on decision implication. The Decision Implication Canonical Basis (DICB) is introduced, offering a set of decision implications with information integrity and extreme simplicity. The chapter conducts a systematic and in-depth study on the capability of knowledge representation, focusing on the adaptability of decision implication to incomplete formal context. The research extends to reasoning based on decision implications, providing insights into the effectiveness of this approach in formal contexts.

In the closing pages of “Big Data Quantification for Complex Decision-Making,” we reflect upon the extensive exploration into the symbiotic relationship between big data and the intricate process of decision-making. As the landscape of information continues to evolve, the imperative for decision-makers to navigate the complexities of data has become paramount. This book, conceived as a guiding light in the era of data dominance, has unfolded an array of methodologies, techniques, and frameworks essential for extracting profound insights from colossal datasets.

The essence of our collective effort lies in recognizing that grappling with big data is not merely a technological evolution but a fundamental shift in the very fabric of decision-making. The core aspiration of this book was to shed light on the dynamic interplay between big data and nuanced decision-making, especially in the face of uncertainty. Each chapter has been meticulously crafted to contribute synergistically, advancing research and knowledge in this transformative field.

From decoding the significance of big data in decision-making to untangling methodological intricacies, handling uncertainty adeptly, and bridging theoretical foundations with real-world applications, this book seeks to empower its readers. The comprehensive toolbox provided within these pages equips individuals across various domains with the skills to navigate the data-rich landscape and make well-informed decisions.

Our unique approach, fusing theoretical frameworks, practical applications, and forward-thinking perspectives, has aimed to deepen the comprehension of strategically leveraging big data to address multifaceted challenges. This book stands as a testament to the belief that understanding the interplay of big data and decision-making is pivotal for ushering in a new era of informed choices amid pervasive uncertainty.

The intended audience, ranging from seasoned researchers to practitioners and aspiring graduate students, reflects the book's broad impact on decision-making across diverse domains. As editors, we express our gratitude to the contributors who have infused their expertise into this collective work. We envision “Big Data Quantification for Complex Decision-Making” as not just a compilation of knowledge but a catalyst for future exploration and innovation in the dynamic realm where big data intersects with decision-making.

In closing, we invite readers to embark on their own intellectual journeys, armed with the insights and tools provided in this book, as they navigate the uncharted waters of big data and continue to shape the future landscape of decision-making.

﻿

# Chapter 1

*   Yu Bin  
    College of Information Science and Engineering, Hunan Normal University, China
*   Xiao Zeyu  
    College of Information Science and Engineering, Hunan Normal University, China
*   Dai Yinglong  
    College of Information Science and Engineering, Hunan Normal University, China

ABSTRACT

This chapter proposes group-oriented multi-attribute decision-making based on mixed advantage-disadvantage degree (GOMADMMADD) method to deal with group-oriented decision analysis. Based on the strict attribute relationship between group members, this method proposes the concepts of “local advantage-disadvantage degrees” and “advantage-disadvantage degrees,” which solves the challenge of group-oriented multi-attribute decision-making (GOMADM). However, this method still has the problem that the number of groups to be evaluated increases exponentially. Therefore, the authors first improved the GOMADMMADD method and proposed decision-making method based on dominance-based rough sets (GMADMDRS). Then, the “advantage” neighborhood operators and “disadvantage” neighborhood operators of groups are introduced to define the “advantage-disadvantage neighborhood degree” (ADND), and the GMADMDRS method is optimized by using ADND. The experimental results show that both GOMADMMADD method and GMADMDRS method effectively evaluate the population, and the optimized GMADMDRS algorithm is consistent in the experimental results, and the time performance has been greatly improved, and the computational performance has been significantly improved, which has practical significance to solve the problem of exponential growth of the number of groups to be evaluated. These methods provide a novel perspective and effective method for group oriented multi-attribute decision-making.

Keywords Dominance-based rough set, GOMADM, GOMADMMADD, GMADMDRS, Optimization of GMADMDRS

INTRODUCTION

Problem Background

Group evaluation and ranking have always been required. In the team ranking, it is necessary to evaluate the team as a whole, in financial investment, portfolio investment is a favorable way to avoid risks, portfolio investment needs to evaluate the financial products of the portfolio as a whole, when the feature selection reveals the correlation between the decision variables and the description variables, consider the interaction between the decision variables, and the overall evaluation of the combination decision variables has far-reaching significance. Evaluate and rank groups as a whole, which are widely used in team ranking, portfolio investment, feature selection, group identification, and more.

A Brief Review of DRSA

In real-life scenarios, decision-makers often encounter problems involving imprecise or fuzzy information. To address such Multi-Attribute Decision Making (MADM) problems, Pawlak introduced rough sets ([Pawlak, 1982](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r28)), later extended by various scholars due to its equivalence relationship limitations. In dealing with dual-level hesitant fuzzy language elements (DHHFLTS), Yu et al. proposed a λ-Rough set model, applying it to multi-attribute decision-making ([Yu et al., 2019a](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r39); [Yu et al., 2019b](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r38)). Gireesha et al. addressed the uncertainty in cloud service provider evaluation by proposing a method based on improved interval-valued intuitionistic fuzzy set weighted aggregation and product evaluation ([Gireesha et al., 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r9)). In the context of bone transplantation selection, Zhang et al. obtained criteria weights using the variable precision intuitionistic fuzzy rough set (CVPIFRS) model and proposed a new MADM method combining CVPIFRS with TOPSIS ([Zhang et al., 2020b](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r49)). Gou et al. introduced an extended probabilistic dual-level language VIKOR method for MCDM in smart healthcare ([Gou et al., 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r10)). Dogan proposed a fuzzy MCDM method based on spherical fuzzy AHP for technology group screening ([Dogan, 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r7)). Zhang et al. addressed the limitations of distance and similarity in DHHFLTS based on algebra and proposed the DHHFL-ELECTRE II method using cosine similarity for financial logistics enterprises' MADM (Zhang et al., 2022). Kang et al. presented a multi-attribute prediction analysis model in fuzzy information systems based on a new fuzzy rough set theory ([Kang et al., 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r19)).

Greco et al. introduced an extension of rough sets called Dominance-based Rough Set Approach (DRSA), where condition attributes are criteria ranked by preference ([Greco et al., 1999](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r13); [Greco et al., 2001](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r14)). Later, they proposed the dominance rough fuzzy set model by integrating DRSA into the fuzzy neighborhood ([Greco et al., 2006](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r12)). Zhang et al. established a general framework of hesitant fuzzy language rough set, constructing decision rules based on hesitant fuzzy language information ([Zhang et al., 2018](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r45)). In the context of hesitant fuzzy language, they proposed a hybrid model incorporating multi-granularity decision theory rough set into the dual universe framework ([Zhang et al., 2020d](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r46)). Ahmad et al. addressed the key step of calculating upper and lower approximations in DRSA by proposing an effective method that directly computes them without considering irrelevant objects ([Ahmad et al., 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r1)). Huang et al. focused on applying DRSA to process composite ordered decision systems, including categorical, numerical, set-valued, interval-valued, and missing attributes ([Huang et al., 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r15)). Palangetić et al. advanced the theory by considering additional properties and explored the application of Ordered Weighted Average (OWA) operators to fuzzy DRSA ([Palangetić et al., 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r27)). Błaszczyński et al. tested a new dataset for auto loan applications using the Dominance-based Rough Set Balanced Rule Ensemble (DRSA-BRE) for financial fraud prediction ([Błaszczyński et al., 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r4)). Kusunoki et al. addressed the issue of too strict approximation under classified data by using variable precision DRSA (VP-DRSA) and variable consistency DRSA (VC-DRSA) parameters to enhance lower approximation in empirical risk minimization ([Kusunoki et al., 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r20)).

A Brief Review of GOMADM

In recent decades, the field of Multi-Attribute Decision Making (MADM) has undergone significant developments, finding applications across various domains. Real-world decision-making problems are often intricate and cannot be adequately addressed through a single criterion. To tackle multi-attribute problems, MADM methods have been developed, greatly enhancing the efficacy of decision-making processes. As a cognitive process, MADM involves selecting the optimal solution by evaluating alternatives in the presence of multiple attributes. Broadly, MADM methods can be categorized into two groups (Figueira et al., 2005): (1) Outranking Techniques: Outranking methods rely on pairwise comparisons of alternatives. Examples include TOPSIS (Technique for Order Preference by Similarity to an Ideal Solution) ([Behzadian et al., 2012](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r2); [Opricovic & Tzeng, 2004](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r25)), VIKOR (Vlsekriterijumska Optimizacija I Kompromisno Resenje) ([Opricovic & Tzeng, 2004](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r25)), ELECTRE (Elimination Et Choix Traduisant la Realit) ([Roy, 1991](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r31)), and PROMETHEE (Preference Ranking Organization Method for Enrichment Evaluations) ([Brans & Vincke, 1985](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r5); [Murat et al., 2015](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r24)). (2) Multi-Attribute Utility and Value Theories: This category involves assigning utility to each alternative, encompassing methods such as UTA (UTilites Additives) (Jacquetlagreze & Siskos, 1982), AHP (Analytic Hierarchy Process) ([Saaty, 1977](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r32)), ANP (Analytic Network Process) ([Saaty, 2004](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r33)), and MACBETH (Measuring Attractiveness by a Categorical Based Evaluation Technique) (Costa, 2010).

With the increasing level of detail in data analysis, Group-Oriented Multi-Attribute Decision Making (GOMADM) is emerging as a trend compared to traditional multi-attribute decision-making. In team ranking, the evaluation of the entire team is essential. Portfolio investment, as a risk-avoidance method in financial investment, requires evaluating combined financial products as a whole. The significance of evaluating combined decision variables as a whole becomes apparent when feature selection reveals correlations between decision variables and descriptive variables. Cohorts are evaluated and ranked as a whole, leading to extensive research in team ranking (Das et al., 2022), portfolio investing ([Zhang et al., 2020c](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r51); [Vo et al., 2019](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r37); [Paiva et al., 2019](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r26)), feature selection ([Mnich & Rudnicki, 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r23); [Pedersen & Vatn, 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r29)), group identification ([Zhang et al., 2018](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r45); [Leng et al., 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r21)), among others.

Group evaluation often involves ranking individuals using multi-attribute decision-making methods and then deriving overall performance based on individual performance. Various methods have been proposed to address the limitations of traditional MADM in group evaluation and ranking problems. These include the MADM integration method proposed by Hussain et al. ([Hussain et al., 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r16)), average formula ranking aggregation, and weighted average consensus ranking aggregation methods by Tidke et al. ([Tidke et al., 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r36)), a new MADM method proposed by Yu et al. to mitigate the influence of attribute weight ([Yu et al., 2022b](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r42)), and a group-oriented multi-attribute decision-making method proposed by Yu et al. ([Yu et al., 2022a](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r40)), further optimized in ([Yu et al., 2023b](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#r41)).

The Motives and Contribution of This Chapter

In this chapter, a group oriented multi-attribute method is designed to solve the problem by using the dominant rough set theory. Specifically, we list several main research motivations of this chapter below.

*   1\. With the improvement of the level of detail of data analysis, compared with traditional multi-attribute methods, GOMADM methods are becoming a trend.
*   2\. Rough set theory is an effective tool to compare the advantages and disadvantages between groups. Therefore, it is necessary to design a group oriented multi-attribute decision-making method based on rough set theory.
*   3\. After designing the GOMADMMADD method, the method has the problems that the number of groups to be evaluated increases exponentially and it is not easy to optimize, and the GOMADMMADD method is improved.

In light of the above study motives, we further sum up evident contributions of this chapter below.

*   1\. Based on the dominance rough set theory, the GOMADM MADD method is designed to meet the challenge of decision analysis, and expand the application prospect of dominance rough set.
*   2\. In order to solve the problem that GOMADMMADD method cannot optimize the exponential explosion problem, the GOMADMMADD method is improved to GMADMDRS method. The experimental results show that the GMADMDRS method is reasonable and lays a solid foundation for solving the exponential explosion problem.
*   3\. In order to optimize the GMADMDRS method, this paper designs the superior and inferior potential neighborhood degree (ADND) which is suitable for the GMADMDRS method based on the dominance rough set, and fully studies the properties of the measure, which expands the applicability of the dominance rough set model.
*   4\. In order to solve the problem that the number of candidate groups increases exponentially, the GMADMDRS method is optimized by using the characteristics of ADND. While ensuring the consistency of the results before and after the optimization of the GMADMDRS method, the time performance of the optimized GMADMDRS method has been greatly improved compared with the optimized GMADMDRS method.

Based on the above literature analysis, we have arranged the structure of this chapter as follows. In Section 2, we reviewed information systems and proposed Advantage (Disadvantage) Neighborhood and (Local) Advantage Disadvantage Degrees based on the advantage of rough sets. In the next section, in order to further optimize, we proposed the Advantage (Disadvantage) Neighborhood Operators and Advantage Disadvantage Neighborhood Degrees. In Section 4, we designed the GOMADMMADD method based on the Advantage (Disadvantage) Neighborhood and (Local) Advantage Disadvantage Degrees, and further improved the GMADMDRS method, and further optimize this method based on Advantage (Disadvantage) Neighborhood Operators and Advantage Disadvantage Neighborhood Degrees. In Section 5, a real case study was conducted using a UCI dataset extracted from real data. Finally, some conclusions were summarized and future research issues were proposed.

BASIC KNOWLEDGE

Information System

Given the apparent advantages possessed by information system (IS) in describing research objects primarily characterized by symbolic values, the basic description of IS is as follows.

An information system (IS) is described as a 4-tuple {U,Q,V,f}, where

*   U, called the universe of discourse, is a non-empty finite object set; 
    *   Q is a non-empty finite attribute set;
*   ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m01](i/ch001.m01.png), where V[a] is the set of values of the attribute a;
*   f: U×Q→V[a], is a map such that f(x,a)∈V[a] for every x∈U and a∈Q.

For any x∈U, we use the notation ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m02](i/ch001.m02.png) to represent the set {a∈Q|f(x,a)=1}, that is, ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m03](i/ch001.m03.png) is the set of all attributes possessing the object x. The cardinality of a finite set X in this study is written as |X|.

Advantage (Disadvantage) Neighborhood and (Local) Advantage-Disadvantage Degrees

This section constitutes the core theoretical foundation for implementing the GOMADMMADD method, including the advantage (disadvantage) neighborhood, the (local) advantage-disadvantage degrees.

Based on the comparison of advantages (disadvantages) between the objects, the definition of the advantage (disadvantage) neighborhood is as follows.

*   Definition 2.1: Let S\={U,A,f,V} be an IS and x∈U. Then, 
    *   (1) ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m04](i/ch001.m04.png) and ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m05](i/ch001.m05.png).
    *   (2) ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m06](i/ch001.m06.png) and ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m07](i/ch001.m07.png).

Remark: In Definition 2.1(1), if ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m08](i/ch001.m08.png), x,y∈U, then we call the object y is better than object x (or object x is worse than object y), and we are more satisfied with y. From the perspective of inclusion between attribute subsets, the advantages and disadvantages between objects are strictly described. A subset ↑x of U is called an advantage neighborhood of object x, that is, ↑x describes all objects that are better than or the same as x. A subset ↓x of U is called a disadvantage neighborhood of object x, that is, ↓x describes all objects that are worse than or the same as x.

In Definition 2.1(2), A subset ↑[+]x of U is called an advantage neighborhood of object x, that is, ↑[+]x describes all objects that are better than x. A subset ↓[+]x of U is called a disadvantage neighborhood of object x, that is, ↓[+]x describes all objects that are worse than x.

According to Definition 2.1, We define some special objects.

*   Definition 2.2: Let S\={U,Q,V,f} be an IS, and x,y∈U. 
    *   (1) If for any y∈U, x∈↑y or y∈↓x, then x is called a Maximum object, also called absolute maximum.
    *   (2) If for any y∈U, x∈↓y or y∈↑x, then x is called a Minimum object, also called absolute minimum.
    *   (3) If x\=↑x, then x is called a maximum object, also called local maximum, Max[U] is the set of all maximum objects.
    *   (4) If x\=↓x, then x is called a minimum object, also called local minimum, Min[U] is the set of all minimum objects.

Based on the above definition, the following properties can be obtained

Hence, the smaller the LADD of the object group is, the better the object group in an IS will be.

By Definition 2.4 (2), the smaller the advantage neighborhood of the object group is, the better the object subset will be; the bigger the disadvantage neighborhood of the object group is, the better the object group will be. Hence, the smaller the ADD of the object group is, the better the object group in an IS will be.

By fusing Definitions 2.4 (1) and (2), the smaller the MADD of the object group is, the better the object group in an IS will be.

*   Definition 2.7: Let S\={U,A,f,V} be an IS and ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m96](i/ch001.m96.png). The advantage-disadvantage neighborhood degree (ADND) of X is defined as:

Remark: According to the semantics of Definitions 2.7 (1) and (2), the smaller is the |D| of the object subset, the better the object subset in an IS. In particular, ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m98](i/ch001.m98.png) and ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m99](i/ch001.m99.png)become empty sets, ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m100](i/ch001.m100.png) and ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m101](i/ch001.m101.png)not become empty sets.We add 1 after ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m102](i/ch001.m102.png)and ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m103](i/ch001.m103.png) to avoid that the denominator and numerator are 0 and affect the meaning of ADND .

In the GOMADMMADD method, firstly, the group metric is constructed by using the object's advantage set (↑x) and disadvantaged neighborhood set (↓x) combined with the idea of rough set approximation. These include local advantage neighborhoods (![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m104](i/ch001.m104.png)) and local disadvantage neighborhoods (![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m105](i/ch001.m105.png)), advantage neighborhoods (![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m106](i/ch001.m106.png)) and disadvantage neighborhoods (![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m107](i/ch001.m107.png)). Secondly, the local advantage-disadvantage neighborhood degree (LADD) is constructed with the help of local advantage and disadvantage neighborhood, and the advantage-disadvantage neighborhood degree (ADD) is constructed with the help of advantage and disadvantage neighborhood. Finally, the subject group's mixed advantage-disadvantage degree (MADD) was constructed by linear combination of LADD and ADD. It is not difficult to see that the idea of MADD is to measure groups from a local and global perspective. The advantage of this is that more comprehensive information can be used, but the disadvantage is that the designed MADD lacks some properties that are conducive to optimization. In the GMADMDRS method of this paper, firstly, the advantage set (D^↑(x)) and disadvantage set (D^↓(x)) of the object are combined with the approximation idea of rough set to construct a group metrics. Include advantage lower neighborhood operator (![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m108](i/ch001.m108.png)) and advantage upper neighborhood operator (![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m109](i/ch001.m109.png)), disadvantage lower neighborhood operator (![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m110](i/ch001.m110.png)) and disadvantage upper neighborhood operator (![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m111](i/ch001.m111.png)). Secondly, the groups are measured separately from the perspective of advantage and disadvantage, which are important because they have similar properties, and then the advantage and disadvantage measures are combined nonlinearly to obtain the advantage-disadvantages neighborhood degree (ADND). It can be seen that ADND considers the advantages and disadvantages, but also considers the local and global, which is reflected in the lower and upper neighborhoods. To sum up, the essential difference between ADND and MADD lies in the different angles of consideration and the different forms of combination, and the resulting change in properties is the real purpose. This is also the reason to consider improving MADD to ADND. Using the properties of ADND can optimize the method and reduce the problems caused by exponential explosion, which MADD cannot.

In order to better understand the ADND, we study their related properties as follows:

On the whole, ADND is a measure based on dominance relations of object subsets, which is mainly used to measure the advantages and disadvantages of object subsets. In an IS, if an object x is worse than or the same as an object y, then ADND({x})≥ADND({y}). For an object subset X, if an object x is selected from the first layer and added to X, then there must be an object y in other layers, so that ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m170](i/ch001.m170.png);Similarly, combining (2) and (4), we get the method of selecting objects, that is, we start from the first layer and select layer by layer to meet the number of selected objects. If the number of selected objects is less than the number of the first layer, we only need to select in the first layer. According to (5), (6) and (7), we select objects from small to large according to the ADND value of the first layer objects.

GOMADMMADD

In this section, we compare the advantages and disadvantages between groups, propose “local advantage-disadvantage degrees” and “advantage-disadvantage degrees”, and design an algorithm based on them.

Algorithm 1: Group-oriented multi-attribute decision-making based on mixed advantage-disadvantage degree (GOMADMMADD)

| Input: k object groups ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m171](i/ch001.m171.png), (m[1],m[2],…,m[k] denote the number of group members), and k ISs S[1]\={X[1],A,V,f}, S[2]\={X[2],A,V,f}, ..., S[k]\={X[k],A,V,f}.  
Output: An object group X.  
Step 1: For each object ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m172](i/ch001.m172.png), calculate ↑x and ↓x by Definition 2.1;  
Step 2: For each object group X[t], t\=1,2,…,k, comp ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m173](i/ch001.m173.png) and ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m174](i/ch001.m174.png) by Definition 2.3;  
   Step 3: For each object group X[t], t\=1,2,…,k, compute the LADD and ADD of X[t] by Definition 2.4(1), (2);  
   Step 4: For each object group X[t], t\=1,2,…,k, compute the MADD of X[t] by Definition 2.4 (3);  
   Step 5: Rank the object groups according to the MADD of each object group. The smaller the MADD(X[t]) is, the better the object group X[i] will be X[i]<X[j] ⟺ MADD(X[i])>MADD(X[j]), i,j\=1,2,…,k. |
| --- |

Note: Analysis of Algorithm 1  
(1) Principle: According to Definitions 2.1, 2.3, and 2.4, and related Notes, the principle of Algorithm 1 is obvious.  
(2) Optimizations: (a) According to Definitions 2.2 (1) and (2), judge whether the object is Maximum or Minimum. If x is a Maximum or Minimum object, then, in Step 1 ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m175](i/ch001.m175.png).  
(3) Limitations: The more complex the object group's neighborhood is, the better the decision-making effect will be, that is, the disadvantage and advantage neighborhoods of each object group are different.  
(4) Advantages: (a) There is no need to predict the original data, just know the dominance and inferiority between attribute values. (b) Algorithm 1 is easy to understand according to the intuitive thinking mode of human beings, and does not rely on information outside the IS, such as attribute weights.  
(5) the time complexity is O(k²)  

GMADMDRS

Based on the semantics of ADND, we design a group-oriented multi-attribute decision-making method to select the optimal group with k objects or more in an IS. The specific method is as follows.

Algorithm 2: Group-oriented multi-attribute decision-making method based on dominance-based rough set(GMADMDRS)

| Input: An IS S\={U,A,f,V}, k.  
Output: Set AOS consist of optimal group with k objects or more.  
  ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m176](i/ch001.m176.png) // {all subsets of U }  
  for j←k; j < |U|;j++ do  
      AS^j←Subsets(U,j) // Subsets(U,j) return subset of all j objects in U  
      for i←1; i≤|AS^j|; i ++ do  
If ADND![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m177](i/ch001.m177.png)\=min(ADND(AS^j)) then  
                     ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m178](i/ch001.m178.png)  
           AOS←{AOS,AOS^j}  
           return AOS |
| --- |

Optimization of GMADMDRS

For Algorithm 2, the time complexity is O(2^(|U|)). When the number of objects increases, the time complexity of Algorithm 2 increases exponentially. To simplify the time complexity of Algorithm 2, we propose the following optimized algorithm according to Theorem 2.3.

Algorithm 3: Optimization of GMADMDRS

| Input: An IS S\={U,A,f,V}, k.  
Output: Set AOS consist of optimal group with k objects or more.

               ADID(i,j)←1  
        else  
               ADID(i,j)←0

    if sum(ADID(:,i))=1 then

  allSet←C[1]

  for l←2; l ≤|U|; l++ do  
       for i←1; i≤|U|; i++ do  
if ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m182](i/ch001.m182.png) and ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m183](i/ch001.m183.png) allSet then

until ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m188](i/ch001.m188.png)

          \[value,index\]←sort(ADND(AS^t))  
          ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.m190](i/ch001.m190.png)  
          AOS←{AOS,AOS^t}  
    return AOS |
| --- |

Note: Optimization principle of Algorithm 3  
(1) According to Definition 2.5, objects are layered. According to Theorem 2.3 (4), the process of selecting objects is from the first layer to the last layer.  
(2) According to Definition.2.5 and Theorem 2.3 (4), the number of selected objects is the minimum number of group selection requirements.  
(3) According to Theorem 2.3 (2), (5), and (6), if the number of selected objects is less than or equal to the number of objects in the first layer, it only needs to calculate the ADND value of each object in the first layer, sort from large to small according to the ADND value, and then select the first k objects.  
(4) According to Theorem 2.3 (7), if only the first level objects need to be sorted, they are sorted according to |D^↓(x)|.  
(5) the time complexity is O(|U|²)  

CASE ANALYSIS

In this section, two examples will be used to verify algorithm 1 and optimized algorithm 3 respectively.

Case Descriptions (Assessment of sales team)

Company T is another kind of private fund company, its main business is real estate investment, equity investment, and wealth management. The company T has set up private affairs department (PAD), major customer department (MCD), and business division (BD) 1,2,3,4, and each department has set up a sales group (team) (SG). The detailed structure is shown in [Figure 1](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#f01).

| Figure 1. Local organization chart of the company T |
| --- |
| ![Figure979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.f01](i/ch001.f01.png) |
| --- |
| *Note: PAD: contact high net worth people, to provide asset allocation and asset management services for investors, and build ''private bank'' for investors. MCD: to serve the third party institutional clients, cooperate with institutions, and achieve win-win wealth.BD 1,2,3,4: provide financial consultation, asset management, and asset allocation services for the QFII.* |

Table 1. The indicators of the appraisal members

| Attribute || Description of attribute features |
| --- | --- |
| Sales task completion rate a[0] || Actual sales / planned sales ×100%, the assessment standard is 100%, and the reduction is not less than 2%. |
| --- | --- |
| Quarterly sales growth rate a[1] || Compared with the sales performance of the previous quarter. If it is increased by 10% or more, recorded as 1, otherwise as 0. |
| Industry application sales a[2] || Develop intentional enterprises. If 2 or more enterprises are successfully developed, recorded as 1, otherwise as 0. |
| Market analysis a[3] || Complete the collection and analysis of market information within the specified time. If the market analysis report is consistent with the actual situation, recorded as 1, otherwise as 0. |
| Completion rate of training plan a[4] || If the test score is higher than or equal  
to 60 after the completion of the training, recorded as 1, otherwise as 0. |
| Completion rate of training plan a[5] || If the sales situation is reported to the person in charge and statistician in time, the communication with the virtual agent customer service, the timely feedback of customer demand, etc., recorded as 1, otherwise as 0. |
| Teamwork a[6] || If the work of the whole team is affected due to personal reasons, recorded as 0, otherwise as 1. |
| Professional knowledge a[7] || If business knowledge and other related knowledge are mastered, recorded as 1, otherwise as 0. |
| Analysis and judgment ability a[8] || If the objective neighborhood to make a more correct judgment quickly, and flexibly applied to the actual work to achieve better sales performance, recorded as 1, otherwise as 0. |
| Communication skills a[9] || If multiple conversation skills are used flexibly, recorded as 1, otherwise as 0. |
| Flexibility and adaptability a[10] || In response to changes in the objective neighborhood, flexibly take appropriate measures, recorded as 1, otherwise 0. |
| Employee attendance a[11] || If the attendance rate of employees reaches 100%, recorded as 1, otherwise as 0. |
| Responsibility a[12] || If in addition to doing well in one’s job, the company also takes the initiative to undertake additional work, recorded as 1, otherwise as 0. |
| Service consciousness a[13] || If there is a customer complaint, recorded as 0, otherwise as 1. |

To improve the management level of the company T, it is necessary to strengthen the assessment of the 6 sales groups (teams) (X[1], X[2], X[3], X[4], X[5], X[6]) of the company T. In particular, a[0] is not considered in the process of building an information system, because each member must contain a[0].

Experimental Analysis (Assessment of Sales Team)

For the 6 sales groups (teams), we make a comparative analysis from the following two perspectives:

*   (1) The commonly used MADM methods (TOPSIS, VIKOR, TODIM, PROMETHEE, MOORA) are directly used. Before showing the ranking results, we provide [Table 9](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#t9) to illustrate the values of the parameters of some methods. In TOPSIS, we measure the distance from the optimal solution and the worst solution by using Euclidean distance. In VIKOR, v is a threshold parameter that can be set by the decision-maker. We suggest the value of 0.5 for the parameter v. In TODIM, is a threshold parameter that can be set by the decision-maker. We suggest the value of 1.0 for the parameter .

TOPSIS, VIKOR, TODIM, PROMETHEE, MOORA are used to rank 28 members of all sales groups (teams). The ranking results are shown in [Figure 2](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#f02).

In the results of the above 5 methods, we see that the ranking results of 28 members are the same, but the ranking positions of 6 sales groups (teams) members are relatively scattered, and it is not easy to integrate into the ranking of 6 sales groups (teams). Next, we use a method to integrate, stack the ranking of each sales group (team) member based on [Figure 2](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#f02), and then sort the 6 sales groups (teams). The ranking results are shown in [Figure 3](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#f03).

For [Figure 3](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#f03), we see that the sorting results of the 5 methods are quite similar, but the results of our method are different from those of these 5 methods. This is also consistent with our previous analysis, that is, if the traditional MADM method is directly used, the team ranking is not easy.

*   (2) Preprocess the information system of 6 sales groups (teams). For [Tables 2](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#t2)\-[7](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#t7), we take the attributes owned by the group (team) members directly as the attributes owned by the groups (teams) to get a new IS S\={U[7],A,V,f} with U[7]\={X[1],X[2],X[3],X[4],X[5],X[6]}, as shown in [Table 10](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#t10).

From [Table 10](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#t10), we see that all sales groups (teams) have the same attributes, so it is impossible to sort the sales groups (teams).

From the above two perspectives, the traditional method is ineffective.

Next, we analyze the effectiveness of our method for the specific situations illustrated in [Tables 2](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#t2)\-[7](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#t7) as follows:

*   (a) The number of members in each sales group (team) who have all the attributes. X[1] has no, X[2] has 2, X[3] has 1, X[4] has 1, X[5] has 2, X[6] has no, so X[2] and X[5] are the best groups (teams).
*   (b) The effectiveness of the number of the sales team. For a group (team), the smaller the number of members is, the higher the effectiveness will be. X[4], X[5], and X[6] have the least number of members, all of them are 4, so the 3 groups (teams) have the highest effectiveness.
*   (c) The average number of attributes owned by group (team) members. The average number of attributes owned by X[1] members is 11.00, the average number of attributes owned by X[2] members is 11.83, the average number of attributes owned by X[3] members is 11.50, the average number of attributes owned by X[4] members is 11.20, the average number of attributes owned by X[5] members is 12.50, and the average number of attributes owned by X[6] members is 10.50. X[5] has the largest average attribute, so X[5] is the best.

To sum up, X[5] is the best sales group (team), which is consistent with our method.

Case Descriptions (Select of Investment Company)

In the following, we apply the proposed Algorithm3 to a real data set from UCI Machine Learning Repository. The data set is about Qualitative Bankruptcy Data Set ([https://archive.ics.uci.edu/ml/datasets/Qualitative\_Bankruptcy](https://archive.ics.uci.edu/ml/datasets/Qualitative_Bankruptcy)).The data set description is shown in [Table 11](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#t11a) and the attribute set information is described as [Table 12](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#t12). The Qualitative Bankruptcy Data Set is used to predict the Bankruptcy from Qualitative parameters from experts based on attribute characteristics. For this data set, we analyze the attribute characteristics and select companies to invest. Purpose of investment: 1. Guarantee income. Invest in a company that is not bankrupt. If the company goes bankrupt, then it means that the income is zero; 2. Reduce risk and invest in multiple companies, that is, diversify investment and reduce risk. In short, we choose k or more non bankrupt companies to invest.

The prospects for demand for product and service,  
Effectiveness of sale network. || P, A, N |
| Class (C): || Bankruptcy,  
Non-Bankruptcy || B, NB |

(Note: P=Positive, A=Average, N=Negative, B=Bankruptcy, NB=Non-Bankruptcy)  

Next, we try to use Algorithm 3 for company selection. To apply Algorithm 3 to the Qualitative Bankruptcy Data set, firstly, we delete the instances with duplicated information of the Qualitative Bankruptcy Data set, then we obtain a new data set with 103 instances, where 25 represents bankrupted companies, 78 firms that did not bankrupt in the prediction, as shown in [Table 12](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#t12).

According to the impact of IR, MR, FF, CR, CO, and OP on the company, it is generally believed that: the greater the industry risk, the easier the company is to go bankrupt; The greater the company's management risk, the easier the company is to go bankrupt; The worse the financial flexibility of the company, the easier the company is to go bankrupt; The worse the credibility of the company, the easier it is to go bankrupt; The weaker the competitiveness of the company, the easier it is to go bankrupt; The greater the operating risk of the company, the easier it is to go bankrupt. According to the above cognition, [Table 12](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#t12) is transformed into [Table 13](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#t13).

Experimental Analysis (Select of Investment Company)

Combined with the investment purpose, selection requirements: the selected companies will not go bankrupt as much as possible, and the number of selected companies shall be as large as possible. In order to effectively verify the validity of Algorithm 3, according to the prediction of bankruptcy (B) and non bankruptcy (NB) in [Table 13](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#t13), we analyze the effectiveness of the object selected by Algorithm 3. In the selected companies, the higher the proportion of non bankruptcy, the better the effectiveness of Algorithm 3. To analyze the effectiveness of Algorithm 3, we select two groups of data, {1,3,9,22,42,58,72}and {1,11,21,31,41,51,61,71}, respectively. The results are shown in [Figures 4](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#f04) and [5](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#f05).

In [Figures 4](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#f04) and [5](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#f05), we take the proportion of non bankrupt companies in the Qualitative Bankruptcy Data set as the benchmark (the benchmark value 0.7573). For Algorithm 3, if the proportion of non bankrupt companies in the selection result is higher than the benchmark, it shows that the selection result of Algorithm 3 is effective. In [Figure 4](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#f04), the k values are chosen according to the object levels. In [Figure 5](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#f05), the k value are taken in equal steps. According to the number of non bankrupt companies in the Qualitative Bankruptcy Data set, the k value does not exceed 78. In [Figures 4](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#f04) and [5](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch001.xhtml#f05), Effective value = number of non bankrupt companies in the selected companies / number of selected companies, the effective values are higher than the benchmark, reaching more than 0.90. Overall, the effectiveness of Algorithm 3 is excellent.

Remark:(1) In the calculation process, we use Algorithm 3. If Algorithm 2 is used, it needs to calculate 2^(103) times. It is difficult to calculate in the existing calculation force.

*   (2) When calculating the effective value, because the selected result is not unique, the average of the effective values of all results is used.

CONCLUSION

This chapter proposes a new GOMADMMADD method based on dominance rough set theory, and proves its effectiveness in evaluating and ranking groups through the evaluation of sales teams. However, it faces two problems: one is the exponential explosion in the number of evaluation and sorting groups, and the other is the lack of optimality in the degree of mixed advantages and disadvantages of this method, which puts it at a bottleneck. To address these issues, the GOMADMMADD method was improved, and the GMADMDRS method was proposed and optimized. In numerical examples, compared with the sorting results of various traditional methods, the sorting results of GMADMDRS are reasonable. Once again, the GMADMDRS method was optimized using the ADND characteristics, and the results before and after optimization remained consistent. The comparison of time performance with numerical examples shows that the optimized GMADMDRS has significantly improved its time performance before optimization. Finally, the optimized GMADMDRS was used to solve the securities investment problem on the bankruptcy company dataset.

The main contribution of this chapter lies in the dominance based rough set theory, which proposes the GOMADMMADD method, improves it, proposes the GMADMDRS method, and optimizes the GMADMDRS method, significantly improving its performance. The GOMADMMADD method can evaluate each group of groups (such as sales groups), and the optimized GMADMDRS method can effectively select the optimal group, which can be applied to practical problems such as portfolio investment problems, queue checking, and queue segmentation. Analyzing the limitations of our proposed method, the algorithms before and after optimization are only suitable for ordinal information systems, not for categorical data.

As for future research directions, we will investigate the following three aspects. First, we will extend GMADMDRS to the fuzzy environment and design the corresponding method in this environment. Second, we will design a new operator to make GMADMDRS suitable for categorical data, and expand the range of data types that GMADMDRS can handle. Finally, we apply GMADMDRS to group-oriented multi-attribute decision-making problems such as group checking, group partitioning, cohort checks, and cohort segmentation by virtue of the validity and time performance of GMADMDRS.

# Chapter 2

*   Liyin Wang  
    Shanxi University, China
*   Yuting Cheng  
    Shanxi University, China
*   Xueqing Fan  
    Shanxi University, China
*   Anna Wang  
    [![Orcid Image](i/orcid.svg)https://orcid.org/0009-0001-5196-9132](https://orcid.org/0009-0001-5196-9132)  
    Shanxi University, China
*   Hansen Zhao  
    Georgia Institute of Technology, USA

ABSTRACT

This chapter focuses on the issue of selecting a blood glucose meter for diabetic patients. To address this problem, the authors have developed a comprehensive evaluation model that combines triangular fuzzy (TF) logic, multi-granularity (MG), three-way decision (TWD), and rough set theory. The evaluation model takes into consideration the performance indicators of the blood glucose meter and user requirements. The authors utilize TF logic to model the indicators and determine their weights, while integrating the TWD method to handle incomplete and uncertain information. Subsequently, they introduce the PROMETHEE method to comprehensively evaluate and rank various alternatives. Finally, within this evaluation model, they select the optimal choice based on its score. Experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of our proposed method in resolving the blood glucose meter selection problem and providing personalized recommendations for diabetic patients.

INTRODUCTION

Problem Background

The advancement of modern medical technology has significantly improved the ability of diabetes patients to manage and monitor their blood glucose levels (Domingo-Lopez, 2022). Glucometers, being convenient medical devices, are extensively utilized in both home and clinical environments. Nonetheless, the market is saturated with a wide array of glucometer models and brands, which presents a challenge for patients in selecting the most suitable one. Factors such as accuracy, quality, after-sales support, and pain sensation must be taken into consideration. Patients must carefully evaluate these factors to choose a glucometer that aligns with their specific needs and preferences. Consequently, in order to aid patients in making well-informed decisions, it is essential to thoroughly comprehend the challenges associated with selecting a glucometer and provide corresponding solutions. Such research endeavors contribute to enhancing the effectiveness of diabetes management, improving patients' quality of life, and guiding medical device manufacturers in meeting the needs and expectations of patients, thus fostering product improvement and innovation.

When selecting a glucometer, patients must take into account various factors, including price, accuracy, and convenience. However, these factors introduce a level of ambiguity and uncertainty that complicates the decision-making process. In order to tackle these challenges, this study presents a comprehensive model that integrates triangular fuzzy sets (TFSs), the TWD, and the PROMETHEE multi-attribute decision-making method to improve the efficiency and accuracy of selecting glucometers.

A Brief Review of TFSs

In 1965, Zadeh ([Zadeh, 1965](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r39)) introduced the concept of fuzzy sets, expanding the representation of uncertain information beyond binary values in real-life scenarios. Fuzzy sets improve the accuracy and reliability of decision-making and have played a vital role in the advancement of fuzzy mathematics. Nevertheless, fuzzy sets also possess certain limitations. For instance, there is often difficulty in setting and selecting appropriate membership functions, and dealing with large datasets and complex problems can result in computational complexity.

To address these limitations, several generalizations of fuzzy sets have been proposed, such as intuitionistic fuzzy sets ([Atanassov, 1986](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r3)), hesitant fuzzy sets ([Torra, 2010](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r27)), and others ([Turksen, 1986](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r28); [Peng & Selvachandran, 2019](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r22); [Zhang et al., 2019](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r47)). These generalizations broaden the scope of fuzzy mathematics and offer additional approaches to handle uncertainty. These generalizations have found extensive application across diverse fields and problem domains. [Ecer (2022)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r9) introduced a novel extension of the MAIRCA framework, namely intuitionistic fuzzy MAIRCA, designed to assess coronavirus vaccines according to specific evaluation criteria. [Mishra et al. (2022)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r19) proposed a multi-attribute decision making method based on hesitant fuzzy sets and the modified VIKOR method, which effectively addresses the limitations of existing MADM methods. [Chai et al. (2023)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r4) combined intuitionistic fuzzy sets, interval-valued fuzzy sets, and cumulative prospect theory to propose a novel fuzzy multi-attribute decision making method for selecting the most sustainable supplier. [Wang et al. (2022)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r32) proposed a novel method for measuring the entropy of Pythagorean fuzzy set, considering both the Pythagorean fuzziness entropy based on membership and non-membership degrees, and the Pythagorean hesitation entropy based on the degree of hesitation. [Zhang et al. (2021a](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r41), [2022a](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r40)) introduced q-ROFSs into the framework of MG TWD and explored a novel approach for multi-attribute group decision making (MAGDM) in q-rung orthopair fuzzyinformation systems. Its main focus was on exploring MAGDM with imprecise and incomplete q-rung orthopair fuzzy information.

TFSs ([Van Laarhoven & Pedrycz, 1983](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r29)) are a commonly employed type of fuzzy set that adeptly captures the fuzzy nature of variables. Their extensive utilization in multi-attribute decision-making problems arises from their capacity to precisely portray the fuzzy characteristics of variables. [Yue et al. (2023)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r38) integrated TFSs and intuitionistic fuzzy sets, redefined and validated the algorithm, expectation function, and distance measure of triangular intuitionistic fuzzy numbers, and introduced a new theory of triangular intuitionistic fuzzy sets (TIFSs). [Garg and Rani (2022)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r11) presented novel distance measures for intuitionistic fuzzy sets in decision-making, utilizing different triangle centers, and validated their effectiveness through various patterns and challenges, while also providing a clustering algorithm for object matching based on confidence levels. In contrast, this paper utilizes a fusion approach that combined TFSs with MG TWD to assess different indicators of glucometer products. The objective is to identify the most suitable product for patients.

A Brief Review of MG TWD

Granular computing is a computational model that focuses on granularity in understanding and processing data and information. It treats them as complex systems consisting of elements at various levels of granularity. Through operations like grouping, classification, and aggregation, the relationships and mappings between different granularities are established, facilitating efficient information processing and management. Among the techniques used in granular computing, TWD stand out as a prominent tool. Unlike traditional two-way decisions, TWD incorporate the concept of delayed decision-making ([Wang et al., 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r30)). This allows decision-makers (DMs) to gather ample information and grasp the associated risks of different choices, leading to more precise decision-making processes.

TWD was originally proposed by [Yao (2010)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r33) in 2010 and have since undergone further expansion and development through subsequent research ([Yao, 2016](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r34), [2018](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r35), [2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r36)). Indeed, there has been a significant amount of research conducted on TWD in recent years. [Wang et al. (2023)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r31) proposed a new decision-making method that combined regret theory with TWDs in fuzzy incomplete information systems. [Yi et al. (2022)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r37) successfully accomplished the effective integration of Pythagorean fuzzy theory and TWD, thereby enhancing three-way decision-making by incorporating risk preference. [Liu et al. (2022)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r16) introduced interval-valued intuitionistic fuzzy sets into the loss functions of decision-theoretic rough sets and proposed an optimization-based approach for interval-valued intuitionistic fuzzy TWDs. Furthermore, in the research of some scholars ([Zhang et al., 2020b](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r45); Lei et al., 2020b; [Zhang et al., 2023a](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r48); [Han et al., 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r13)), they have combined the TWD approach with linguistic sets to address a wider range of problems and domains. [Qian et al. (2010)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r23) proposed MG rough sets as an extension to the single-granularity granular computing model, acknowledging that a single-granularity approach can offer only a limited perspective on complex problems. By incorporating multiple equivalence relations from diverse viewpoints, MG rough sets provide advantages in information fusion and analysis. By integrating TWDs with MG computing, some scholars ([Zhang et al., 2020a](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r44); [Pang et al., 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r21); [Zhang et al., 2021b](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r46); [Zhang et al., 2022b](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r42); [Zhang et al., 2022c](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r43); [Zhang et al., 2023b](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r49)) offered a multidimensional solution approach for MAGDM. Therefore, this paper adopts the MG TWD method, enabling flexible adjustments and improvements tailored to the specific problem. It offers an effective decision support and guidance mechanism.

A Brief Review of PROMETHEE

MG TWDs have limitations, and certain simplified models of MG TWDs may encounter issues such as a singular threshold or a singular decision matrix. These problems can hinder the effectiveness of the decision-making process.

In response to the above issues, [Akram et al. (2023)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r2) developed two effective and practical multi-criteria group decision-making approaches by leveraging the groundbreaking theory of the PROMETHEE family of outranking methods. [Li et al. (2022)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r15) introduced a new DEMATEL method that is based on grey relation analysis using objective data within a decision matrix. [Ridha et al. (2023)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r24) have introduced a new integration of the best worst method and preference ranking organization for enrichment evaluations method, along with a group decision making mechanism, to sort and rank optimal sets of pareto optimal front solutions based on expert opinions with full consistency ratios and stable ranking configurations. In addition, many scholars have also discussed their applications (Seikh & Mandal, 2023; [X. Sang et al., 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r25); [Moreira et al., 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r20); [Ahmadi & Peivandizadeh, 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r1)). Therefore, this paper employs the PROMETHEE method, which enables the evaluation and selection of multiple DMs and multiple decision matrices to achieve optimal decision outcomes.

The Motives and Contribution of This Chapter

Based on the comprehensive literature review, this research aims to integrate TFSs with MG techniques to propose a new MG TWD method within the PROMETHEE framework. The motivation for this study is as follows:

*   1\. Traditional rough set models have limitations when dealing with uncertainty and complexity, necessitating models that are better suited for high-dimensional, complex, and uncertain data. The concepts of TFSs and MG play crucial roles in describing uncertain information. Constructing a MG TFS information system allows for a more accurate completion of the information description process.
*   2\. It is necessary to introduce the concept of adjustable MG TFSs in the information fusion process of glucometers. This is because the MG TWD paradigm provides an effective tool for integrating multi-source information, thereby minimizing the overall decision-making risk.
*   3\. In the application of MG TWD, determining the thresholds is an additional critical task for classifying all objectives. Thus, the integration of effective MAGDM tools is necessary to complete the information analysis process. By utilizing MAGDM, challenges stemming from uncertainty in attribute weights, attribute values, decision preferences, and complex interactions among attributes can be addressed, ultimately improving decision efficiency and accuracy.

Based on the mentioned study motives, we can now summarize the significant contributions of this chapter as follows:

*   1\. In order to enhance the information description capability of TFSs, the concept of the MG TFS information system is introduced.
*   2\. To merge the MG TWD paradigm with TFSs, the concept of adjustable MG triangular fuzzy probabilistic rough set (PRS) is introduced.
*   3\. The MABAC method is employed to determine the most suitable thresholds, forming the foundation for the development of a TF MAGDM method that incorporates MG TWD.

Based on the aforementioned literature analysis, we have organized this chapter as follows. In Section 2, we present several key concepts. Section 3 introduces the concept of an adjustable MG TF PRS model. Section 4 focuses on the development of a novel glucometer selection decision model based on this theoretical framework. In Section 5, we apply the proposed selection decision method to a real-world case study for practical analysis. Finally, we summarize the key findings and conclusions, and provide an outlook on future research directions.

BASIC KNOWLEDGE

TFNs

Triangular fuzzy numbers (TFNs) are valuable in representing uncertain information in diabetes diagnosis, aiding doctors in evaluating the multifactorial aspects of the disease. The following presents the fundamental definition of TFNs.

Definition 2.1: ([Gilbey, 2009](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r12); [Van Laarhoven, & Pedrycz, 1983](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r29)) Let a real number x\=(x^L,x^M,x^U) satisfy 0<x^L≤x^M≤x^U, then x is called a TFN with a membership degree of:

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.m01](i/ch002.m01.png) (1)

In the above formula, x^L and x^U respectively represent the lower and upper bounds of the TFN x, x^M represents the internal maximum possible value. When x^L\=x^M\=x^U, the TFN x is equivalent to a fixed value of real numbers.

In practical decision-making, uncertain information is frequently encountered. Making judgments solely based on real numbers is challenging. TFN offers a partial solution to this problem by integrating the concept of fuzzy sets.

Definition 2.2: ([Mallak et al., 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r17)) Let U be a non-empty finite domain, and let A be a TFS on U. It can be represented as ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.m02](i/ch002.m02.png), where A(x)=(x^L,x^M,x^U).

To investigate the TF rough set in multiple granularities, the concept of triangular fuzzy relation (TFR) is introduced.

Definition 2.3: Let U and V be two non-empty finite domains. The TFR R is defined as ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.m03](i/ch002.m03.png),where for any (x,y)∈U×V, R(x,y) represents the possible degree of membership of the relationship between x and y.

The concept of a score function for TFNs has been introduced to facilitate the comparison and analysis of distinct TFNs.

Definition 2.4: ([Das, 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch002.xhtml#r7); Mao, 2022) Given two TFNs x\=(x^L,x^M,x^U) and y\=(y^L,y^M,y^U), then the following rules hold:

For a TFS A, the complement of set A is denoted as:
