The feature subset (8 features) determined based on RF is {Age, RPDE, DFA, HNR, Sex, Shimmer: APQ3, Jitter (Abs), Shimmer: APQ11})* |

Effect of SOM Clustering

[Table 5](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#t5) presents the performance of the proposed LDFSF model, both before and after incorporating SOM clustering, along with the corresponding time consumption. This analysis aims to highlight the impact of SOM clustering on the prediction model.

As shown in [Table 5](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#t5), the prediction model's running time is reduced on both datasets after SOM clustering. This can be attributed to the high similarity of features within the same cluster and the low similarity between clusters. The proposed method focuses on the relationship between candidate features within the same cluster and feature subsets during dynamic feature selection, avoiding the consideration of irrelevant features in other clusters, thus reducing the computational cost of the model. For the prediction results, the performance of the model after SOM clustering is improved in both classification and regression prediction, which is consistent with the results of [Nilashi et al. (2019)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r13) and [Nilashi et al. (2020)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r12). This outcome may be attributed to SOM clustering's ability to identify feature relevance, and dynamic feature selection methods that incorporate redundancy and complementarity can better analyze the intrinsic connections between features. Specifically, SOM clustering groups features based on similarity, and the high similarity within each cluster suggests potential redundancy. For example, as shown in [Table 4](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#t4), cluster 3 contains features with high inter-feature relevance ([Despotovic et al., 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r3)). Since they measure voice signals similarly, some redundant features may need to be eliminated. However, the dynamic feature selection method based on SOM clustering not only reduces computational costs but also helps remove redundant features, thereby enhancing the prediction performance of PD regression models.

Table 5. Comparison of effects of SOM clustering on prediction model performance and time consumption

| Dataset  || Evaluation metrics || Not used SOM || Use SOM |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| PTDS || Motor-UPDRS || MAE || 1.64 || 1.62 |
| Time (s) || 259.15 || 258.77 |
| Total-UPDRS || MAE || 2.14 || 1.99 |
| Time (s) || 259.07 || 258.77 |

Performance Comparison With Existing Methods

In order to further validate the effectiveness of the proposed LDFSF framework and its combination with RF for prediction, this section compares the LDFSF method with traditional FS methods and recent literature results.

[Table 6](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#t6) presents a comparison of the effects of traditional FS methods and the LDFSF method on model performance. The traditional FS methods, such as ReliefF, mRMR, and Boruta ([Tunc et al., 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r21)), select features based on relevance to the target value and redundancy between features. The LDFSF framework in this chapter adds complementarity to this foundation to explore the intrinsic connections between features. To verify the effectiveness of combining relevance, redundancy, and complementarity for feature selection, [Table 6](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#t6) compares the performance impact of different feature selection algorithms on the prediction model using the same regressor. RF performs well in PD regression prediction ([Tsanas et al., 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r20)). Therefore, in [Table 6](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#t6), each feature selection method is combined with RF to compare the performance of the FS method. Additionally, the same number of features is set in this section to compare the effectiveness of the selected feature subsets. In summary, this section provides a comprehensive comparison of different feature selection methods, highlighting the effectiveness of the proposed LDFSF framework combined with RF for prediction.

Table 6. Comparison of the results between the proposed method and the traditional feature selection methods

| FS method  || RF regressor |
| --- | --- |
| Motor-UPDRS || Total-UPDRS |
| ReliefF || 1.80 || 2.12 |
| mRMR || 1.78 || 2.12 |
| Boruta || 1.82 || 2.19 |
| LDFSF(ours) || 1.62 || 1.99 |

[Table 6](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#t6) demonstrates that the proposed LDFSF method outperforms traditional FS algorithms in PD severity progression prediction tasks. ReliefF only considers feature relevance, while mRMR and Boruta focus on selecting features with high relevance and low redundancy. However, these methods do not consider the complementarity between candidate features and feature subsets. In contrast, the LDFSF method combines relevance, redundancy, and complementarity for feature selection, resulting in the proposed prediction model shows superiority with the same regressor RF in [Table 6](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#t6). Specifically, the MAE of motor-UPDRS predicted by the LDFSF method is 10.00%, 8.99% and 10.99% lower than that of ReliefF, mRMR and Boruta respectively, and the MAE of total-UPDRS predicted is 6.13%, 6.13% and 9.13% lower than that of ReliefF, mRMR and Boruta respectively.

Furthermore, [Table 7](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#t7) compares the results of the proposed LDFSF method with those of existing literature. Specifically, this chapter compare the results of [Ji et al. (2018)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r7), [Yoon & Li (2019)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r27), [Tsanas et al. (2021)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r20), and [Despotovic et al. (2020)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r3) with the results of the proposed LDFSF method on the PTDS dataset.

[Table 7](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#t7) reveals that the LDFSF model achieves better prediction results compared to the literature. For regression, [Ji et al. (2018)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r7) and [Yoon & Li (2019)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r27) utilize transfer learning to address the limited data issue in PD patients, but face challenges in measuring similarity across different time dimensions. [Tsanas et al. (2021)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r20) employ four FS methods combined with RF for PD severity assessment, but potential bandwidth restrictions or transmission errors may affect accuracy. [Despotovic et al. (2020)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r3) achieve better prediction results with the Gaussian process regression model based on ARD, but ARD does not consider redundancy and complementarity when ARD calculates the weights of individual features and selects the feature with higher weight to form a feature subset. In comparison of [Table 7](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#t7), the LDFSF method surpasses the proposed method by [Despotovic et al. (2020)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r3) in terms of prediction performance, validating the effectiveness of feature selection based on relevance, redundancy, and complementarity. Overall, the LDFSF method enables the selection of a small yet effective subset of PD voice features, providing certain reference value for accurate PD severity assessment. However, the rough extraction of traditional voice features from the whole voice data makes the model accuracy limited.

Experiment Results of the Proposed GWT-RF-Att Model

Effectiveness of GWT

To provide further insight into the effectiveness of the GWT proposed features, the Spearman correlation coefficient is calculated to quantify the correlation between the features and UPDRS ([Tsanas et al., 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r20)). [Figure 9](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#f09a) displays the Spearman correlation coefficients between PD baseline voice features and UPDRS, as well as between frequency features and UPDRS.

The strength of the correlation between each feature and UPDRS is indicated by the absolute value of Spearman's correlation coefficient. In [Figure 9](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#f09a), the deeper the red or blue color, the stronger the correlation between the corresponding feature and UPDRS. It can be observed that compared to the baseline PD features, the frequency features extracted by GWT exhibit a significantly higher number of features with a high Spearman correlation coefficient with UPDRS, confirming the effectiveness of these frequency features.

| Figure 9a. Spearman correlation coefficient between features and UPDRS in cluster1 |
| --- |
| ![Figure979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.f09a](i/ch003.f09a.png) |
| --- |

| Figure 9b. Spearman correlation coefficient between features and UPDRS in cluster2 |
| --- |
| ![Figure979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.f09b](i/ch003.f09b.png) |
| --- |

| Figure 9c. Spearman correlation coefficient between features and UPDRS in cluster3 |
| --- |
| ![Figure979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.f09c](i/ch003.f09c.png) |
| --- |

| Figure 9d. Spearman correlation coefficient between features and UPDRS in cluster4 |
| --- |
| ![Figure979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.f09d](i/ch003.f09d.png) |
| --- |

[Figure 9](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#f09a) also reveals that some features extracted by GWT have a weak association with UPDRS. The original RF model averages the results of all decision trees, assuming that each tree is accurate. However, if a decision tree selects a large number of features with little correlation with UPDRS, it may reduce prediction performance. By employing an attention layer to adaptively learn and assign different attention weights to the decision trees generated by RF, the model can focus more on decision trees with better prediction performance. Therefore, the RF model improved by the attention mechanism may build a more accurate prediction model.

Clustering Results

To validate the clustering results, [Figure 10](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#f10) displays the MAE for each cluster. [Tsanas et al. (2021)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r20) pointed out that when the MAE of the PD severity prediction model is less than 5 points, this prediction model will be very useful in practice. The bar chart in [Figure 10](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#f10) demonstrates that after dividing the Parkinson's telemonitoring dataset into four clusters, the MAE of the proposed GWT-RF-Att model for the prediction of motor-UPDRS and total-UPDRS are all less than 5, which indicates the practicability of this model.

| Figure 10. Performance results for PD progression assessment in each cluster |
| --- |
| ![Figure979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.f10](i/ch003.f10.png) |
| --- |

The UPDRS consists of three main parts: “Mentation, Behavior and Mood,” “Activities of Daily Living,” and “Motor Examination.” The motor-UPDRS is specific to assessing motor signs of Parkinson's disease, including voice features. On the other hand, the total-UPDRS incorporates not only motor-UPDRS but also mental health and daily activities. Therefore, it is assumed that motor-UPDRS is more closely related to voice disorders in sound-generated motor activities compared to total-UPDRS ([Despotovic et al., 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r3)). This explains the lower MAE of predicted motor-UPDRS in each cluster compared to total-UPDRS, as depicted in [Figure 10](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#f10).

The statistical significance of GWT features is assessed using the p-values, as presented in [Table 8](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#t8). The p-value is determined through t-tests to evaluate the significance of the method. It is observed that in all cases, the GWT features exhibit a statistically significant correlation with UPDRS (p-value < 0.05) ([Tsanas et al., 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r20)). Consequently, the GWT features obtained in this chapter are deemed statistically significant.

Table 8. The p-value for performing t-test for the GWT features

| UPDRS  || GWT features |
| --- | --- |
| Cluster1 || Cluster2 || Cluster3 || Cluster4 |
| Motor-UPDRS || 0.0002 || 0.0058 || 0.0002 || 0.0047 |
| Total-UPDRS || 0.0197 || 0.0020 || 0.0002 || 0.0107 |

Comparison of Results

To evaluate the performance of our proposed GWT-RF-Att model, we compared it with traditional machine learning methods and state-of-the-art methods from the literature. Baseline methods including SVR, XGBoost, and RF were used in the experiment. Additionally, we compared our proposed method with GWT combined with SVR, XGBoost, and RF (i.e. GWT-SVR, GWT-XGBoost, GWT-RF) to highlight the influence of GWT-extracted features, and with RF combined with attention mechanism (RF-Att) to assess the impact of attention weights on prediction results. To ensure fairness, all models were trained and tested on the same datasets with optimized parameters.

[Table 9](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#t9) presents the MAE of motor-UPDRS and total-UPDRS predicted by the aforementioned models on the test set. The results indicate that replacing baseline features with GWT-extracted features improves the prediction performance of traditional methods, and the addition of attention weighting reduces the MAE of RF. Furthermore, we compared our proposed model with several advanced methods from the literature, including FDAMF, PTL, FS-RF, and GPR-ARD. The performance of these methods on the same Parkinson's telemonitoring dataset is also reported in [Table 9](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#t9).

Table 9. Comparative results for predicting Parkinson's disease progression assessment

| Model  || Input feature || Att || Motor-UPDRS || Total-UPDRS |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Traditional machine learning methods || SVR || Baseline || No || 2.77 || 3.53 |
| XGBoost || Baseline || No || 1.72 || 2.44 |
| RF || Baseline || No || 1.69 || 2.09 |
| RF-Att || Baseline || Yes || 1.66 || 2.04 |
| GWT-SVR || GWT || No || 2.63 || 3.33 |
| GWT-XGBoost || GWT || No || 1.71 || 2.16 |
| GWT-RF || GWT || No || 1.67 || 2.08 |
| Advanced methods || [Ji et al. (2018)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r7) || Baseline || No || 3.00 || 4.60 |
| [Yoon & Li (2019)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r27) || Baseline || No || 2.45 || 3.02 |
| [Tsanas et al. (2021)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r20) || Baseline || No || 2.65 || 3.17 |
| [Despotovic et al. (2020)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r3) || Baseline || No || 1.65 || 2.02 |
| Proposed || GWT-RF-Att || GWT || Yes || 1.53 || 1.91 |

The specific prediction results of each model in [Table 9](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#t9) can be analyzed as follows. Among traditional machine learning methods, both XGBoost and RF outperform the single prediction model SVR due to their integrated algorithms. Moreover, RF combines bagging and random feature selection, effectively addressing the overfitting issue observed in XGBoost. This not only enhances the robustness of the RF model but also improves its prediction accuracy. Consequently, compared to SVR and XGBoost models, RF demonstrates a reduction in MAE of 40.07% and 40.79% for motor-UPDRS and total-UPDRS, respectively, affirming its effectiveness in predicting PD severity progression. Additionally, compared to SVR, XGBoost, and RF, GWT-SVR, GWT-XGBoost, and GWT-RF models achieve reductions in MAE of 5.05%, 0.58%, 1.18%, and 5.67%, 11.48%, 0.48% for motor-UPDRS and total-UPDRS, respectively. [Figure 11](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#f11) compares the effects of attention mechanisms on the model. It can be seen that the errors are reduced after the addition of attention mechanism. Furthermore, the MAE of motor-UPDRS and total-UPDRS predicted by the GWT-RF and RF-Att models was reduced by 1.18%, 1.78% and 0.48% and 2.39%, respectively, compared to the RF model. Therefore, these results demonstrate that GWT can extract more effective PD features, and the attention-weighting-based RF can focus on the decision trees with better prediction performance. By combining these two methods, the proposed GWT-RF-Att model further reduces the MAE and improves the prediction performance.

| Figure 11. Attention weighted RF predicts the MAE for motor-UPDRS and total-UPDRS |
| --- |
| ![Figure979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.f11](i/ch003.f11.png) |
| --- |

Moreover, [Table 9](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#t9) reveals that the proposed model outperforms the existing literature in terms of predicting PD severity, achieving the lowest MAE values. The results show that the MAE of the proposed method for predicting motor-UPDRS and total-UPDRS are 1.53 and 1.91, respectively. The proposed method achieved a significant improvement compared to the best results of the literature in [Table 9](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#t9). The MAE was reduced by 7.27% and 4.05%. In previous studies, methods like FDAMF ([Ji et al., 2018](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r7)) and PTL ([Yoon & Li, 2019](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r27)) attempted to predict the UPDRS score of PD patients by learning from the information of other patients. However, it was challenging to measure the similarity of PD patients in different time dimensions, which limited the reduction of prediction errors. FS-RF ([Tsanas et al., 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r20)) and GPR-ARD ([Despotovic et al., 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#r3)) used feature selection algorithms to select important PD features, which improved prediction accuracy. However, this approach may result in the loss of crucial information, affecting the performance of the model. In contrast, the proposed GWT-RF-Att model overcomes these limitations. It utilizes graph wavelet transform to capture more effective PD features and employs an attention weighting mechanism to focus on decision tree models with different prediction performance. As a result, the proposed model effectively enhances the prediction accuracy. Compared with the results of LDFSF method in [Table 7](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch003.xhtml#t7), the prediction error of GWT-RF-Att method is lower, which indicates the effectiveness of the features extracted by GWT-RF-Att method. However, since the method requires wavelet transform and attention layer training, the limitation of the proposed model is that the running time of the model may be long.

CONCLUSION AND FUTURE WORKS

This chapter proposes LDFSF and GWT-RF-Att methods for PD severity prediction from the perspectives of feature selection and feature transformation, both of which improve prediction performance. The LDFSF method analyzes the relevance, redundancy, and complementarity among PD voice features. It utilizes the maximal information coefficient to determine feature relevance and selects feature subsets with high relevance, low redundancy, and high complementarity using a dynamic feature selection method based on SOM clustering. The GWT-RF-Att method is based on graph wavelet transform and attention weighted random forest. It predicts the severity of current symptoms in PD patients using effective frequency features extracted from PD voice features. This method adaptively focuses on decision tree models with better performance in the random forest for prediction, resulting in improved accuracy. Experimental results on real public PTDS dataset show that both LDFSF and GWT-RF-Att methods have achieved better predictive performance compared to existing methods, thus verifying the effectiveness of these two methods in PD severity prediction.

In future research, combining the two proposed methods to achieve better results is an interesting attempt. Additionally, the causal feature selection method will be introduced to uncover the Markov boundary of the target variable UPDRS. This approach aims to identify voice features that have a causal relationship with UPDRS, thereby enhancing the robustness and practicality of the diagnostic model.

ABSTRACT

The Durban University of Technology is now engaged in a project to create a data lake house system for a Training Authority in the South African Government sector. This system is crucial for improving the monitoring and evaluation capacities of the training authority and ensuring efficient service delivery. Ensuring the high quality of data being fed into the lakehouse is crucial, since low data quality negatively impacts the effectiveness of the lakehouse system. This chapter examines quality control methods for ingestion-layer pipelines in order to present a framework for ensuring data quality. The metrics taken into account for assessing data quality were completeness, accuracy, integrity, correctness, and timeliness. The efficiency of the framework was assessed by effectively implementing it on a sample semi-structured dataset. Suggestions for future development including enhancing by integrating data from a wider range of sources and providing triggers for incremental data intake.

INTRODUCTION

In South Africa, Sector Education and Training Authorities (SETAs) are government-established entities responsible for managing skills development and training in various sectors of the economy. These entities are referred to as Government-Sector Training Authorities (GTAs), and they play a crucial role in the country's efforts to improve skills and training across many sectors. The Durban University of Technology (DUT) has partnered with a South African Government Technical Agency (GTA) to enhance the data management strategy used by the GTA for an ongoing project. This is achieved by assisting DUT students in developing supplementary talents. In order to enhance the data management capabilities of the GTA, it was recognized that a comprehensive system was required to store data and generate reports automatically. Following the discussion, the DUT team suggested using Microsoft Azure services to establish a data warehousing solution. Additional context for the project is presented by ([Mthembu et al. 2024](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r23)). This chapter focuses on establishing a Data Lakehouse for a Training Authority in the South African Government sector. One crucial aspect is investigating techniques to ensure the integrity of data as it moves through the system, particularly during the Incremental Extract-Load-Transform Pipelines at the Ingestion Layer employing Data Orchestration.

In the age of Big Data, where the vast amount of information poses both advantages and challenges, effectively handling and using data has become essential for organizational success. The wide range of data types, including structured, semi-structured, and unstructured forms, requires a sophisticated approach for data manipulation ([Azad et al., 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r2)). The Extract, Load, Transform (ELT) framework is a versatile tool that is well acknowledged for its efficacy in negotiating the complexities of contemporary data settings ([Singhal & Aggarwal, 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r30)). However, as data pipelines get larger and more complicated, guaranteeing the integrity of data quality (DQ) has become more important.

The emergence of big data has necessitated the use of Distributed Data Warehouses (DLH). [Harby and Zulkernine (2022)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r14) suggested that the big data age has brought up new issues for traditional Data Warehouses (DWs). The increase in diverse data quantities caused by digital transformation presents a difficulty for traditional data warehouse solutions in businesses ([Čuš & Golec, 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r7); [Giebler et al., 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r12)). Furthermore, ([Barika et al. 2019](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r3)) highlight the challenges faced by researchers in organizing, controlling, and implementing big data workflows, which differ significantly from typical workflows. After undergoing transformation and being placed into the data warehouse (DW), the original filtered information is no longer retained (Figueira, 2018). According to Conventional, ([Nambiar and Mundra 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r24)), the ETL procedure is deemed inadequate for fulfilling certain data management requirements.

A Data Lake (DL) is a comprehensive storage and exploration system specifically intended to manage large amounts of varied data. It has been widely recognized as the preferred method for processing and storing various data ([Begoli et al., 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r4)).An further research undertaken by the DUT team emphasizes the significance of data governance in establishing authority over data utilization and decision-making via the implementation of processes, rules, organizational structures, and responsibilities ([Mthembu et al., 2024](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r23)).Failure to regularly maintain a data lake might result in expensive and difficult data management. Insufficient execution of data governance in DL systems may result in the creation of a chaotic “Data Swamp,” which undermines the worth of information in knowledge management and analytical systems owing to the presence of difficult-to-process and analyze data.

DLH emerged as an innovative concept that integrates the benefits of DL and DW, enabling companies to efficiently handle, analyze, and store vast quantities of data in a versatile and cost-efficient way. According to ([Harby and Zulkernine 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r14)), this is intended to expedite the process of extracting information effectively.

The objective of this chapter is to provide a structured system for managing and incorporating data into a Data Lakehouse (DLH) to guarantee the accuracy and reliability of the data. The research outlines the following goals, which are aligned with the aim:

*   • Analyze current literature to identify crucial parameters and quality control measures for data orchestration and intake.
*   • Create and implement a framework that incorporates the essential elements and quality control measures indicated for efficiently managing and integrating data in a distributed ledger healthcare (DLH) system.
*   • To assess the efficacy of the established framework and provide suggestions for data quality management in the orchestration and ingestion of data for a distributed ledger technology (DLT) healthcare system.

By offering further study data on the use of Azure Data Factory (ADF) for the orchestration and ingestion of data into a data lake it enhances the existing knowledge base to facilitate future study in data orchestration and ingestion using Azure Services. Identify the limitations of establishing a pipeline for data orchestration and ingestion. Furthermore, offering comments for prospective investigations in this domain enhances the importance of our study. The study explores the concept of data orchestration, which is a crucial area that combines data management and quality control. The main objective is to provide quality control procedures in incremental ELT pipelines inside the Ingestion Layer of a Data Warehouse (DW). The research highlights the difficulties associated with using semi-structured data and recognizes the need for specific approaches to guarantee optimal data quality and integrity. The study result describes the creation of a pipeline and framework that allows data from SharePoint to be imported into a specific container inside the data lake architecture. The pipeline includes essential steps for converting semi-structured data into a structured format and utilizes data orchestration methods to ensure the correctness, completeness, and consistency of the data being processed.

Section 1 clearly explains the cause of the gap and offers a strong remedy, which is shown by the objectives and driven by the desire to provide valuable insights to the field of study and achieve specific research outcomes. The explicit references to the study's aims, scope, and delimitations provide a comprehensive understanding of the content and boundaries of this research. The methodology, datasets, matrices, and tools and platforms to be used in the experiment are described in Section 2. This provides sufficient time for thorough preparation for the subsequent Chapter Four. Section 3 demonstrates the results of the experiment and assesses the efficacy of the created artifacts. In addition, Section 3 discusses the constraints and offers an evaluation of the experiment's execution. Section 4 presents the findings of the experiments and evaluates the overall performance of the manufactured artifacts, in line with the experimental aspect of the study.

LITERATURE REVIEW

(Figueira, 2018) posits that the primary objective of the area of big data is to provide a means of managing vast quantities of data in order to generate valuable insights and knowledge. ([Gupta, 2023](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r13)) explains that the concept of big data originated from the three main factors known as the 3V's. These factors include: volume, which refers to the amount of data available ([Liu & Zhang, 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r21); [Pratsri & Nilsook, 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r27)); variety, which encompasses a wide range of data types and sources, such as structured, semi-structured, and unstructured data ([Liu & Zhang, 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r21)); and velocity, which represents the speed at which new data is generated and transferred ([Liu & Zhang, 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r21)). Subsequently, the 5V's framework emerged, including the original 3V's and incorporating the following additions: The veracity of Big Data is of utmost importance, as ensuring accurate data without duplication is a challenging and time-consuming task. Value refers to data that is valuable and holds significance for business applications, including analysis for summarization and strategic planning, with the aim of enhancing product value and improving competitiveness in the target market. [Figure 1](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#f01), created by Sandhu in 2021, illustrates many instances of the 5V's.

| Figure 1. Representation of the 5V’s |
| --- |
| ![Figure979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.f01](i/ch004.f01.png) |
| --- |

The three categories of content format sources are structured data, semi-structured data, and unstructured data. Structured data pertains to data that is systematically organized in a certain hierarchical pattern. Storing data in this manner facilitates search engines in presenting and comprehending it more effortlessly. Semi-structured data is characterized by an irregular and implicit structure, without a clearly defined data model. In this particular database format, the distinction between data and schema is not readily apparent. In contrast to organized data, unstructured data functions as its complete opposite. The absence of a well-defined framework results in the processing of data being a time-consuming task.

Semi-structured data presents the most difficulty in handling due to its lack of adherence to a pre-established data model and absence of a stable schema. This is a result of its irregular and implicit framework ([Dickson & Asagba, 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r9)). (Nadal et al. 2022) have identified factors that make the ingestion of semi-structured data into a pipeline with schema flexibility challenging presenting difficulties in creating a standardized framework for processing since it may be necessary to accept various data examples.

([Raj, et al. 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r28)) asserts that ETL and ELT are the primary classifications of data pipelines. Data lakes (DLs) use the Extract, Load, and Transform (ELT) methodology, in which the first step involves loading the “raw data” followed by its subsequent transformation ([Cherradi & El Haddadi, 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r6)).The ELT technique, in contrast to the ETL procedure, follows a similar fundamental idea but differs in terms of sequencing. Instead of doing the transformation before loading, the data is loaded first and then converted afterwards. Data orchestration has a wider range of activities compared to data integration. While data integration mainly concentrates on consolidating data for reporting purposes, data orchestration includes tasks such as resource allocation, monitoring, data discovery, preparation, and integration, processing, and linking enriched data across various landscapes ([Dinh & Nguyen, 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r10)). ([Kevin and Ginger 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r17)) highlight the objective of the system to efficiently handle and combine data, consequently improving or establishing corporate operations.

Data intake is usually the first layer in the design. The primary function of the intake layer is to acquire data from various sources and integrate it into the distributed learning system. ([Cherradi & El Haddadi, 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r6)) have shown that data intake at the ingestion layer may be performed in several ways, depending on the source and destination. These methods include batch processing, real-time processing, or a combination of both. According to ([Isah et al. 2019](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r16)), stream processing involves the handling of continuous or dynamic data, in contrast to batch processing. Stream processing, a technique that allows for real-time data analysis without relying on persistent storage, plays a crucial role in social media platforms such as Twitter and Facebook. The velocity at which data is received greatly impacts this process ([Nambiar & Mundra, 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r24)). Stream processing excels at handling large amounts of data that is constantly flowing at high speeds, and it can be quickly expanded to accommodate increased demands ([Hlupić & Puniš, 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r15)). ([Nambiar and Mundra, 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r24)) provide a hybrid processing strategy that combines batch and stream processing methods. This approach aims to achieve higher accuracy while minimizing processing time.

The chosen DQ qualities are correctness, validity, data integrity, accuracy, completeness, and timeliness. Although the threshold conditions were met, it was considered unnecessary to evaluate consistency for ingestion-layer processes. Consistency guarantees the consistent presentation of data, its alignment with other data, and its freedom from contradiction. This was considered more relevant to source systems and transformation-oriented pipelines. Assessing the comprehensibility of data is primarily important for systems-level governance, rather than being a central concern at the data input layer.

Performing a data integrity check during data import is essential for preserving data dependability and preventing data corruption throughout the transfer process between providers and repositories ([Anastasova & Yanev, 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r1)), assesses whether data values adhere to predetermined criteria, and any variation suggests possible issues in the process of collecting the data. According to ([Peng et al. 2015](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r25)), data validity pertains to the integrity of data, namely its consistency and correctness throughout its existence. Data accuracy refers to the extent to which information precisely and faithfully portrays its intended topic or process ([Anastasova & Yanev, 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r1)). Completeness refers to the state of data including all the anticipated properties and linked instances, which determines the extent of information ([Koltay, 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r19)).Timeliness refers to the age of the data and is of utmost importance in industrial systems that handle continuous production. Any delays or gaps in the data might lead to inaccurate management choices. ([Gabarro and Stewart, 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r11)) emphasize that the effectiveness of an orchestration's dependability may be assessed using two separate measures. Firstly, by examining its behavioral comprehensiveness measured by the number of its calculated results.

| Parameters || Data warehouse || Data lake |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Information || Data warehouses solely pay attention to business procedures. || Data lakes keep all processing information |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Processing || Extensively treated data || Most data are not processed |
| Type of Data || Mostly in tabular and structured forms || they may be classified as semi-structured, structured, or unstructured |
| Work || optimized to retrieve data || Distribute data stewardship |
| Agility || Compared to data lakes, this arrangement is fixed and less flexible. || Very flexible and able to adjust and reposition as need |
| Users || extensively used by business analysts and experts || Data scientists, data developers, and business analysts utilize data lakes. |
| Repository || Expensive storage with quick reaction times is used || Data lakes are designed for inexpensive storage. |

Collection of Data

The provided dataset is a CSV file that includes personnel information sourced from datablist.com. The collection includes User ID, First Name, Last Name, Date of Birth, and Job Title for 100 entries. Only the first 80 recordings will be subjected to ingestion for the purpose of this experiment. The following 20 records are allocated for testing data import after the first phase. The data entries are delimited by semi-colons, and these delimiters will be eliminated when converting the CSV file to a SQL database table. It is crucial to recognize that the first row of the CSV denotes the column headings.

Process of Dataflow

[Figure 4](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#f04) depicts the flow of data from the source, which is represented by SharePoint. The dataset being transferred is represented as a CSV file, which contains semi-structured data. Arrows symbolize the conduit that will be used for the transmission of the data. Significantly, Azure Data Factory will be used for this procedure. The data will be transported from the source to the DL and then duplicated in a container known as “Raw”. The data contained inside this container will stay in its original, unaltered state, prohibiting any modifications to be made. Data timestamp is used to monitor new instances, indicating the current status of the data. Subsequently, the data residing in the Raw container will be replicated and transformed into a table inside an Azure SQL database, facilitating the implementation of data orchestration. Two more columns will be included, namely hashing and date timestamp. Hashing generates a distinct hash value that serves as a unique identifier for a record, hence preventing record duplication. The date timestamp will record the precise moment that the data was either added or changed. Subsequently, the organized information will be placed in the data lake inside a container referred to as “Curated”. However, this study will specifically examine the functionality of data orchestration for curate data. Data orchestration will be used to prevent the duplication of previously added records. This will reduce data duplication caused by hashing. If there are any modifications to the data of an existing record, it will be updated. Furthermore, to enable the gradual intake of data, a trigger mechanism will be deployed to automatically execute the pipeline at certain intervals.

RESULTS AND ANALYSIS

Computation of Metrics

The term “reference dataset” in this study refers to the anticipated quantity of records that should be included in the dataset ([Mahanti, 2019](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#r22)). The expected number of records refers to the anticipated number of records, whereas the actual number of records is the outcome obtained after doing the experiment. Frequently, a “benchmark” dataset is used as a reference for comparison. This benchmark is considered to provide a full and accurate depiction of the topic under consideration. The benchmark is a projection of the anticipated quantity of records used to assess the comprehensiveness of the dataset in issue.

Wholeness

Completeness pertains to the degree to which the whole of the anticipated records is acquired. The completeness of the dataset is assessed by calculating the ratio between the actual number of records and the total anticipated number of records. The completeness result might be represented either as a percentage or as N/A. The success criteria for the result are 100%.

The following illustrates the meaning of the symbols in the equation:

*   • Comp%: The rate of completeness of the dataset.
*   • Arecords: The actual number of records presented in the dataset.
*   •
    
    Erecords: The expected number of records in dataset.
    
    ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.m01](i/ch004.m01.png) (1)
    

If:

Arecords\>Erecords; Comp%\= N/A

No data ingested then; Acc % = N/A

Correctness

Data correctness pertains to the absolute lack of mistakes; it adheres to a binary idea where data is classified as either accurate or incorrect, with no middle ground. Accuracy may vary, but for data to be considered accurate, it must mostly be right. Correctness refers to the absolute adherence to truth and accuracy in data, whereas accuracy quantifies the extent to which the data accurately reflects the world it represents. However, in order for any data to be acknowledged as accurate, its fundamental accuracy is indisputable. The measure of accuracy is represented by either a “Yes” or “No” or “Not Applicable”. The success criteria for the result are zero.

The following illustrates the meaning of the symbols in the equation: Corr: the degree of data completeness. Arecords: the aggregate count of factual entries included in the dataset. Erecords: the overall anticipated quantity of records inside the collection. The absolute difference between the values of Erecord and Arecords is represented by the variable Corr.

*   where: Corr > 0; indicates a fault in the accuracy of the dataset.

A correlation coefficient of 0 implies that the ingestion of the dataset is accurate. No data has been processed or included; Correlation is not applicable.

Accuracy

The following depicts what the symbols represent in the equation:

*   • Acc%: The rate of accuracy of the dataset.
*   • Trecords: The total number of records presented in the dataset.
*   • Tacc: The number of accurate records in the dataset.
*   • Arecords: The total number of actual records presented in the dataset.
*   •
    
    Erecords: The total number of expected records in the dataset.
    
    ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.m02](i/ch004.m02.png) (3)
    

If:

Arecords≠Erecords then; Tacc\=Erecords

Arecords=Erecords then; Tacc\=Arecords

No data ingested then; Acc% = N/A

Validity and Integrity

Validity refers to the accuracy and soundness of information, whereas integrity refers to the consistency and reliability of data.

A record would be considered legitimate if the hashing is accurate. Essentially, this is the total number of legitimate records among the total number of records that were consumed. The result of validity and data integrity may be shown either as a percentage or as N/A. The success criteria for the result are a 100% achievement rate.

The follow depicts what the symbols represent in the equation:

*   • Vi%: The rate of the integrity records of the dataset.
*   • Tvalid: The total number of valid records presented in the dataset.
*   • Arecords: The total number of records presented in the dataset.
*   •
    
    Tinacc: The total number of inaccurate records presented in the dataset.
    
    Tvalid = Arecords – Tinacc (4)
    
    ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.m03](i/ch004.m03.png) (5)
    

If:

No data ingested then; Vi% = N/A

Timeliness

Timeliness, in the context of data management, pertains to the velocity at which data is provided or made available to business users. Essentially, it measures the time difference between the instant data is generated and when it is shown to the user. The “Occurrence Time” is a vital component of timeliness since it precisely identifies the specific instant when a real-world event, represented by the data, occurred. It also represents the moment when a certain characteristic or value associated with an object first became tangible. Essentially, “Occurrence Time” denotes the first time. In order to quantify this, the duration of data transmission is determined by calculating the difference between the end time and the start time, which is expressed in seconds. The success criteria for the result must be achieved within a time frame of 120 seconds. Significantly, several programs provide information on the length of the execution.

The following depicts what the symbols represent in the equation:

*   • Time(s): The rate of time it takes to ingest the dataset in seconds.
*   • ST: The Start Time (Occurrence time) of the dataset.
*   •
    
    ET: The End Time (Data delivery time) of the dataset.
    
    Time(s) = ET – ST (6)
    

If:

Time(s) < 120s; then valid

Time(s) > 120s; then not valid.

Test Case

Test cases provide a methodical methodology to evaluate data using the previously specified DQ criteria. Users may record the initial data, any changes or additions made throughout the process, and the expected outcomes inside this framework. Additionally, it has a segment for overseeing many test endeavors, assessing their success or failure, and adding supplementary comments. The last part offers a concise overview of the overall effectiveness of the test case. The header provides details on the test case and scenario in the given test case. The test number is denoted by <number>, while the scenario number is indicated by n, and the title is represented by <title>. The dataset contains a count of entries located below the header. The input variable represents the original number of rows in the dataset, while the variable reflects the current number of rows. The process variable indicates the number of new rows that will be added to the dataset. Ultimately, the result accurately portrays the anticipated conclusion of the ingesting process. The next section presents the test results, including the computation of the matrices, which demonstrates the efficiency of the pipeline for that specific situation. Subsequently, the success is judged based on the established criteria for success. This may be indicated by either a positive response (Y) or a negative response (N). Supplementary remarks or observations for each matriculation might be recorded if necessary.

The overall findings represent the last segment of the test case, indicating whether the experiment achieved success or failure. The quantity of favorable results is recorded, and the success rate is computed. Finally, the ultimate determination is made on whether the experiment was ultimately successful or unsuccessful, depending on the specified success criteria.

Success Rate Calculation

The success criterion of the success rate outcome is 100%, thus the verdict will be Success, and otherwise the verdict is Fail.

If:

Srate\= 100%; success

Srate< 100%; fail

Framework for Managing and Coordinating Data

[Figure 6](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#f06) represents the structure that will facilitate the assessment of data orchestration. Prior to anything else, it is important to finish the scenario instances. In the subsequent stage, it is necessary to document the beginning count of rows, the procedure, and the specific specifics of the conclusion in the test case. After completing the previous procedures, the following step is to execute the pipeline and finally record the outcomes in the test case and assess the overall result of the experiment.

Procedure for establishing the pipeline using Azure services:

*   Step 1: You need to establish an Azure account.

In order to access the Azure portal and make use of Azure Services, it is necessary to register an Azure account. Moreover, the utilization of certain services necessitates the acquisition of credits, which incur charges commensurate with the nature of the services generated and used.

Step 2: Establishing the environment Begin by creating a resource group to serve as storage for all the resources that will be used. It is important to pick the resource group that will be used for future purposes, if it is relevant. This resource group should be the one that has been formed. To proceed, the subsequent action involves establishing a storage account to securely retain the source data. After a successful deployment, proceed to establish a container. Then, inside that container, create a folder and upload the necessary files into it. Blob storage was used for testing reasons. Subsequently, establish an Azure SQL Server for the purpose of storing the Azure Database. This study employs SQL authentication as the chosen technique of authentication. Users are required to log in with a password in order to access the contents of the database server. Subsequently, proceed to establish an Azure SQL Database. This location functions as the destination where the semi-structured data, in the form of a CSV file, is converted into structured data and then saved in a table. Building a database incurs costs, but building the database server does not. The configuration of the database is determined by the desired performance and intended use of the database. The cost summary will compute the monthly expenses by considering the chosen database configurations. After creating the database, the Azure Data Factory (ADF) instance is created. Once the deployment is finished, the ADF studio may be launched.

*   Step 3: Transfer the contents of a CSV file to a database table.

Create connected services for both the source and sink in ADF. Establish a link service for blob storage, where the CSV file is saved as a blob for the purpose of this study. The second service that is connected is an Azure SQL Database, which is linked to the database. Next, build a new pipeline and choose the dataflow activity. A new data flow has to be generated and assigned as the data flow for the data flow activity. Subsequently, generate datasets for each of the associated services. Subsequently, create a new pipeline and opt for the copy action. Assign the source to the dataset containing the CSV file, and assign the sink to the dataset containing the SQL database. Choose the option to automatically build a table if the table does not already exist in the database. Subsequently, verify the pipeline and troubleshoot any issues.

*   Step 4: Establishing the data orchestration pipeline

Generate a novel data flow, thereafter establish a fresh pipeline, and use the data flow activity. Subsequently, establish the data flow to match the previously generated data flow. Establish two sources: the first source will retrieve data from the blob storage, while the reference source will get data from the database. These sources will be compared. Activating the data flow debug is necessary since it enables the data flow to execute. Generate Link a derived column transformation to the initial source and generate a column for hashing and another for the date timestamp. Given the introduction of two more columns, it is necessary to establish new columns in the database. It is important to note that the data types of the column, the expression returned in the “Dataflow expression builder,” and the database must be the same. Subsequently, it is necessary to establish a connection with a Lookup transformation in order to validate if the records already exist or are considered new. To do this, you may configure the lookup stream to match the reference source. The lookup condition is used to provide the criteria for comparing the two sources with each other. In order to ascertain the outcome of the record after comparison, a conditional split transformation was linked to the dataflow. This study encompasses three potential streams: new record, update, and no action. The state of each stream is determined in its respective condition “Dataflow expression builder”. The process will follow its respective stream and finally reach the sink, depending on the condition that is satisfied. The fresh record stream is linked to the sink, but the update stream needs an extra transformation in comparison. In order to enable record updates, it is necessary to attach an alter row transformation to the update stream. Finally, the sink determines the target dataset where the records will be stored. The update technique varies between the new record stream and the update stream in terms of the selected choices. In the new record stream, only the insert option is chosen, whereas in the update stream, both the insert and update options are selected. Subsequently, the columns in the data flow must be accurately linked to the corresponding columns in the destination dataset.

*   Step 5: Implement the pipeline

To start the pipeline, choose the pipeline built thereafter, and verify the pipeline for any potential errors. Assuming there are no issues, execute the pipeline by choosing the debug option. To see the count of rows written, choose the output option and then pick the data flow details.

Assessment of Framework

Before acquiring and using the ADF environment, this research used a theoretical strategy to execute the experiment and evaluate the efficacy of the generated artifacts. This theoretical assessment included both favorable and unfavorable outcomes for each given situation. The same possibilities were assessed both theoretically and practically.

Assessment of Outcomes

Scenario 1 assessed the initial data import process by absorbing a total of 80 records. Significantly, the reference dataset did not include any previous entries. The newly imported records were cross-referenced with the reference dataset to see whether any records already existed (this process was applied to all scenarios). In addition, the records that are ingested would be assigned a date timestamp and undergo hashing to ensure data integrity. Unfortunately, the data timestamp was not visible. Consequently, a total of 80 records were successfully processed, aligning with the anticipated result. The measures of completeness, accuracy, and validity/integrity achieved a perfect score of 100%, while correctness received a score of 0 (Y). Thus, Scenario 1 was deemed successful.

Scenario 2 was used to evaluate the intake of new records. This was achieved by consuming 10 new records. It is worth mentioning that there are a total of 80 entries included in the reference dataset. Consequently, a total of 10 records were successfully swallowed, aligning with the projected outcome of 90 records being eaten. The metrics of completeness, accuracy, and validity/integrity achieved a perfect score of 100%, however correctness received a score of 0 (Y). Thus, Scenario 2 achieved success.

Scenario 3 included the modification of preexisting records.15 new records were tested, with 10 being new and 5 being pre-existing. The success requirements include the absence of any newly ingested data and the updating of five existing records. The completeness and accuracy achieved a score of 100%, while the validity/integrity achieved a score of 95%. However, correctness received a score of 0 (Y).The compromise of validity and integrity implies a problem in the constructed pipeline, since the preservation of data quality is crucial in a real-world context. An error in the data might render a company's system unreliable and could result in financial consequences. Upon examination, the new records were included, but no records were modified. Consequently, a grand total of 100 recordings were swallowed. Consequently, Scenario 3 did not provide the desired outcome.

Scenario 4 included testing the intake of pre-existing records. The initial amount of records ingested was 100, and 10 pre-existing records were used to test this situation. The anticipated outcome did not need the consumption of any documents. The measures of completeness, accuracy, and validity/integrity achieved a perfect score of 100%, while correctness received a score of 0 (Y). Thus, Scenario 4 achieved its objective.

[Figure 7](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#f07) depicts the time it takes for the pipeline to execute and provide outcomes. The pipeline execution did not beyond 120 seconds, indicating that all scenarios resulted in good results. Scenario 3 took the longest time to finish the experiment and provide results. This scenario included the process of entering and modifying data. Significantly, with every iteration of the pipeline, the execution time decreased. However, because the first run of Scenario 3 was successful, this particular duration was recorded.

General Performance

Upon completion of the experiment, the statistical analysis reveals that the total success rate of 84% surpasses the failure rate of 16% by a ratio of 5 to 1 (84:16 = 5.25:1 ⇒ 5:1). Based on the statistics, this resulted in a favorable outcome. Nevertheless, according to the specifications of data quality (DQ), accuracy is a necessary condition. Consequently, this experiment is considered unsuccessful since the pipeline was unable to update records. The pie chart shown in [Figure 8](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#f08) provides a visual representation of the total performance rate.

Obstacles

The primary obstacle encountered during the actual execution of the experiment was acquiring the necessary credit to access and exploit resources inside the Azure environment. The credit balance imposed limitations on resource creation, namely on the creation of additional “SQL Databases”. In addition, it is necessary to watch the Azure credits while constructing the data flow, since running the debug process incurs costs that depend on the duration of its use. The update stream failed to operate as anticipated, resulting in data that cannot be relied upon. Efforts were undertaken to solve this puzzle; but, the intended result was not achieved. This discrepancy may be attributed to a mismatch in the hashing process. If none of the requirements were satisfied, no records will be impacted. In addition, the date timestamp was successfully implemented in the query editor of the “Azure SQL Database”. However, the pipeline failed to incorporate a datetime stamp for the ingested entries.

Upon completion of the experiment, it was seen that using the copy activity at the beginning of the procedure was not advisable. This occurs when data is sent from the source to the sink without checking for any duplicate entries. While this study does not specifically focus on data cleansing, it is still an important issue to take into account. It is important to document and acquire the schema for the sink in a more effective manner for future study. The decision to use blob storage as the source, rather than alternative sources like SharePoint, was motivated by the goal of resolving any issues or difficulties. Difficulties arose, making it more convenient to comprehend and retrace the error's origin. This methodology facilitated enhanced comprehension, which has the potential to serve as a roadmap for future implementation using other sources. The sink was not configured for a distributed ledger (DL), since this work would be addressed in future research. The sink was configured to the “Azure SQL Database”, facilitating the visualization of the conversion of the data from semi-structured to structured format. Nevertheless, next research will ultimately focus on deep learning (DL). The framework demonstrated efficacy in capturing the outcomes of each scenario event. The design was suitable; nevertheless, it has to be updated due to the identification of defects throughout the trial. Furthermore, it was noted that the remark part was optional, which might be considered a matter of personal taste.

CONCLUSION

The aims of this study were accomplished by identifying crucial criteria and implementing data quality control measures based on available literature. Multiple DQ traits were found, prompting a further in-depth investigation of their occurrence and importance. The DQ features used for this study were specifically intended to guarantee the data's quality throughout the process of intake. After successfully achieving the initial study objective, the discovered data quality criteria were employed as measurements in the design and development of the framework for data orchestration and ingestion. The efficacy of the established framework was assessed by implementing the data orchestration and watching the results of the experiment. Therefore, the final research aim has been accomplished. The efficacy of the created frameworks was first assessed in a theoretical manner and then validated via actual evaluation. Theoretical assessment alone would not have completely corresponded to real-world situations; nonetheless, it did contribute to comprehending the outcomes of using the DQ framework, both in terms of success and failure. To fully understand the efficiency of the study's artifacts, it was necessary to practically apply the pipeline. The experiment's practical execution revealed that the assessment of timeliness should be based on the anticipated quality that would be consumed. The figures given in Section 3 indicate that the experiment was successful, with a success rate of 84% and a failure rate of 16%. Despite the positive outcomes, this experiment is considered a failure based on the criteria of DQ. In order to achieve a Defects Quantification (DQ), it was necessary for the experiment to provide a success rate of 100%. The artifacts produced in this research demonstrated a certain level of efficacy, while the findings indicate that they need more refinement to fully capitalize on their potential and enhance their efficiency. Significantly, another crucial outcome of this investigation included the acquisition curves. It facilitated comprehension and firsthand experience of the intricacies involved in designing and executing pipelines, as well as comprehending the functioning of data orchestration. Utilizing cloud technology incurs significant expenses. The primary constraint of this study is the restricted availability of loans. The economic implications of debugging led to a reduction in the experimentation of data orchestration components. Nevertheless, despite the constraints of this study, it has enhanced the comprehension of DQ maintenance and data orchestration. By presenting a comprehensive set of principles for the design and execution of data orchestration systems, it significantly enhances the current knowledge base. Furthermore, it provides valuable insights for the creation of a data orchestration pipeline. Future research should further investigate the adaptation of data transfer between SharePoint and DL, using the analytical framework and empirical insights offered by this study. Additionally, it would be intriguing to ascertain if the findings of this investigation will yield similar outcomes on other cloud platforms such as Amazon Web Services and Oracle. Future endeavors should prioritize the integration of the study's discoveries with deep learning techniques and improving the process of extracting data from SharePoint. Further study is advised to give priority to the creation of a robust framework for managing metadata activities. Moreover, it is essential to provide a trigger function to enable the gradual intake of data from the source. Conducting thorough testing is crucial to determine the efficiency of the framework in data orchestration. Significantly, future research should specifically target the deficiencies found in this study. This involves efforts to enhance the DQ metrics, test case, and address issues related to data updates. Furthermore, it was determined that there is a need to investigate a more efficient approach for first transferring data from the source to the sink. This technique should only prioritize importing the schema utilized from the source to the sink, since the approach employed just copied the entries without confirming the presence of any duplication. Future work should also include the preservation of updated records in a DLH, without overwriting them, in order to retain a comprehensive historical record. Instead, new entries should be introduced and clearly indicated as a distinct version of the current record. The data timestamp might be used in the future for the development of this solution. According to [Table 1](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch004.xhtml#t1), the trait of correctness was recognized as one of the DQ attributes mentioned in the literature. Hence, future study should investigate an additional DQ characteristic. Furthermore, it is crucial to prioritize the analysis of different types of changes in future endeavors, since there is a potential for a multitude of changes to transpire throughout time. It is important to take into account the concept of progressively changing dimensions. Finally, there are still many aspects to be improved and taken into account, since this study just scratches the surface of the potential of data orchestration. Hence, future study should delve into more complex aspects of data manipulation within this domain.

KEY TERMS AND DEFINITIONS

Data Ingestion: The process of collecting and importing information from a variety of sources into an archive, e.g. the Data Lakehouse, so that it can be made available for analysis.

Data Lakehouse: A unified data management architecture that enables organizations to store and analyze structured and unstructured data on a single platform, combining the benefits of Data Lakes and Data Warehouses.

Data Orchestration: This is to ensure efficient data flows and integration into a system, often facilitated through automated working processes, by coordinating and managing different processing tasks.

Data Quality: The degree to which data are accurate, complete, timely, and consistent, providing reliable and meaningful information for decision making.

Semi-Structured Data: Data which has an irregular and implicit structure that lacks a defined data model.
