Evaluation of Outcome

The primary function of the outcome in three-way decision making is to assess the process’s effectiveness. This assessment focuses on identifying and analyzing the differences that emerge before and after implementing the trisecting and acting steps. To quantify this change, we use the following general formula ([Gao & Yao, 2017](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.xhtml#r2)):

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m01](i/ch006.m01.png)

In this equation, Q(π) and Q(π’) denote the initial and subsequent quantified outcomes, respectively. This measurement can evaluate aspects such as benefit, cost, or utility. While a direct calculation of Q(π) and Q(π’) might not always be feasible, this formula still offers a means to gauge the difference. Occasionally, the measure Q(π’|π) can be assessed from alternative perspectives.

In existing research, much emphasis is placed on the aspects of tripartition and action, with limited focus on outcome evaluation. Yao and Jiang, in their pioneering work, proposed a comprehensive metric framework for measuring outcomes based on Trisecting and Acting, as indicated in the following equation:

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m02](i/ch006.m02.png)

Here, Q(π) signifies the overall results, which may encompass cost, benefit, or risk. Q(P[i]), i\=1,2,3 represents the outcome of a specific region P[i], and w[i] is the weight assigned to Q(P[i]), i\=1,2,3. This framework facilitates a more nuanced and comprehensive evaluation of the outcomes, contributing significantly to the field of three-way decision-making.

Change-Based Three-Way Decisions

Definition 8.1: ([Jiang et al., 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.xhtml#r4)). An evaluation space is defined by a triplet E\=(U,S,e), where U is a finite non-empty set of objects, S is a set of states, and e is an evaluation from U×S to the set of real number ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m03](i/ch006.m03.png), namely, ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m04](i/ch006.m04.png). For a pair of an object x∈U and a state s∈S, e(xs) denotes the evaluation value of x in state s.

As a special case of ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m05](i/ch006.m05.png), we may use a three-valued set {-1,0,+1} to qualitatively indicate some properties of objects. We may also use the unit interval \[0,1\]. The evaluation value e(xs) represents the object in states. By looking at the two states, we can observe changes.

Definition 8.2: ([Jiang et al., 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.xhtml#r4)). In an evaluation space E(U,S,e), given a pair of states ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m06](i/ch006.m06.png), the change of evaluation values of an object x∈U is defined by:

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m07](i/ch006.m07.png)

In the definition above, we produce a new evaluation method. It does not merely evaluate an object in one state, and it uses the object’s values in two different states by calculating their difference. The new evaluation extends the traditional evaluation methods. More specifically, we employ the difference between two values as changes. It does not relate this difference to the initial value e(xs). Sometimes it needs to consider changes relative to the value e(xs).

Definition 8.3: ([Jiang et al., 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.xhtml#r4)). In an evaluation space E\=(U,S,e), suppose e(xs)≠0 for all x∈U and s∈S. Given a pair of states ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m08](i/ch006.m08.png), the relative change of values of an object s∈U is defined by:

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m09](i/ch006.m09.png)

where abs (.) denotes the absolute value of a real number.

In some situations, we may want to evaluate the overall changes of a set of objects. The simplest way is to summarize the changes of the objects in the set.

Definition 8.4: ([Jiang et al., 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.xhtml#r4)). Suppose X⊆U is a subset of objects. The overall change of objects in X is defined by:

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m10](i/ch006.m10.png)

where X\= {x[1],x[2],x[3],…,x[n]}.

The summation collects the differences of all objects in the set X in two states. If the summation result is over zero, the strategy achieves a positive effect; if the result is zero, there is no change; otherwise, the approach will offer a negative effect, so the summation can provide a qualitative characterization for a strategy.

Reinforcement Learning

Reinforcement Learning (RL) (Kaelbling, 1996), denoted as a method in machine learning, emphasizes how agents learn the optimal strategy in an environment through their actions and the feedback from rewards received. Assuming that the environment possesses Markovian characteristics, sequential reinforcement learning problems can be aptly modeled through Markov Decision Processes (MDPs).

The change of trisecting states by strategy acting adheres to Markovian properties, meaning that the subsequent trisecting state solely depends on the current state and policy, irrespective of history. A Markov Decision Process is represented by the tuple π[c], A,P,R\>. Here, π[c] is a finite set encompassing all possible trisecting states, while A is a finite set of all applicable strategies. P represents the probability of transition to the next state transition probability ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m11](i/ch006.m11.png), indicating the likelihood of transitioning to the next state ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m12](i/ch006.m12.png) under the current state π[c] and strategy A. The reward function ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m13](i/ch006.m13.png) quantifies the reward obtained for transitioning to state ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m14](i/ch006.m14.png) under the conditions of state π[c] and strategy A. Owing to its capacity to effectively encapsulate the dynamic interaction between an agent and its environment, MDPs are commonly used to describe reinforcement learning tasks.

The Q-learning algorithm in reinforcement learning seeks the optimal strategy in a Markov Decision Process (MDP) context by continuously updating a Q-table through exploration and learning. This table records the value function for each pair of state-actions, representing the expected return from executing a specific action. The algorithm updates the Q-function Q(π[c],A) using the formula:

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m15](i/ch006.m15.png)

The balance between exploration and exploitation is managed by strategies like ε-greedy. This Q-learning approach, applied to 3WD, models the evolution of trisecting states under different strategies, optimizing strategy selection based on state dynamics. The algorithm aims to converge the Q-table towards the optimal value function, enabling the best action selection in any given state.

In this chapter, we define utility generated from changes in trisecting states as rewards, denoted as ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m16](i/ch006.m16.png), where π[c] and ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m17](i/ch006.m17.png) are the current and subsequent trisecting states, and A is the applied strategy. The Q-function, Q(π[c],A), updated with these rewards, guides strategy selection to maximize cumulative rewards. We introduce a target reward value, R[target], and consider exploration complete when the sum of rewards ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m18](i/ch006.m18.png) from sequential strategies reaches R[target]. The process iterates until a stable and efficient strategy path emerges, as indicated by consistent attainment of R[target] in the fewest cycles.

Integrating reinforcement learning with the study of changes in 3WD, the utility generated by the fluctuation in trisecting states can be utilized as rewards. These rewards serve to calculate and update the Q-function. Decisions are then made based on the Q-function to select strategies that maximize cumulative rewards. Given the ongoing changes in trisecting states under various strategies, this chapter proposes setting a target reward value. The exploration is deemed complete when the cumulative rewards generated by the changes in trisecting states under consecutive strategies reach this target value. Following this methodology, multiple explorations are conducted until a stable and shortest strategy path is consistently identified.

STRATEGY SELECTION AND UTILITY MEASUREMENT BASED ON REINFORCEMENT LEARNING

This section employs the Markov Decision Process to model maximizing utility through continuous optimization of strategy selection in changing trisecting states. Initially, quadratic programming is used to solve for state transition probabilities. Subsequently, the rewards generated by state changes are calculated using the cumulative prospect theory. Finally, the Q-learning algorithm is employed to identify the strategy that maximizes rewards in each cycle.

| Figure 2. Three-way decision based on reinforcement learning (RL-3WD) |
| --- |
| ![Figure979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.f02](i/ch006.f02.png) |
| --- |

[Figure 2](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.xhtml#f02) illustrates the process of updating the Q table using the Q-learning algorithm. For a trisecting state ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m19](i/ch006.m19.png) in cycle t, a strategy A is chosen using the ε-greedy approach, which, when applied, yields a more effective trisecting state ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m20](i/ch006.m20.png) in cycle t+1, thus generating a reward r[t]. This reward is then used to update the Q-table. This process is repeated through multiple training iterations until the Q-values in the Q-table stabilize. The following outlines the specific approach for a series of strategy trainings on the trisecting states {R^+,R⁰,R^(\-)}:

State Transition Probability Matrix: Calculate the transition probability matrix resulting from the application of each strategy. Based on this matrix, determine the trisecting states for the next cycle.

Use cumulative prospect theory to calculate the utility value of the new trisecting state generated by each strategy application and consider this as a reward in reinforcement learning.

Strategy Selection: Choose the strategy for each trisecting state in each cycle from the Q table using the ε-greedy approach.

Set Training Termination Criteria: Establish a target reward value during strategy training. Training stops when the cumulative reward of the training reaches this target value.

Conduct multiple trainings until the Q values in the Q-table stabilize, then select the strategy with the highest Q value for each cycle.

States and Transition Probability Matrix

In the Markov Decision Process related to three-way changes, the sequence of trisecting states that vary with cycles is considered as the state set. The application of different strategies yields various state-transition probability matrices. These matrices determine the distribution of trisecting states in the next cycle. This section employs quadratic programming to solve for the state transition probability matrix between different cycles.

Suppose there are n cycles in a simulation round, each cycle generating a trisecting state after the application of the strategy. The vector representing the distribution of trisecting states in the initial cycle is:

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m21](i/ch006.m21.png)

Where ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m22](i/ch006.m22.png), χ={+,0,-} indicates the distribution of trisecting regions in the initial cycle.

After k cycles, the estimated probability that an object is in the trisecting region ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m23](i/ch006.m23.png) is ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m24](i/ch006.m24.png), hence the state distribution vector after the k step transition is:

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m25](i/ch006.m25.png)

Let ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m26](i/ch006.m26.png) be the estimated value of the state transition probability matrix in the Markov predictive model,

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m27](i/ch006.m27.png)

Therefore, the estimate ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m28](i/ch006.m28.png) of π[c](k) is:

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m29](i/ch006.m29.png)

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m30](i/ch006.m30.png)

k\=1,2,…,n.

Let C[χ](k) denote the error between the estimated probability ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m31](i/ch006.m31.png) and the actual value ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m32](i/ch006.m32.png) of the changed tripartition R^χ after the k\-step transfer:

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m33](i/ch006.m33.png)

Where j\={+,0,-}, χ={+,0,-}, and + ≻ 0 ≻ –.

Then n cycles, the sum of squares of the errors E[χ] in region R^χ is given by:

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m34](i/ch006.m34.png)

In each cycle, the sum of squared errors D in the transition process of objects within the changing trisecting regions is as follows:

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m35](i/ch006.m35.png)

We introduce constraints into the model to establish an optimization model to solve the Markov state transition probability matrix by utilizing the concept of the least-squares method. The objective function of this model is to minimize the sum of squared errors D in the transition process at each stage in each state. The constraints of the model are the row-sum conditions and non-negativity conditions. The specific formulation is as follows.

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m36](i/ch006.m36.png)

There are various methods for solving quadratic programming, such as Lemke, active set, and linear transformation methods. Here, the model is transformed into a linear programming model for the solution, simplifying the complexity of solving the model.

Strategy Selection

For each change-based tripartition state in every cycle, there exist multiple strategies that can be applied, which are considered as the action set in the Markov Decision Process. The objective is to select a strategy that maximizes the cumulative reward from the initial to the terminal state of the change-based tripartition state. To achieve this, we employ the Q-learning algorithm, which continuously explores and learns to update a Q-table. This table records the value function of each pair of state-actions.

In the process of strategy selection, a Q-table is initially constructed with column labels as change-based tripartition states ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m37](i/ch006.m37.png) and row labels as applicable strategies A[i]. At the beginning of the cycle, all values in the Q-table are initialized to zero. For the change-based tripartition state corresponding to the cycle in the Q-table, a -greedy strategy is used to choose the strategy to apply. Specifically, when the action value function Q(π[c],A) is greater than ε or all Q(π[c],A) values are zero, a strategy is randomly selected for implementation. Otherwise, the strategy corresponding to the maximum value Q(π[c],A) in that cycle is chosen.

The values of Q(π[c],A) in the Q\-table are continuously updated. The specific formula for updating Q(π[c],A) is as follows. Suppose strategy A[m] is applied to the change based tripartition state ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m38](i/ch006.m38.png) of cycle i, then the real calculation formula for ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m39](i/ch006.m39.png) is

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m40](i/ch006.m40.png)

Here, r[i] represents the reward value for the change-based tripartition state ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m41](i/ch006.m41.png) generated by applying strategy A[m] in the i\-th cycle, γ is the discount factor, and ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m42](i/ch006.m42.png) denotes the maximum Q value for the change-based tripartition state ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m43](i/ch006.m43.png) in cycle i+1 in the Q-table.

In the Q-table, the value of ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m44](i/ch006.m44.png) at the position corresponding to the application of strategy A[m] on the change-based tripartition state ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m45](i/ch006.m45.png) is the value function updated after previous training, considered as the estimated value ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m46](i/ch006.m46.png). ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m47](i/ch006.m47.png) represents the difference between the estimated and real values:

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m48](i/ch006.m48.png)

Based on the above formula, the Q value at the position corresponding to the change based tripartition state ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m49](i/ch006.m49.png) and strategy A[m] in the Q-table is updated by

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m50](i/ch006.m50.png)

where σ is the learning rate.

For updating the Q-table, manual calculations are impractical; hence the Q-learning algorithm is developed for computation as follows.

According to Algorithm 1, an updated Q-table can be obtained. The strategy with the highest Q value for each change-based tripartition state in the updated Q-table can be selected to form an optimal strategy sequence to achieve the target reward in the shortest time. This sequence of strategies, learned from experience, represents the strategies that can achieve maximum utility, thus guiding future strategy selection.

Reward and Future Utility

In the Markov Decision Process of three-way decision making, each change in the change-based tripartition state generates a reward to update the Q-table. We employ the cumulative prospect theory to calculate the rewards generated in each cycle.

Cumulative prospect theory describes decision-makers’ behavior and attitudes toward risk under conditions of uncertainty and risk. Its principal ideas can be summarized in three aspects: Outcomes are viewed as gains or losses relative to a reference point, rather than as final states of wealth. Second, decision-makers exhibit risk aversion for gains, and risk-seeking for losses, and are more sensitive to losses than gains. Lastly, in the face of low-probability events, decision-makers tend to magnify their impact, while for high-probability events, they tend to downplay it. The Cumulative Prospect Theory suggests that decision-makers prefer decisions with the highest cumulative prospect value. Using the value function v(x[h]) and the decision weight function ψ[h], the cumulative prospect value function v is given by:

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m55](i/ch006.m55.png)

For the change-based tripartition state ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m56](i/ch006.m56.png)in the k^(th) cycle, applying strategy A results in a regional change of objects in the universal set U, leading to the change-based tripartition state ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m57](i/ch006.m57.png)in the k+1^(th) cycle. The application of strategy A could lead to ideal, uncertain, or nonideal changes in objects in the ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m58](i/ch006.m58.png) region, with similar potential changes in the ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m59](i/ch006.m59.png) and ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m60](i/ch006.m60.png)regions. Let x[k] denote the utility generated by objects in the ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m61](i/ch006.m61.png) region changing to ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m62](i/ch006.m62.png).

Decision-makers need to set a reference value ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m63](i/ch006.m63.png); if objects in the ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m64](i/ch006.m64.png) region under the influence of strategy A undergo ideal or uncertain changes, then ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m65](i/ch006.m65.png), indicating a gain. Otherwise, a loss is incurred. As empirically validated, Tversky and Kahneman provided a detailed formula for the value function v(x[h]):

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m66](i/ch006.m66.png)

Here, let μ=v\=0.88 and θ=2.25. As shown by the formula above, the value function is a monotonically increasing function.

By substituting the change utility value x[k] into the value function formula v(x[h]), the value function for each region in the most effective change-based tripartition state of cycle k+1, resulting from the change-based tripartition state of cycle k under the influence of strategy A, can be obtained as detailed in [Table 1](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.xhtml#t1):

The rewards generated in each cycle during every training process can be determined based on calculated rewards, thereby estimating the future utility of three-way decision changes. The Q-learning algorithm is used to update the Q-table and to identify the shortest path to the target reward through a sequence of strategies based on the Q-table. Combined with the state transition probability matrix, a future sequence of change-based tripartition states can be predicted, and the reward for each cycle is calculated using cumulative prospect theory. In this process, the future utility of three-way changes is given by:

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m96](i/ch006.m96.png)

Where r[i] represents the reward obtained in the i^(th) cycle of the shortest simulated cycle.

Since this method employs cumulative prospect theory to calculate rewards generated in each cycle for predicting future utility, it more accurately reflects the decision-maker’s behavioral preferences. Future utility calculated using this method can estimate outcomes before a series of strategies are executed and provide relatively accurate predictions.

As delineated in this paper, the three-way decision framework employing reinforcement learning offers an innovative approach to optimizing strategy selection and enhancing utility measurement. The application of reinforcement learning to the three-way decision method enables the maximization of cumulative rewards, demonstrating a significant stride in decision-making methodologies. Nevertheless, this approach presents opportunities for further refinement. Presently, the state transition matrix relies on quadratic programming for its resolution. This aspect could be more efficiently addressed by employing advanced algorithms, such as policy gradient methods inherent in deep reinforcement learning. Moreover, incorporating affective computing into the reward calculation could align the reward function more closely with the decision-maker's preferences, thereby enhancing the model's applicability and accuracy.

While integrating reinforcement learning into the three-way decision framework marks a theoretical advancement, its practical application is still nascent and warrants extensive exploration. Future research should pivot towards applying this model in concrete scenarios, such as medical decision-making or financial investment decisions. This application-oriented approach would involve collecting real-world data and establishing Markov process models specific to the problem at hand. Utilizing the proposed method in these real-life scenarios would validate its effectiveness in practical settings and develop it into a reliable tool for supporting three-way decisions. Empirical analyses stemming from these applications could provide critical insights, thereby enabling further refinement of the method. This evolution would substantially augment its ability to address complex decision problems effectively.

Illustrative Example

This section illustrates the concept of this study using an example of a store operation. The objective here is to select a strategy that enables the store to reach the target reward in the shortest possible time within a limited number of cycles and to predict the store’s future revenue under the current state.

The operators regularly conduct various promotions on the platform to increase sales. Depending on the nature of the promotions, the sales volume of the products in the store can change to varying degrees. Based on the change in product sales volume, if the sales increase of a product exceeds 10, it is classified as a hot selling item; if the sales decrease by 10, it is classified as a potential item; otherwise, it is considered a promotional item, represented by R^+, R^(\-), and R⁰, respectively. The process of changing product sales in the store with each planned promotion is considered a cycle, typically lasting 3-5 days.

Currently, there are three traffic-driving activities a[1], a[2], a[3] on the platform. For products in the store, appropriate traffic-driving activities are applied to the three different areas R^+, R⁰, R^(\-). The combination of activities applied to the three areas in each cycle is considered a strategy, and the product sales in the store change differently depending on the strategy implemented. Based on experience, three sets of strategies are now set: A[1]\={a[1],a[3],a[2]}, A[2]\={a[3],a[1],a[2]}, A[3]\={a[2],a[2],a[1]}.

A Single State Transition Process

Now, 76 promotions-sensitive products are selected from the store as sample data. In the store’s past data, 20 cycles of data for each strategy A[1], A[2], A[3] are extracted. Based on these data, the initial distribution of hot sales, potential and promotional products for strategies A[1], A[2], A[3] is determined to be \[21,30,25\], \[15,36,25\], \[28,21,27\] respectively. After the 20th cycle, the distribution of these three categories of products changes to \[46,14,16\], \[36,25,15\], \[42,17,17\], respectively. Using the aforementioned method, the state transition probabilities for the store’s products under the influence of strategies A[1], A[2], A[3] can be approximately calculated as follows:

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.m99](i/ch006.m99.png)

Multiple Simulation Results Analysis

Now, we conduct a future utility prediction for the current sales volume change-based tripartition state \[12,26,38\], assuming the store’s target profit is 50,000. Set the ε in the ε-greedy strategy to 0.8, the learning rate σ to 0.1, and the discount factor γ to 0.8. Using the Q-learning algorithm, the Q-table is updated.

| Figure 3. Average cycle variation of multiple simulations |
| --- |
| ![Figure979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.f03](i/ch006.f03.png) |
| --- |

Around 300 training iterations, the number of cycles needed to reach the target profit stabilized at nine cycles. [Figure 3](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.xhtml#f03) shows the trend in the number of cycles required to achieve the target profit over multiple training sessions. It is observed that as the number of training sessions increases, the number of cycles needed to reach the target profit shows a downward trend. After training, the Q-table is exported as shown in [Table 5](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.xhtml#t5):

As can be seen from [Table 5](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch006.xhtml#t5), applying the strategies {A[2], A[1], A[3], A[2], A[3], A[1], A[1], A[3], A[1]} sequentially over nine cycles can achieve the target profit most quickly, providing guidance for the store’s subsequent strategy selection. Following this strategic plan can improve the efficiency and profitability of store operations.

In the last simulation, the rewards generated in cycles 0-8 were approximately 6988.68, 6279.39, 5442.3, 5733.48, 5464.21, 6451.19, 6386.86, 6394.74, and 6393.75, respectively. Therefore, the future utility of the current change-based tripartition state π[c] can be calculated as follows:

G()= 6988.68 + 0.8 × 6279.39 + 0.8² × 5442.3 + 0.8³ × 5733.48 + 0.8⁴ × 5464.21 + 0.8⁵ × 6451.19 + 0.9⁶ × 6386.86 + 0.8⁷ × 6394.74 + 0.8⁸ × 6393.75 ≈26870.91

The above future utility of the change-based tripartition state is based on the maximum profit predicted using the optimal strategy. It holds a significant reference value for assessing the current operational status of the store. The method proposed in this chapter can better assist stores in strategy selection and utility assessment, guiding operators to better manage and run their stores, achieve profit maximization, and enhance market share and competitiveness, thus holding substantial practical significance.

CONCLUSION

This chapter discusses the ‘Acting’ and ‘efficiency’ within the TAO model of change-based three-way decision making, introducing reinforcement learning into strategy selection and utility measurement of change-based three-way decisions. Utilizing Cumulative Prospect Theory, it calculates the rewards generated by state transitions in each cycle and uses these as feedback signals. The Q-learning algorithm is then used to update the Q-table, from which the shortest sequence of strategies to reach the target reward is selected and future utility is predicted.

The introduction of reinforcement learning into the three-way the model enhances decision efficiency and quality of the decision, offering novel and effective solutions for other types of decision models. However, this chapter also has some limitations, such as not considering the issue of changing thresholds for trisecting regions with environmental changes and not describing methods to adjust the parameters of the Q-learning algorithm according to specific problems and environments. Future research will further explore these areas.

# Chapter 7

Smart Supplier Selection

*   Hatice Kübra Özensel  
    Necmettin Erbakan University, Turkey
*   Burak Efe  
    Necmettin Erbakan University, Turkey

ABSTRACT

Supplier selection, which is one of the phases of supply chain management, was examined in this chapter. In the line of four expert opinions and literature review, four main criteria, including environmental, economic, social, and smart criteria, and 18 sub-criteria were evaluated in this study. An interval-valued q-rung orthopair fuzzy (IVqROF) number based MAIRCA (multi attributive ideal-real comparative analysis) method is proposed for the ranking of 5 alternative suppliers. In this chapter, fuzzy number was proposed for the MAIRCA method, but unlike the literature, IVqROF number was applied to the MAIRCA method. It has been seen that the IVqROF number, which is the extension of the q-rung orthopair fuzzy number obtained by developing the intuitionistic fuzzy number and the Pythagorean fuzzy number, provides an advantage in evaluating expert opinions. In addition, Industry 4.0 criteria were examined under the smart criterion and contributed to the supplier selection problem. The study examined the smart supplier selection problem of an automotive sub-industry company.

INTRODUCTION

The retail market is growing day by day by becoming global, and the product produced in one continent can be used as raw material in another continent and sold to other continents. In addition to the advantages that this globalization brings, it also makes supply chain management more complex and difficult for companies. It pushes businesses to seek more systematic, technological and faster ways for sustainable supply chain management. Businesses that can build these systematic, sustainable supply chains and realize their competitiveness with error-free supply processes as well as the right product, provide a competitive advantage over other businesses in the same sector. Despite the importance of the supply chain, it is also seen that until a certain time, not many empirical studies have been carried out by experts on the definition and integration of supply chain management within all other business policies. Supplier selection is the first and one of the most significant links in the supply chain to reduce costs. Many businesses aim to establish strong ties and cooperate with their suppliers in order to rise their competitiveness, to comply with the planned delivery dates given to their customers with the least deviation, and to increase the effectiveness of management ([Shin et al., 2000](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r24)).

Supplier selection has become one of the most important management issues. Supplier selection aims to identify the supplier with the highest performance to consistently meet the needs of the business. Choosing the best supplier among many possible suppliers with various qualifications is a complex task ([Guarnieri and Trojan, 2019](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r13)).

Since customer and supplier relationships are very important in businesses, choosing the right suppliers requires much more than cost comparison and the choices are predicted by various quantitative and qualitative factors. Once a supplier is fully integrated into an adequately run and organized supply chain, that relationship has a long-term impact on the competitiveness. Therefore, the supplier selection problem has a very important role in organizing an effective system for the supply chain. The supplier selection problem aims to decrease purchasing risk, maximize value for the customer, and establish short and long term connections between the consumer and suppliers. Developments in smart technologies over the years inevitably affect supply chains. Advanced smart technologies positioned as components of Industry 4.0, such as the internet of things, cyber systems, big data and artificial intelligence, ensure that all links forming the supply chain are more perceptible, interpretable, predictable, controllable and optimized. Supply chains where these smart technologies are integrated are defined as smart supply chains.

Supply chains are subject to increasingly faced with natural, human-induced or technological risks such as natural disasters, transportation accidents, worker strikes, terrorist attacks due to globalization. The consequences of these factors cause supply chain disruptions due to losses in productivity, profit, competition and revenue. Therefore, making supply chains smart and sustainable has become a necessity to protect the customer from shortages and interruptions. A business with a sustainable supply chain perspective needs to both communicate with the supplier and satisfy the customer, government and shareholders. In addition, in order to produce sustainable products in a sustainable supply chain, sustainability criteria must be taken into consideration when selecting suitable suppliers ([Hosseini and Khaled, 2019](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r15)).

In smart supply chain management, smart technologies are used at every stage, from transportation to the analysis of operators' working processes, to provide higher sustainability in social, environmental and economic terms. Sustainable supplier selection is one of the most significant components for the continuity of this sustainability. Because the actions of suppliers have great importance for sub-companies to achieve sustainable competitive advantage. It is very important to use smart technologies in evaluation process to achieve this sustainability in selection of suppliers ([Chen et al., 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r7); [Ghadimi et al., 2019](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r12)).

Traditional supplier selection is largely based on criteria such as affordable price and close distance. With the use of smart information technologies in supply chains, that smart supplier selection criteria such as supplier information management, warehouse automation, digitalization in the logistics system and data security, have emerged ([Hasan et al., 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r14); [Torğul and Paksoy, 2023](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r27)).

In general, decision makers face many uncertain factors when providing decision preferences for on-demand suppliers. Supplier selection depends on cost, quality, performance, technology, etc. It is a complex problem involving many criteria. Therefore, in order to obtain a systematic supplier selection process in line with existing criteria, a method is needed in which the criteria can be evaluated and prioritized ([Tam and Tummala, 2001](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r26)).

RELATED WORKS

This chapter uses many abbreviations and it can be seen in Appendix 1. In the dealer selection study for a telecommunication company conducted by Tam et al., a review of the decision-making methods in the literature is presented in order to support the dealer selection process. Based on extensive research in the literature, the study proposes a model based on AHP (Analytic Hierarchy Process) to include decision makers with conflicting goals. The result of the case study shows that AHP ensures decreasing the time for supplier selection ([Tam and Tummala, 2001](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r26)). An approach for supplier classification is proposed by integrating ELECTRE (Elemination and Choice Translating Reality English) TRI, which is used in classification problems, and hesitant fuzzy set. The proposed method is examined in the automotive industry ([Galo et al., 2018](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r11)). Kaya and Yet proposed DEMATEL (Decision Making Trial and Evaluation Laboratory) method to form a knowledge-based Bayesian network in line with the information obtained from multiple experts. The proposed method is applied in a case study of supplier selection at an automobile manufacturer ([Kaya and Yet, 2019](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r18)). Liu et al. examined a hybrid multi criteria decision making (MCDM) model combining fuzzy-based BWM (Best Worst Method), VIKOR (Multi-Criteria Optimization and Compromise Solution), PT (Prospect Theory) methods to make innovative supplier selection in the electric vehicle industry ([Liu et al. 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r20)). Bai et al. examined socially sustainable supplier selection using the fuzzy grey number, BWM (Best Worst Method) and TODIM integration ([Bai et al., 2019](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r5)). Liang et al. proposed the prospect theory-based distributed linguistic ORESTE (Organization Rangement et Syntese de Donnees Relationnelles) method to describe the three superiority relationships (preference, indifference, and incomparability) in terms of the limited rationality of the decision maker in line with flexible and intelligent criteria. It is applied in the selection of battery suppliers in new energy vehicles enterprises ([Liang et al., 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r19)). Ghadimi et al. added Industry 4.0 criteria to the sustainable supplier selection problem ([Ghadimi et al., 2019](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r12)). Chen et al. propose the interval valued type-2 fuzzy set based TOPSIS (Technique for Order of Preference by Similarity to Ideal Solution) and DEMATEL methods for smart supplier selection ([Chen et al., 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r7)). Hasan et al. proposed fuzzy set based TOPSIS method to rank the alternative suppliers under logistics 4.0 ([Hasan et al., 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r14)). Pınar and Boran proposed q rung orthopair fuzzy (q-ROF) number based TOPSIS and ELECTRE methods for supplier selection ([Pınar and Boran, 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r23)). Alavi et al. determined the weights of economic, social and cyclical criteria for sustainable supplier selection by using fuzzy best-worst method (BWM). The supplier with the highest performance is defined by using fuzzy inference system ([Alavi et al., 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r2)). [Abdollahzadeh et al. (2023)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r1) handled fuzzy best- worst method and fuzzy gray relational analysis methods to select the food suppliers in hospitals. [Bisht and Pal (2024)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r6) handle social network analysis considering the trust relationships among experts and suggest q rung orthopair fuzzy decision making framework for green supplier selection. [Singh et al. (2024)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r25) used TODIM (TOmada de Decisao Interativa Multicriterio) method based fuzzy prospect theory for optimal hydrogen fuel cell component supplier selection. [Meng et al. (2024)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r21) presented TOPSIS approach based on graph neural networks in Pythagorean fuzzy environment.

Although fuzzy sets are very successful in evaluating uncertainties, they are insufficient in explaining the degree of non-membership/hesitancy of decision makers. In order to fill this deficiency of fuzzy sets, Atanassov presented an intuitionistic fuzzy set model in which both membership and non-membership degrees can be evaluated ([Atanassov, 1986](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r4); [Ali et al., 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r3)).

Pythagorean fuzzy sets, which are considered an extension of intuitionistic fuzzy set models, were introduced as a result of the need for a wider definition range. In an intuitionistic fuzzy set, the sum of the membership and non-membership degrees is allowed to be at most 1, and if it is less than 1, the difference is defined as hesitancy. In Pythagorean fuzzy sets, the sum of the squares of the membership and non-membership degrees is allowed to be at most 1 for a wider definition range. However, Pythagorean fuzzy sets are also lacking in some real applications. In order to achieve more accurate results in these real practices, a generalized q-ROF sets model can adjust the restriction on membership and non-membership degrees from a parameter type as an extension of Pythagorean fuzzy sets ([Ali et al., 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r3)).

Some studies used q rung orthopair fuzzy number for different areas such as natural gas pipeline project risk assessment ([Efe and Efe, 2023b](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r9)) and glove design ([Efe and Efe, 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r10)). MAIRCA based on q rung orthopair fuzzy number was presented for selecting emergency service of patients. ([Efe and Efe, 2023a](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r8)). It seems that the q-ROF sets model and its hybrid structures, which are a powerful idea, attracted the attention of researchers and caused them to deepen their studies on it. One of these studies presented a hybrid structure integrating interval-valued fuzzy set and q-ROF set ([Joshi et al., 2018](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r16)).

This chapter contributes to the literature as follows:

*   •MAIRCA is extended by using interval valued q-ROF number unlike existing studies in the literature. This study simultaneously ensures the advantages of MAIRCA and interval valued q-ROF number. It ensures to prevent information loss of the experts for the weights of criteria and criteria based alternative evaluation.
*   •Smart supplier selection is a new trend. This chapter also handled Industry 4.0 criteria except traditional supplier selection criteria. It used a novel method, which is interval valued q-ROF number based MAIRCA, for smart supplier selection problem.

MAIN FOCUS OF THE CHAPTER

This section presents the steps of MAIRCA method and definitions of interval valued q-ROF number.

The steps of the MAIRCA method are summarized below ([Pamucar et al., 2018](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r22)).

*   Step 1:
    
    Establishing the decision matrix. The criterion (C[j]) values for each alternative (A[i]) are included in Equation (1).
    
    C1 C2 … Cn
    
    ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m01](i/ch007.m01.png) (1)
    
*   Step 2:
    
    It is essential for the decision maker to be impartial. While m is the total number of alternatives, the priority of i^(th) alternative (PAi) is calculated with the help of Equation (2). Since the decision maker is equidistant from each of the alternatives, all priorities are equal as in Equation 3.
    
    [] = ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m02](i/ch007.m02.png)[] = 1; i=1, 2, 3,…..m (2)
    
    [1]\=[2]\=⋯=[] (3)
    
*   Step 3:
    
    Establishing of the matrix of theoretical ponder. [] matrix is found by multiplying the priorities of the alternatives ([]) with weights the criteria (w[j]).
    
    T[P=]![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m03](i/ch007.m03.png) (4)
    
*   Step 4:
    
    The matrix of real ponder ([]) matrix is obtained by making use of the [] matrix and matrix. Matrix elements are calculated with the help of equation (5) for maximization type criteria and equation (6) for minimization type criteria.
    
    ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m04](i/ch007.m04.png) (5)
    
    ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m05](i/ch007.m05.png) (6)
    

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m06](i/ch007.m06.png) shows the maximum value getting from alternative (![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m07](i/ch007.m07.png)\= max ([1], [2], …, []), ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m08](i/ch007.m08.png) shows the minimum value getting from alternative (![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m09](i/ch007.m09.png)\= min ([1], [2], …, []).

*   Step 5:
    
    The total gap matrix (TG) is obtained by subtracting the matrix of real ponder ([r]) from the matrix of theoretical ponder ([]) with the help of Equations (7)-(8).
    
    []\=[]\- [] (7)
    
    ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m10](i/ch007.m10.png) (8)
    
*   Step 6:
    
    In the last step of the method, the sum of the criterion values in TG matrix is found separately for each alternative with the help of equation (9). The alternative with the smallest value among the values obtained from this equation is considered the best alternative.
    
    ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m11](i/ch007.m11.png)g[ij], i=1,2,….,m (9)
    

[Yager (2016)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r28) proposed the q-ROF number to overcome the shortcomings of the intuitionistic fuzzy number and the Pythagorean fuzzy number. IVqROF number whose definition is given below, has been proposed by expanding q-ROF numbers. [Ju et al. (2019)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#r17) defined IVq-ROF number operators below.

*   Definition 1: Let X be a non-empty fixed set, an IVq-ROF number on X can be defined as follows:
    
    ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m12](i/ch007.m12.png) (10)

It must be ensured ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m15](i/ch007.m15.png) ⊆ \[0,1\], ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m16](i/ch007.m16.png) ⊆ \[0,1\], ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m17](i/ch007.m17.png) conditions.

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m18](i/ch007.m18.png)

shows the hesitancy of x∈X.

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m19](i/ch007.m19.png)

is shown as ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m20](i/ch007.m20.png) to ensure the convenience.

*   Definition 2: Let be ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m21](i/ch007.m21.png) and ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m22](i/ch007.m22.png) two IVq-ROF numbers.
    
    ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m23](i/ch007.m23.png) (11)
    
    ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m24](i/ch007.m24.png) (12)

    ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m26](i/ch007.m26.png), λ>0 (14)
    
*   Definition 3: If ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m27](i/ch007.m27.png) is an IVq-ROF number, score function S(a) and accuracy function P(a) are defined below.
    
    S(a) =![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m28](i/ch007.m28.png) (15)
    
    P(a) =![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m29](i/ch007.m29.png) (16)
    

For 0≤ S(a)≤1, the higher the S(a) score point, the higher the IVq-ROF number will be. If S(a)=1 then a=\[1,1\],\[0,0\]; if S(a)=0 then a=\[0,0\],\[1,1\]. Likewise, for 0≤ P(a)≤1, the higher the P(a) score, the higher the IVq-ROF number will be. The score function is used to rank fuzzy numbers.

*   Definition 4: IVq-ROF numbers (a[1],a[2],…,a[n]) can be combined by using geometric mean aggregation operator below. This operator is used to aggregate the opinions of the experts.
    
    \=![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.m30](i/ch007.m30.png) (17)
    
*   Definition 5: If a[1]\= (\[a[1],b[1]\],\[c[1],d[1]\]) and a[2]\= (\[a[2],b[2]\],\[c[2],d[2]\]) are two IVq-ROF numbers, the distance among them is calculated as follows:

SOLUTIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS

In this part of the study, the application of this new proposed model in a company in the automotive sub-industry sector is shown. The specified business was established in 1988 and is one of the pioneers in its sector. In addition to Automotive, it also has over 1000 types of products in the Marine-Locomotive and Stationary Engine groups. [Table 1](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#t1) shows the pseudo code of the proposed method.

Table 1. The pseudo code

| Inputs: The expert group, criteria, alternatives, the judgments of expert group, the value of q |
| --- |
| \-begin |
| --- |
| \-Start algorithm and data entries |
| for each expert, collect the criteria weights judgments |
| for E1 to E4 |
| \- Collect the judgment of each criteria weight by using linguistic terms  
\- Transform to q-ROF numbers from linguistic terms  
End |
| Aggregate the judgments of experts |
| for each expert, collect the criteria based alternative judgments |
| for E1 to E4 |
| \- Collect the judgment of each criteria based alternative by using linguistic terms  
\- Transform to q-ROF numbers from linguistic terms  
End |
| Aggregate the judgments of experts |
| Calculate the criteria based alternative  
Apply the steps of MAIRCA based on IVqROF number |
| Rank the alternative suppliers and find the best supplier |
| End |

In the first stage of the application, 18 sub-criteria under 4 main criteria and 5 different alternatives (A1, A2…A5) were determined using the experience of 4 experts (E1, E2, E3, E4). The obtained data through a comprehensive literature research and the opinions of the experts are presented in [Table 2](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#t2). Expert 1 is the general coordinator who has over 20 years of experience in the business and has visited the suppliers determined as alternatives. Expert 2 is the purchasing manager who has been working in the business for 8 years. Expert 3 is the purchasing engineer who currently carries out purchasing activities in the business, Expert 4 is the stock manager who has been working in the industry for 15 years and extensive experience and deep technical knowledge about the product supplied.

Table 2. Smart supplier selection criteria

| Main criteria  || Subcriteria |
| --- | --- |
| Economical (C1) || Price (C11) |
| Quality (C12) |
| Delivery time (C13) |
| Technology capacity (C14) |
| Flexibility (C15) |
| Environmental (C2) || Environment pollution control (C21) |
| Environment management system (C22) |
| Eco-design recycling (C23) |
| Green point (C24) |
| Social (C3) || Occupational health and safety (C31) |
| Staff development (C32) |
| Local community effect (C33) |
| Information disclosure (C34) |
| Smart (C4) || Industry 4.0 staff (C41) |
| Digital customer relations (C42) |
| Smart maintenance (C43) |
| Industry 4.0 utilization (C44) |
| Prediction ability (C45) |

The price criterion located under the economical criterion is the minimization type and is used to evaluate the high cost of the product to be supplied. The quality criterion is the maximization type and was utilized to handle the quality standards of supplier. Delivery time is the minimization type and was used to evaluate the length of delivery time offered by the supplier in line with its location and logistics management. The technology capacity is the maximization type and was employed to handle the pace of development and future production capabilities of supplier. The flexibility criterion is the maximization type and was utilized to handle the shortness of lead time, speed of resolving disputes, and adaptability of supplier.

Environment pollution control and environment management systems, which fall under the main environmental criterion, are maximization type criteria and were used to evaluate the abundance of the supplier's documentation on environmental management and the degree of procedures they have implemented to protect the environment from manufacturing waste. Eco-design recycling criterion is the maximization type and are used to evaluate the extent to which production waste is recycled. The green point is the maximization type and was used to evaluate how packaging waste is utilized by suppliers.

The occupational health and safety criterion, which is under the social main criterion, is the maximization type and is used to evaluate the occupational health and safety documents and procedures of the suppliers. The staff development criterion is the maximization type and was used to evaluate the supplier's personnel training. The local community effect is the maximization type and has been used to assess the supplier's returns to the community in which it resides. Information disclosure is the minimization type and is used to assess the supplier's rate of sharing and dissemination of existing data.

The criteria under the smart criterion are determined for smart supplier selection. Industry 4.0 staff and industry 4.0 utilization criteria are maximization type criteria and were used to evaluate the integration rate of the units and personnel within the supplier into smart technologies. The digital customer relations criterion is the maximization type and was used to evaluate the supplier's current infrastructure and speed that will enable the customer to quickly communicate their demands through the digital environment. The smart maintenance criterion is the maximization type and is used to evaluate the rate at which the supplier maintains the product it provides by using smart technologies such as artificial intelligence and the internet of things, detects its malfunction, repairs it or prevents it. The prediction ability criterion is the maximization type and was employed to handle the ability of supplier to forecast customer demand by analyzing historical customer data through smart technologies.

The weights of criteria were calculated using the interval-valued q-ROF number in line with the linguistic data collected from 4 experts. In the study, q = 3 was accepted. In the first stage, the opinions of experts were received from experts for 4 main criteria and 18 sub-criteria in line with linguistic data. [Table 3](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#t3) shows the linguistic terms and their IVq-ROF number scales. These scales are used to determine the importance degrees of criteria.

In the last part of the application, 5 alternatives were ranked with the IVq-ROF number based MAIRCA method. First, 4 experts were asked to evaluate the performance of the alternatives according to the criteria in line with the linguistic term scales in [Table 3](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#t3). [Table 11](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#t11) shows the importance ratings of the alternatives determined by experts in linguistic terms according to the criteria. Group opinions were obtained by using the IVq-ROF number aggregation operator in Eq. 17. [Table 12](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#t12) shows the group opinions of experts for criteria based alternatives.

After obtaining group opinions, the steps of the IVq-ROF numbers based MAIRCA method were applied. The group opinions in [Table 12](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#t12) were multiplied as in Equation 12 to obtain the weighted normalized decision matrix, which is the first stage of IVq-ROF number based MAIRCA. [Table 13](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#t13) shows the weighted normalized decision matrix for the IVq-ROF numbers based MAIRCA method.

In the total gap matrix, the criterion ratings of each alternative were added as in Eq. (9), the weight degrees of the alternatives were calculated. The alternative with the smallest value among the obtained values was accepted as the best alternative. [Table 17](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#t17) shows the ranking of alternatives. In the ranking obtained by IVqROF numbers based MAIRCA, the most suitable smart supplier alternative for the business was found to be A2. A2 alternative is followed by A1-A4-A3 and A5 alternatives, respectively. A sensitivity analysis is performed on the impact of varying q values and the result is shown in [Figure 1](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#f01). Different q values present the same ranking for suppliers. [Figure 2](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch007.xhtml#f02) presents a comparison analysis. The proposed method is compared with classic MAIRCA, VIKOR and TOPSIS methods. The results of the proposed method and classic MAIRCA are the same. The results of the proposed method, classic VIKOR and TOPSIS are the same except A3 and A5. This shows that the proposed method is useful for supplier selection problem.

FUTURE RESEARCH DIRECTIONS

The limitation of this chapter is to define the weights of the criteria by using an aggregation operator. It aims to use a pairwise comparison matrix among criteria in future studies. This proposed new MCDM (IVqROF numbers based MAIRCA) method can be used in all complex decision making problems involving conflicting and uncertain criteria, except for supplier selection or smart supplier selection problems. There are many MCDM methods in the literature and new methods continue to be added. In future studies, research can be conducted using interval-valued q-ROF number integration of different decision-making methods. Interval valued q-ROF numbers can be developed with new extensions and used as a basis for both MAIRCA method and other MCDM methods in the literature.

CONCLUSION

The concept of supply chain is an indication that no business has complete control over the production and delivery of the products it provides to the customer. The participation of other businesses involved throughout all processes and the interactions between different parties are also important. In this period where increasingly strong competition prevails under the influence of changing environmental and social factors in recent years and businesses strive to provide competitive advantage, a strong supply chain is considered the key to companies' continued success. For this reason, in order to build strong supply chains, businesses examine the links that make up the supply chain and make efforts to improve, strengthen and make each of them sustainable. In this improvement process, they include their suppliers in the process with performance scoring reports and development programs.

Supply chain management has become very important in real life businesses and literature due to increasing globalization. Businesses aim to establish strategic collaborations with the most ideal suppliers in order to increase their competitiveness by building efficient supply chains. Choosing the right supplier will significantly reduce the total cost, as the supplier selection decisions are very effective in the profitability of the business and directly affect the delivery times of the enterprises. However, this process is complex and difficult because supplier selection may involve multiple conflicting and unclear criteria.

In its simplest form, a decision problem can be thought of as making a choice among options according to a goal or criterion. Decision makers need accurate and reliable predictions for the decisions they will make. Taking scientific criteria into consideration while doing this enables them to make better decisions. Classical MCDM techniques are often not sufficient to evaluate these unclear and contradictory criteria.

In this study, MAIRCA method based on interval valued q-ROF numbers, which is not included in the literature, is proposed to evaluate conflicting and uncertain criteria. This new proposal was applied on the smart supplier selection problem in an automotive supplier industry company. In addition to the traditional criteria, smart criteria have been added with the detailed literature study while determining the criteria for smart supplier selection. In the study, interval valued q-ROF numbers were used to reflect the uncertainties in the statements of the experts to the problem. In order to investigate the weights of the criteria constituting the concept of smart supplier selection for alternatives, the linguistic expression definitions of the experts were transformed into fuzzy numerical expressions by using IVqROF numbers based MAIRCA.
