# Chapter 9

*   Yuge Niu  
    [![Orcid Image](i/orcid.svg)https://orcid.org/0009-0000-5242-8090](https://orcid.org/0009-0000-5242-8090)  
    Shanxi University, China
*   Kexin Liu  
    [![Orcid Image](i/orcid.svg)https://orcid.org/0009-0005-4336-6170](https://orcid.org/0009-0005-4336-6170)  
    Shanxi University, China
*   Fanghui Lu  
    Shanxi University, China
*   Jiayi Zhang  
    Trinity College Dublin, Ireland

ABSTRACT

Multi-attribute decision-making (MADM) analysis is an important field widely used in the decision-making process. As the volume of data increases, it becomes critical to have a comprehensive understanding of large-scale data collection. However, current research lacks a holistic approach to obtaining large-scale data. This chapter aims to address this research gap by summarizing classic papers, open datasets, remote sensing data, sentiment analysis, and questionnaire survey data collection forms in MADM research. Classic papers provide a wealth of foundational knowledge, while open datasets provide diverse and large-scale data. Additionally, remote sensing data provides real-time information for urban planning and environmental management decisions. Finally, sentiment analysis leverages social media to gain unique insights, and questionnaire surveys are valid. Overall, this chapter helps researchers and professionals improve their selection and design of data collection methods to ensure reliable and impactful data collection, thereby improving the ability to make informed decisions.

INTRODUCTION

With the rapid development of emerging technologies such as Internet technology, cloud computing, and the Internet of Things, the scale of data has grown explosively. The accumulation and storage of massive amounts of data provide huge opportunities and challenges for national decision-making and enterprise operations. This data contains a wealth of information in various fields that can help people better understand how the real world works and evolves. Big data dynamically reflect the development trend of various economic and social indicators, and reflects the effect of policy implementation in multiple dimensions and levels, making social management more refined and intelligent, and providing people with a more scientific and data-based decision-making thinking ([Yaqoob et al., 2016](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r53); [Zhang et al., 2024](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r61)). MADM is an important part of decision science, which refers to the decision-making process that requires comprehensive consideration of multiple attribute factors in the decision-making process to achieve the optimal decision-making goal ([Zanakis et al., 1998](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r55)). Decision-making problems in real life are often affected by multiple attribute factors, and the influence of multiple attributes needs to be considered comprehensively ([Zhang, 2008a](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r65); [Wang et al., 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r46)). By comprehensively considering multiple attribute factors, we can evaluate the decision-making object more comprehensively, grasp the decision-making environment more accurately, and make more scientific and reasonable decisions, which is helpful to improve the accuracy and effectiveness of decision-making ([Zhang & Yager, 2008b](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r66); [Zhang et al., 2023](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r62)).

Data collection is a crucial part of MADM research, as its quality and comprehensiveness directly affect the final decision outcome ([Platts et al., 2002](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r31)). At present, there are five main data collection methods commonly used in academia:

*   (1) Obtaining Data from Classic Papers: Classic papers refer to research results that have important influence and representation in a certain field. Classic papers contain a wealth of data that can be used for the analysis and decision-making of MADM. Through in-depth analysis and evaluation of this data, decision-makers (DMs) can better understand the nature and context of the problem, so that they can make more accurate decisions ([Van Oudenhoven et al., 2018](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r44)). These data can be used to establish a variety of different types of decision-making models, such as analytic hierarchy process (AHP), fuzzy comprehensive evaluation method, entropy weight method, etc., and can also be used to optimize decision-making schemes, so as to help DMs evaluate and compare different decision-making schemes, so as to choose the optimal scheme and improve the scientificity and efficiency of decision-making ([Zhang et al., 2019](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r60)). However, the data in classic papers may have problems such as update lag, difficulty in obtaining, and lack of multi-dimensional information.
*   (2) Sentiment Analysis: Sentiment analysis, also known as opinion mining or sentiment analysis, refers to the use of natural language processing and machine learning (ML) techniques to identify and understand the emotions and sentiments expressed in text. There is a close relationship between sentiment analysis and multi-attribute decision making ([Ji et al., 2018](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r19)). Sentiment analysis can help DMs gain a more comprehensive understanding of the emotional tendencies and attitudes of decision objects, thereby better considering the emotional factors of decision objects in MADM (Tan et al., 2022; [Awajan et al., 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r1)). By taking into account the results of sentiment analysis, emotional factors can be incorporated into the consideration scope of MADM, thus enabling a more comprehensive evaluation of the impact of different choices on emotional factors ([Wu & Zhang, 2019](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r50)). This can help in formulating decisions and strategies that better align with market demands and consumer emotional preferences. Therefore, sentiment analysis provides a more detailed and comprehensive decision basis for MADM, contributing to improved decision accuracy and specificity. However, sentiment analysis can also lead to biased results due to problems such as imperfection of emotional vocabulary, language and cultural biases.
*   (3) Remote Sensing Data: Remote sensing data are the information of the earth's surface obtained through long-distance means such as satellites and aircraft, including various attributes such as land cover type, temperature, humidity and so on ([Zhang et al., 2016](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r64)). These multi-attribute remote sensing data can be widely used in environmental monitoring, urban planning, agricultural production and other fields. In MADM, you need to consider the correlation and impact between different attributes. Remote sensing data provide information on a variety of attributes, which can provide comprehensive geographic information, real-time information, high accuracy, and cross-regionality, and provide a scientific basis for decision-making. Data support for MADM, e.g., in urban planning, environmental protection, and agricultural resource management. Through the analysis of multi-attribute information of remote sensing data, it can provide DMs with a more comprehensive understanding and more accurate decision-making basis.
*   (4) Open Datasets: Open datasets refer to publicly available and accessible datasets provided by governments, organizations, or communities. These datasets cover a wide range of topics, such as population statistics, environmental data, economic data, and more. They can contain various attributes relevant to decision-making, providing more comprehensive and accurate information and avoiding subjectivity and bias in the decision-making process. By analyzing these datasets, correlations, trends, and other information can be extracted to help DMs better understand the context of their decisions ([Barlas et al., 2015](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r2)). Open datasets can provide historical and trend data for analysis, foster community engagement, and save time and costs associated with data collection ([Zhang et al., 2020a](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r57)). However, they may have data incompleteness, privacy and security issues, as well as data quality and consistency concerns, which can affect the reliability and accuracy of MADM ([Immonen et al., 2018](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r17)).
*   (5) Questionnaire Survey: The questionnaire survey method is a scientific approach to accurately grasp the phenomena or problems in the complex macro world through effective use of numbers. It is a research method that provides a realistic basis for solving problems, improving the current situation, and planning for the future. It is a method of studying the objective facts produced by various social forces behind social phenomena. The emergence and widespread use of the survey method provide a feasible way for DMs to obtain objective and effective data. If researchers can effectively use this method, they undoubtedly have a powerful tool to access unknown areas of knowledge. The questionnaire is the most frequently used data collection tool or instrument for quantitative studies especially surveys ([Taherdoost, 2016](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r39)).

The contributions to this chapter are as follows:

*   (1) This chapter will comprehensively discuss various data collection methods, including open datasets, remote sensing data, sentiment analysis, questionnaire survey, etc. Through a comprehensive comparison of these methods, researchers and practitioners can better choose the appropriate data collection method.
*   (2) This chapter will focus on the practical application of different data collection methods. By discussing how to access and leverage this data, it is possible to provide practical recommendations for DMs to better use the data to support decision-making analysis.
*   (3) This chapter will focus on newer data collection methods, such as remote sensing data and sentiment analysis. The application of these methods in MADM research has been relatively seldom explored. Through the discussion of these emerging methods, researchers and practitioners can be provided with new ideas and methods to enrich the data sources of MADM research.

The remainder of the chapter is organized as follows: Section 2 delves into the evolution of the five data collection methodologies within the realm of MADM. Section 3 elaborates on the application of these five data collection techniques to MADM, accompanied by illustrative examples. Section 4 addresses the obstacles associated with these methodologies and suggests potential trends for future research. Lastly, Section 5 provides a summary of the entire text.

LITERATURE REVIEW

Literature Review on Obtaining Data From Classic Papers

Obtaining data from classic papers, which refers to extracting data from published research papers for subsequent analysis and application, is a common research method in which researchers can collect, organize, and analyze data from the existing literature to support their own research ([Zahedi-Seresht et al., 2016](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r54)). This approach is commonly used in study design, such as literature reviews, meta-analyses, and secondary data analysis. In the field of MADM, there are also many researchers who obtain data from classic papers. Operators, fuzzy sets, and granular computation are important mathematical tools in the MADM. In terms of operators, [Zhang et al. (2018)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r63) used [Wei (2017)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r47) numerical examples to verify the effectiveness of the proposed method in the study of fuzzy Dombi Heronian mean operators and its application in MADM. In terms of fuzzy sets, [Li et al. (2019)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r22) used the example of [Ju and Wang (2013)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r20) as a numerical example in the study of multi-attribute group decision-making. In terms of granular computing, [Zhang et al. (2020b)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r58) used the data of [Zhang et al. (2017)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r59) in order to effectively compare and analyze with the existing multi-attribute group decision-making methods in the study of multi-granularity probabilistic models to deal with multi-attribute group decision-making problems and interval neutrosophic information. Obtaining data from classic papers helps policymakers understand historical decision-making processes and outcomes, leading to a better understanding of the current decision-making environment and challenges ([Brehmer, 1992](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r4); [Cobo et al., 2014](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r8)). This historical data can also be used to predict future trends and directions ([Sanchez-Nunez et al., 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r35)), thereby helping DMs make more accurate and reliable decisions.

Literature Review of Sentiment Analysis

The term “sentiment analysis” was coined by [Nasukawa and Yi (2003)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r30). Sentiment analysis can be conducted at three levels: sentence level ([Tang et al., 2015](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r41)), document level ([Pu et al., 2019](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r32)), and aspect level ([Ma et al., 2019](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r25); [Nandhini & Pradeep, 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r28); [Nandwani & Verma, 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r29)). Sentiment analysis techniques are typically categorized into dictionary-based methods ([Bernabe-Moreno et al., 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r3)), machine learning-based methods ([Tiwari et al., 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r42); [Maslej-Kresnakova et al., 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r26)), deep learning-based methods ([Dashtipour et al., 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r9)), and hybrid methods ([Ersahin et al., 2019](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r11)). The application of sentiment analysis in MADM can be divided into two categories. One is text-based sentiment analysis, used to extract sentiment information from user feedback, comments, and other text to provide references for decision-making. The other category is emotion recognition-based MADM, which considers emotional factors as one of the decision attributes. Regarding text-based sentiment analysis, previous studies have proposed sentiment analysis methods based on sentiment lexicons. For instance, [Cheng et al. (2023)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r6) constructed a sentiment lexicon for blood glucose meters and combined text analysis techniques such as text cleaning to extract and analyze user evaluations of different blood glucose meters, using the results as an important indicator for blood glucose meter selection. Additionally, researchers have utilized ML algorithms to conduct sentiment mining on large-scale text data, providing support for product marketing, public opinion monitoring, and other MADM. [Rahman and Hossen (2019)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r33) used five ML classifiers, including Bernoulli Naive Bayes, decision trees, support vector machines, and maximum entropy, to perform sentiment analysis on collected movie data reviews, thereby supporting the corresponding MADM. [Wu et al. (2023)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r51) conducted sentiment analysis on medical service reviews using the deep neural network BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers) to support patient selection of online doctors. As for emotion recognition-based MADM, some studies have combined psychological theories and decision models to explore the influence of emotions on decision outcomes, providing theoretical foundations for decision-making behaviors under different emotional states. [Sudhakaran et al. (2022)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r38) developed an emotion recognition-based music model by integrating psychological theories, facial expressions, and deep learning technology. In summary, applying sentiment analysis to MADM can help DMs better understand public opinion, improve the objectivity and democracy of decision-making, and thus better meet public needs and optimize decision outcomes.

Literature Review of Remote Sensing Data

The application of remote sensing data in MADM can be classified into qualitative and quantitative categories. Qualitative applications mainly involve using remote sensing data for land cover classification and identification, providing spatial information support for decision-making. Quantitative applications, on the other hand, involve extracting land cover features from remote sensing data and combining them with other attribute data for multi-attribute decision analysis.

In the literature review, researchers have focused on the qualitative application of remote sensing data in MADM, particularly on the classification methods and techniques of remote sensing imagery, as well as the application of different remote sensing data in land cover identification and classification. For instance, studies have been conducted on land use type classification using high-resolution remote sensing imagery ([Shao et al., 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r36); [Cao et al., 2023](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r5)), as well as on estimating vegetation cover using multi-source remote sensing data ([Hu et al., 2017](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r15)). In terms of quantitative applications, researchers have concentrated on the fusion methods of remote sensing data with other attribute data and the construction of multi-attribute decision analysis models. For instance, research has been conducted on using remote sensing data to extract urban land use information and combining it with economic, social, and other multi-attribute data for urban planning decisions ([Liu et al., 2017](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r24)), as well as on environmental monitoring using remote sensing data and combining it with pollution sources, meteorological, and other multi-attribute data for environmental management decisions ([Ehlers et al., 2006](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r10)).

In conclusion, the application of remote sensing data in MADM covers qualitative and quantitative categories. The relevant literature review demonstrates the wide-ranging application prospects of remote sensing data in land cover identification and classification, multi-attribute decision analysis, and other areas. However, further research and exploration are still needed to enhance the effectiveness and level of application of remote sensing data in MADM.

Literature Review of Open Datasets

The development of open datasets can be traced back to the 1980s. At that time, governments and academic institutions gradually realized the importance of data and began to open up large amounts of data so that more people could access and utilize this information. In this phase, open datasets mainly consisted of government data and academic research data, and were limited by the technology and network conditions of the time, making it relatively difficult to access and utilize the data ([Uhlir & Schroder, 2007](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r43)). With the widespread popularity of the internet and technological advancements, open datasets began to be more widely promoted and applied. In 2007, the US government launched the data.gov website, which made a large number of government datasets available to the public, marking the beginning of open government data. Subsequently, more and more countries and organizations have also launched similar open data platforms. Open datasets in this phase also covered a wider range of fields, including economics, society, environment, and various other aspects of data.

The development of open datasets has also benefited from the open-source community and the rapid development of data science ([Janssen, et al., 2012](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r18)). Open datasets provide rich resources for data scientists, engineers, and researchers, which has promoted the development of data analysis and data mining. At the same time, the application of open data has gradually expanded into commercial and social fields, providing new possibilities for innovation and problem-solving.

With the development of artificial intelligence and big data technology, open datasets have played an important role in intelligent applications and data-driven decision-making. In the early 21st century, an increasing number of governments and non-governmental organizations began promoting the release and utilization of open datasets. Some countries and regions established specialized open data platforms to encourage public and private sectors to share data and promote data reuse and secondary development ([Harper & Konstan, 2015](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r14)).

Entering the mid-21st century, open datasets became a global trend, expanding into various industries and fields. In addition to government-released open datasets, more and more enterprises and research institutions began opening up their own data to promote innovation and collaboration ([Molloy, 2011](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r27)).

In the digital and intelligent age, open datasets have become an important support for promoting economic development, scientific research, and social governance. An increasing number of countries and regions have begun to attach importance to open data policies, intensifying the construction of open datasets, and further expanding the application scope of open datasets in various fields.

Literature Review of Questionnaire Survey

The survey method first appeared in 1882 when British anthropologist Galton established an anthropological testing laboratory in London, printing the questions to be investigated on questionnaires, distributing and collecting these questionnaires to collect relevant data. Since then, survey methods have played a significant role in exploring and understanding the mysteries and regularities of social phenomena. Researchers analyze and make decisions based on the collected data (Cobanoglu et al., 2003). With the development of new technologies and improvements in various statistical analysis methods, survey methods have become faster and more scientific in collecting and organizing data, which is an important reason for their widespread use. Some scholars refer to the survey method as a “written survey method” and a “fill-in-the-blank method,” with the key point being that researchers provide designed questionnaires to relevant individuals in any feasible way according to standardized procedures, and then analyze the results to obtain information, thus making decisions and predictions ([Wettergren, et al., 2016](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r48)).

The mid to late 20th century saw a significant shift towards computer-assisted survey methods, which revolutionized data collection and analysis. The advent of online surveys and data collection platforms in the 21st century further transformed the landscape of survey research, making it more accessible and cost-effective ([Wright, 2005](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r49)).

Today, survey methods continue to be a fundamental tool for collecting and analyzing data in various fields, including social sciences, marketing, public opinion research, and healthcare. The ongoing development of survey techniques, including adaptive survey designs, mobile data collection, and advanced statistical methods, ensures that survey research remains a versatile and powerful approach for understanding human behavior and attitudes.

The survey technique is an efficient way to collect large sample data, making it easy to analyze and present data in a structured way. There are various classification methods in survey research, each with its own advantages and limitations; however, they complement each other. Through survey collection and investigation, researchers can obtain effective and rigorous results, helping them make the most accurate decisions.

SPECIFIC METHODS

Specific Methods of Obtaining Data from Classic Papers for MADM

A specific step to obtain data from a classic paper and apply it to MADM is as follows:

*   (1) Determine data needs: Determine what data to take from a classic paper, which may involve experimental data, survey results, statistics, etc.
*   (2) Literature research: Search academic databases, journals or related professional websites (such as Google Scholar, IEEE Xplore, Elsevier, Springer, etc.) to find relevant classic papers to ensure the credibility and academic value of the papers. Obtain experimental data, case data, survey data, etc. from papers to support data analysis and decision-making for MADM.
*   (3) Organize the data: Organize the data obtained from the paper into a structured form, including attributes, attribute values, weights, and other information. This may require converting the descriptive data in the text into a data format that can be calculated and analyzed, such as converting the description into digital data.
*   (4) Determine attribute weights: Determine the weight of each attribute according to the method or experimental results proposed in the paper. Common methods include AHP, principal component analysis, etc. According to the specific method in the paper, the data need to be processed and calculated to obtain the weight value of each attribute.
*   (5) Analyze the data: Use the data and methods in the paper to analyze the data of MADM objects. This may include calculating the score or ranking of individual attributes, calculating the overall score, etc. These analyses can be carried out based on the models or methods proposed in the paper, ensuring the accuracy and reliability of the data analysis.
*   (6) Make decisions: According to the results of data analysis, combined with the weight of multiple attributes, formulate decision-making strategies or make decision-making recommendations. This may include the evaluation, ranking, or scoring of different decision-making options, as well as the final decision recommendation or decision-making basis.

It is important to note that when obtaining data, it is important to comply with the relevant laws and regulations and data use agreements. Some data may be subject to copyright protection or require special permissions for access and use. Therefore, it is important to be mindful of the relevant legal requirements when obtaining data.

Next, let us take the example of a research in the field of environmental management, with the purpose of evaluating the environmental quality of different cities in order to formulate environmental protection policies, to better explain this form of data collection. Here is an example of a concrete step (The full framework is presented in [Figure 1](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#f01).):

| Figure 1. The framework of environmental quality protection MADM. |
| --- |
| ![Figure979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.f01](i/ch009.f01.png) |
| --- |

*   (1) Gather data on the environmental quality of each city, including air quality index, water quality index, garbage disposal, green space coverage, etc.
*   (2) Conduct a thorough search of academic databases to identify a seminal paper that offers a comprehensive investigation and evaluation of the environmental quality of different cities. The chosen paper should provide detailed data and analysis results. In order to facilitate a better understanding, we have vividly demonstrated how to search for relevant papers on the IEEE Xplore official website, as shown in [Figure 2](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#f02).
*   (3) Extract relevant data such as air quality index, water quality index, garbage disposal, and green space coverage of each city from the selected paper. Organize this information into a structured format for further analysis and decision-making.
*   (4) Determine the weight of each environmental quality attribute based on the methodology or experimental results outlined in the selected paper. This may involve establishing the importance and weight value of each indicator according to survey results and expert opinions presented in the paper.
*   (5) Utilize the data and methodologies outlined in the selected paper to analyze the environmental quality of each city. Calculate scores or rankings for each index to obtain a comprehensive environmental quality evaluation.
*   (6) Based on the results of the data analysis and the assigned weight of each index, develop environmental protection policies or decision-making recommendations. This may involve proposing tailored environmental protection measures and policy recommendations for different cities.

| ![Figure979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.f02](i/ch009.f02.png) |
| --- |

Specific Methods of Sentiment Analysis for MADM

A specific step-by-step application of sentiment analysis to MADM is as follows:

*   (1) Determine the decision object: Clearly identify the object for MADM, such as a product, service, or brand.
*   (2) Collect data: Collect relevant text data for MADM, such as user reviews, surveys, social media comments, etc. These data should cover multiple attributes, such as product performance, price, appearance, etc. Researchers also use datasets from the field of emotion and sentiment analysis, such as SemEval, Stanford Sentiment Treebank, International Survey on Affective Reasoning and Emotion, to evaluate the effectiveness of their models. Additionally, using web scraping tools to collect online reviews is also a good form of data collection.
*   (3) Data preprocessing: Clean and preprocess the collected data, including removing noise, text standardization, and sentiment word extraction, to prepare for sentiment analysis.
*   (4) Sentiment analysis: Use sentiment analysis techniques to identify and analyze the emotional tendencies in the text data. Sentiment analysis can be based on ML models or deep learning models to recognize the emotional polarity (positive, negative, neutral) and emotional intensity in the text.
*   (5) Determine attribute weights: Based on the multiple attributes of the decision object, determine the weight of each attribute using methods such as expert evaluation, AHP, data mining, etc.
*   (6) Calculate Sentiment score: Typically involves sentiment word extraction, classification, and emotional intensity calculation. Then, calculate the sentiment score and polarity of the text based on the emotional intensity values and frequency of occurrence of sentiment words to evaluate the emotional impact of users on each attribute.
*   (7) Make decisions: Based on the sentiment scores and weights of attributes, comprehensively evaluate the various attributes of the decision object to formulate a comprehensive emotional evaluation result, thereby developing corresponding decision strategies, such as product improvement, adjustment of market positioning, etc., to better meet the emotional needs and expectations of users.

Here, we will use the research conducted by [Yang et al. (2023)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r52) on the purchase of personalized new energy vehicles as an example to explain how sentiment analysis can be better applied to MADM. The full framework is presented in [Figure 3](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#f03).

| Figure 3. The framework of personalized new energy vehicle purchase MADM |
| --- |
| ![Figure979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.f03](i/ch009.f03.png) |
| --- |

Step 1: The “Koubei” interface on the “Autohome” platform provides rich evaluations of purchased new energy vehicles by users. Therefore, Yang et al. chose to use the platform's review information to help “Autohome” improve its personalized product recommendation service.

Step 2: Yang et al. used web scraping tools to select 6 electric vehicles (Lingbao T03, Wuling Hongguang MINIEV, BYD Han, BYD Tang, NIO ES6, and XPeng P7) from the “Autohome” platform ([https://www.autohome.com.cn](https://www.autohome.com.cn)) and categorized the evaluations of these electric vehicles into eight attributes: space, power, handling, energy consumption, comfort, appearance, decoration, and value for money. In order to facilitate understanding, we have displayed the owner reviews of these vehicles on the “Autohome” platform, as shown in [Figure 4](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#f04).

Step 3 and Step 4: Yang et al. utilized Gooseeker ([http://www.gooseeker.com/](http://www.gooseeker.com/)) on the Product Recommendation Platform for each new energy vehicle. They conducted sentiment analysis by cleaning data, establishing sentiment dictionaries, obtaining data trends, and transforming the evaluations of each attribute of the new energy vehicles to obtain the quantity of positive, negative, and neutral comments.

Step 5: Based on a formula, they calculated the proportion of the three different sentiment polarities for each product attribute and converted them into corresponding values. Subsequently, they constructed a multi-attribute integrated sentiment matrix.

Step 6: Using the q-rung orthogonal fuzzy weighted partition Mourehd mean operator, they obtained the q-rung orthogonal fuzzy numbers for each situation by aggregating the information matrix. After that, the attribute is divided into three parts according to its actual meaning, and the weight of each attribute is set by the user. They then calculated the product score according to the equation of the score function.

Step 7: They recommended the new energy vehicle that aligns with the personalized preferences of users based on the comparison rules of the score function.

| Figure 4. Vehicle types and reviews on Autohome.com |
| --- |
| ![Figure979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.f04](i/ch009.f04.png) |
| --- |

Specific Methods of MADM With Remote Sensing Data

The application of remote sensing data to MADM typically involves the following specific steps:

*   (1) Data acquisition: The first step is to obtain relevant remote sensing data, which may include satellite imagery, aerial photography images, lidar data, etc. This data can be used to obtain information on the topography, vegetation cover, land use, etc. of the surface.
*   (2) Data preprocessing: The obtained raw remote sensing data may need to be preprocessed, including atmospheric correction, geometric correction, image registration, etc., to ensure the accuracy and availability of the data.
*   (3) Feature extraction: Use remote sensing data to extract relevant geographic information features, such as vegetation coverage, land type, water distribution, etc. These features are usually achieved through image processing and remote sensing techniques.
*   (4) Data integration: Different features extracted from remote sensing data are integrated to form a multi-attribute dataset, which may include vegetation cover, land type, terrain undulation, etc.
*   (5) Attribute weighting determination: For different attributes, you need to determine their importance and weight in the decision. This can be determined by expert evaluation, AHP, fuzzy synthesis evaluation, etc.
*   (6) Establishment of decision-making model: According to the multi-attribute data and its weight, the MADM model is established, and the common models include weighted linear combination model, fuzzy comprehensive evaluation model, TOPSIS model, etc.
*   (7) Decision analysis: The established decision model is used to evaluate and analyze different solutions or options to determine the optimal decision-making plan.
*   (8) Result display: Visualize the results of decision analysis for DMs and stakeholders to understand and reference.

The above steps are the general process of applying remote sensing data to MADM, and may be appropriately adjusted and expanded according to specific problems and data conditions in practical applications.

The specific steps involved in MADM typically include the following (The full framework is presented via [Figure 5](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#f05).):

*   (1) Data acquisition: Relevant remote sensing data needs to be obtained, which may include satellite images, aerial photographs, LiDAR data, etc. These data can be used to obtain information about the terrain, vegetation cover, land use, etc. Data can be obtained from platforms such as USGS Earth Explore, LAADS, Copernicus Open Access Hub, Geospatial Data Cloud, and more.
*   (2) Data preprocessing: The acquired raw remote sensing data may need to undergo preprocessing, including atmospheric correction, geometric correction, image registration, etc., to ensure data accuracy and usability.
*   (3) Feature extraction: Utilize remote sensing data to extract relevant geographic information features, such as vegetation cover, land types, water distribution, etc. These features are typically obtained through image processing and remote sensing techniques.
*   (4) Data integration: Integrate the different features extracted from remote sensing data to form a multi-attribute dataset, which may include attributes such as vegetation cover, land types, terrain variability, etc.
*   (5) Attribute weight determination: For different attributes, it is necessary to determine their importance and weight in the decision-making process. This can be achieved through methods such as expert assessment, AHP, fuzzy comprehensive evaluation, etc.
*   (6) Decision model establishment: Based on the multi-attribute data and their weights, establish a multi-attribute decision model. Common models include weighted linear combination model, fuzzy comprehensive evaluation model, TOPSIS model, etc.
*   (7) Decision analysis: Use the established decision model to evaluate and analyze different alternatives or options, and determine the optimal decision.
*   (8) Results presentation: Visualize the results of the decision analysis for the understanding and reference of DMs and stakeholders. This may include maps or other visual representations of the optimal decision outcome.

| Figure 5. The framework of MADM with remote sensing data |
| --- |
| ![Figure979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.f05](i/ch009.f05.png) |
| --- |

Specific Methods of Open Datasets for MADM

Open datasets can support various types of MADM, whether it is government decision-making, business management, or community development. It is a major method for providing data support for decision-making ([Kirchner et al., 2015](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r21)). There are thousands of data repositories on the Web, providing access to millions of datasets ([Zhang et al., 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r56)). National and regional governments, scientific publishers and consortia, commercial data providers, and others publish data for fields ranging from social science to life science to high-energy physics to climate science and more. Access to this data is critical to facilitating reproducibility of research results, enabling scientists to build on others' work, and providing data journalists easier access to information and its provenance ([Uhlir & Schroder, 2007](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r43)).

The process of obtaining data from open datasets can involve the following steps (The full framework is presented in [Figure 6](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#f06).):

| Figure 6. Framework of obtain data from open datasets |
| --- |
| ![Figure979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.f06](i/ch009.f06.png) |
| --- |

*   (1) Find relevant open datasets: Search for suitable open datasets using internet search engines, data portals (such as data.gov, Kaggle, etc.), or dedicated data resource platforms.
*   (2) Understand the content and format of the dataset: Before choosing a dataset, it is important to understand the types of data, the volume, the data structure, and other relevant information ([Janssen, et al., 2012](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r18)). This helps in determining if the data fit the requirements and deciding how to extract and process the data.
*   (3) Obtain access permission: Some open datasets may require users to register or obtain access authorization in order to download and use the data. In this step, it is important to comply with the rules set by the data provider and adhere to legal and compliance principles.
*   (4) Download or import the dataset: Depending on the data set's availability, you can choose to directly download the data files or import the data through APIs, database connections, etc.
*   (5) Data cleaning and processing: After obtaining the data, it may require data cleaning and processing operations. This includes handling missing values, outliers, and standardizing data formats to ensure data quality and consistency. [Zhang et al. (2023)](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r62) used data from open datasets, and after data cleaning and analysis, they used the obtained dataset to support forest fire related decision-making, providing a good example.

For instance, Kaggle datasets is an open data science competition platform that provides various datasets for data scientists and ML experts. As shown in [Figure 7](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#f07), the open datasets on Kaggle encompass different domains such as finance, healthcare, transportation, etc. Data scientists can utilize these datasets to solve real-world problems, such as predicting stock market trends, diagnosing diseases, optimizing traffic flow, and more. For instance, researchers can use healthcare datasets available on Kaggle to analyze patients' electronic medical records using ML algorithms, identify risk factors for diseases, and provide better decision support in healthcare. Thus, to get started, one can log in to the official Kaggle website, search for relevant keywords to find datasets, and choose the dataset that fits their research methodology and algorithms. As Kaggle datasets are derived from real-world scenarios and have undergone accuracy and reliability verification, researchers can utilize them relatively reliably for analysis and modeling purposes. Nevertheless, Kaggle datasets may have limitations such as incompleteness or biases, thus it is crucial to perform data cleaning and preprocessing after accessing or downloading the dataset. Moreover, the openness of datasets may raise privacy and security concerns, especially when personal data is involved. Therefore, researchers need to comply with relevant regulations and ethical guidelines while using Kaggle datasets and ensure data security and privacy protection.

| Figure 7. Data on healthcare datasets on Kaggle datasets |
| --- |
| ![Figure979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.f07](i/ch009.f07.png) |
| --- |

Specific Methods of Questionnaire Survey for MADM

Questionnaire survey is a popular data collection method for academic or marketing research in a variety of fields. Face-to-face, telephone interviews and postal surveys are traditional approaches of completing questionnaire surveys. However, with the growing access to the internet facility globally ([Evans & Mathur, 2005](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r12)), for instance, internet penetration in Nepal, a low-income country, increased exponentially in the past two decades, from less than 50 users in 1995 to 11.9 million users (about 45% of total population) in 2015 ([Wright, 2005](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r49)) and the price of technology devices (e.g. tablet computers, hardware) and software continuing to reduce ([Van Selm & Jankowski, 2006](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r45)), a novel internet-based data collection technique such as online questionnaire survey has become popular in recent years ([Wettergren, et al., 2016](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r48)). Recently, qualitative data collection through online focus groups is also emerging (Stewart et al., 2016), suggesting, research participants in the digital age can now interact with each other and the interviewer or facilitator in an online- multimedia setting.

Therefore, in order to obtain effective questionnaire data, there are six methodological components that are crucial for successful online surveys when developing and operating online surveys ([Lietz, 2010](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r23)). These are: user-friendly design and layout; selecting survey participants; avoiding multiple responses; data management; ethical issues. Once a survey questionnaire is designed and completed, it is necessary to extract data from it. It is important to understand the nature, structure, and characteristics of the data obtained from a survey questionnaire before analyzing it using available software modules. For example, the proportion of waste in the proportion is usually the most commonly included content in survey reports. In addition to proportions, contingency tables are also one of the main presentation forms and analysis bases for survey data. Therefore, to obtain data from a survey questionnaire ([Lietz, 2010](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r23)), you can follow the steps below (The full framework is presented in [Figure 8](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#f08).):

| Figure 8. The framework of using data on questionnaire for MADM |
| --- |
| ![Figure979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.f08](i/ch009.f08.png) |
| --- |

*   (1) It is necessary to design a targeted and operational questionnaire, including open-ended and closed-ended questions to obtain comprehensive and specific information, it must be User-friendly design and layout.
*   (2) Appropriate survey participants need to be selected to ensure that they can genuinely reflect the required data.
*   (3) Surveys can be conducted through face-to-face, telephone, online, or other methods to ensure the comprehensiveness and accuracy of the data.
*   (4) The collected data needs to be organized and analyzed to draw conclusions and make recommendations, which includes analyze proportion, set contingency tables and examine ethical issues.

Through these methods, data can be effectively obtained from survey questionnaires, providing strong support for subsequent decision-making, as shown in [Figure 9](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#f09).

| Figure 9. Obtain data from a questionnaire. |

| --- |

QUESTIONS AND EXPECTATIONS

Questions and Expectations of Obtaining Data From Classic Papers

Obtaining data from classic papers is a traditional method of data acquisition. However, with the development of artificial intelligence, metaverse, and ML, this method also faces several challenges. Firstly, the demand for large-scale and diverse data is increasing with the advancement of AI and ML technologies. Traditional classic papers may not provide sufficiently diverse and large-scale data, necessitating the exploration of alternative data sources to meet the needs of algorithms and models. Secondly, data from classic papers may have issues related to quality and accuracy, particularly when it comes to data from virtual worlds such as the metaverse. Future efforts need to address how to ensure the quality and accuracy of data to enhance the precision and robustness of AI and ML models. Additionally, as the demand for large-scale data increases, issues related to data privacy and security become more prominent. Safeguarding user privacy and data security during the data acquisition process presents a significant challenge. Lastly, as AI technology advances, there may be a need for more automated and intelligent data acquisition methods to meet the demand for large-scale data. This will require the integration of AI technology to achieve automated data retrieval, cleaning, and integration.

In summary, the method of obtaining data from classic papers faces numerous challenges when applied in the fields of artificial intelligence, metaverse, and ML. Therefore, there is a need to continuously improve data acquisition methods and technologies to meet the demand for large-scale, diverse, and high-quality data. This will contribute to the advancement of the field of MADM and propel the development of artificial intelligence and ML.

Questions and Expectations of Sentiment Analysis

In the future, sentiment analysis will face a series of challenges, particularly with the continuous development of artificial intelligence, the metaverse, and ML. Initially, sentiment analysis needs to continuously enhance its understanding of diverse contexts and user evaluations. With the advancement of AI technology, users express emotions in increasingly diverse ways in different environments, placing higher demands on sentiment analysis to more accurately capture user emotional needs and evaluations. Secondly, with the rise of the metaverse, sentiment analysis needs to adapt to the emotional experiences and expressions of people in virtual environments. In the metaverse, people may express emotions in different ways, such as through virtual characters and environments. Sentiment analysis needs to understand and interpret these new forms of emotional expression to better support user interaction and experiences in the metaverse. Additionally, the development of ML technology presents both challenges and opportunities for sentiment analysis. With the continuous progress of deep learning and natural language processing technologies, sentiment analysis can better learn and understand emotions from massive data. However, it also needs to address issues such as data bias and model generalization to improve the accuracy and robustness of sentiment analysis.

In conclusion, when facing the challenges of artificial intelligence, the metaverse, and ML, sentiment analysis needs to continuously improve algorithms and models to enhance its understanding of diverse contexts and user evaluations. It needs to adapt to the emotional experiences and expressions of people in the metaverse, while leveraging the advantages of ML technology to more accurately capture user emotional needs and evaluations. This will provide more reliable support for MADM, thereby driving the development of artificial intelligence and metaverse technologies to create more intelligent and personalized experiences and services for people.

Questions and Expectations of Remote Sensing Data

Remote sensing data exhibit certain limitations in MADM, including complex data processing and analysis, which requires professional technology and equipment; Data quality and accuracy are affected by a variety of factors, and errors may occur; At the same time, the use of remote sensing data may involve personal privacy and security issues, and relevant information needs to be handled and protected carefully. Therefore, when using remote sensing data for MADM, it is necessary to carefully evaluate the reliability and accuracy of the data, and strengthen data protection and privacy protection measures to ensure the scientificity and security of decision-making.

In the future, with the development of artificial intelligence and metaverse technology, the application of remote sensing data in MADM will become more intelligent and comprehensive. Artificial intelligence can help process and analyze large amounts of remote sensing data, providing more accurate and rapid information extraction and decision support. Metaverse technology can integrate remote sensing data with virtual reality, augmented reality, and other technologies to achieve a comprehensive simulation and visualization of various Earth attributes, providing DMs with more intuitive and immersive decision support. This will propel human society towards a more intelligent and sustainable direction.

Questions and Expectations of Open Datasets

Obtaining data through open datasets can support MADM, helping DMs gain a more comprehensive and accurate understanding of the decision context, providing scientific evidence, enabling prediction and planning, promoting information sharing, and innovation. This brings more transparency, accuracy, and credibility to the decision-making process.

However, obtaining data from open datasets faces numerous challenges. Attention needs to be given to data quality, privacy and security issues, as well as the adaptability and specificity of the data. Researchers should conduct a comprehensive evaluation when selecting and using datasets and combine other methods and data sources to validate and support research findings. In the field of artificial intelligence, training algorithms and models require a large amount of high-quality data, and the uncertainty and inconsistency of data quality may lead to model instability and inaccurate predictions (Antony & Salian, 2021). In the metaverse field, the construction of virtual environments similarly requires real and accurate data to ensure the realism and authenticity of the virtual world.

Secondly, privacy and security issues related to data cannot be overlooked. With the continuous collection and sharing of data, the protection of personal privacy data becomes particularly important. In the field of artificial intelligence, it is necessary to ensure the reasonable use and protection of sensitive data to prevent data leaks and misuse. In the metaverse field, users' personal information and virtual identities need to be fully protected to prevent privacy infringements and security vulnerabilities in virtual environments.

Additionally, data diversity and interoperability are also challenges. The effective integration and utilization of data from different sources and formats pose a complex problem. In the field of artificial intelligence, interoperability and consistency between different data sources need to be addressed to ensure the effective use and analysis of data. In the metaverse field, the establishment of unified standards and protocols is needed for data exchange and interconnection between different virtual environments to achieve interconnectivity in the virtual world.

Therefore, to overcome these challenges, it is necessary to strengthen data governance and management, while promoting data standardization and sharing. Furthermore, there is a need to enhance technological research and innovation to address the constantly emerging data challenges and promote the development of artificial intelligence and metaverse technologies.

In the future, we can expect open datasets to become more comprehensive, covering a wider range of topics, and being continuously updated. This will enable DMs to access real-time and up-to-date information to make informed decisions ([Kirchner et al., 2015](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r21)). Additionally, advancements in technology, such as artificial intelligence and ML, will further enhance the analysis and utilization of open datasets by automating data processing, extracting valuable insights, and generating predictive models. Furthermore, with increased collaboration and partnerships between different stakeholders, the quality control processes for open datasets will become more robust. This will ensure the reliability and accuracy of the data, building further trust among users. Data standards and interoperability will also be improved, allowing for easier integration and comparison of data from different sources.

Overall, the future of obtaining data from open datasets holds great potential for empowering DMs with valuable information, driving innovation, and improving transparency. With advancements in technology and increased collaboration, open datasets will continue to evolve and play a vital role in shaping-making landscape.

Questions and Expectations of Questionnaire Survey

Obtaining data from surveys faces challenges in the fields of artificial intelligence and metaverse. Firstly, survey data are often static and fragmented, and cannot fully reflect users' real behaviors and emotions in virtual environments. This requires comprehensive analysis by combining other data sources, such as behavioral data in virtual reality environments and social media data, to better understand user behaviors and needs. Secondly, due to the rapid development of AI and metaverse technologies, the topics and areas involved in surveys are also constantly expanding and deepening. This requires survey designers and data analysts to have broader professional knowledge and skills to better respond to complex and changing survey needs ([Fenton, et al., 2001](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r13)). Finally, with the increasing awareness of personal data privacy protection, survey data also need stricter protection and compliance management to ensure the safety and privacy of user data. Therefore, surveying in data acquisition for AI and metaverse fields needs continuous innovation and improvement to meet increasingly complex challenges.

To address these issues, several measures can be taken. Firstly, more advanced statistical methods (such as factor analysis or regression analysis) can be used to analyze the data, in order to identify patterns and relationships in the responses. Secondly, improving questionnaire design to ensure clear and unambiguous questions can enhance respondent understanding and accuracy of responses ([Ikart, 2019](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch009.xhtml#r16)). Additionally, the adoption of new technologies such as online surveys can facilitate more efficient data collection and easier implementation of randomized experiments and other advanced survey designs. Furthermore, with the increasing awareness of data privacy and security, strengthening measures to protect data privacy and security, including the application of encryption technology and other security measures to prevent the disclosure of sensitive information, is a future development trend.

In conclusion, although there may be some challenges in obtaining data from questionnaires, continuous improvement of methods and technologies can ensure that questionnaire surveys remain an important tool for researchers and practitioners to obtain valuable information.

CONCLUSION

Data collection methods in MADM are one of the important research directions in this field. This chapter systematically discusses the development process of the five data collection methods, and introduces their application in MADM in detail, including example description, data processing, and model building. We put forward the challenges of these methods, such as how to ensure data quality and how to improve data processing efficiency, and discussed the development trend of future research. The research results of this chapter provide a basic framework and reference for future research, and also provide valuable guidance for DMs and practitioners. In future research, we need to further explore how to ensure the accuracy and reliability of data in the data collection process, and how to combine different data collection methods to achieve more comprehensive and accurate MADM.

# Chapter 10

*   Shaoxia Zhang  
    Shanxi University of Finance and Economics, China

ABSTRACT

Knowledge representation and reasoning based on decision implication is to perform an extremely compact representation of the decision information implied in data, and obtain all decision information in data by means of reasoning, based on the current representation. The existing research in logic and data aspects of decision implication proposes a set of decision implications with information integrity and extreme simplicity (non-redundancy and optimality), i.e., decision implication canonical basis (DICB), which lays a solid foundation for constructing a knowledge representation and reasoning framework in formal contexts. This chapter conducts a systematic and in-depth study on the important issues of knowledge representation capability, and incomplete formal context adaptability of decision implication, and reasoning based on decision implications.

INTRODUCTION

A Brief Review of Formal Concept Analysis

Formal Concept Analysis (FCA) is an order-theoretic method for concept analysis and visualization, pioneered by Wille ([Wille, 1982](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r15)) in the mid-80s. A concept is a pair constituted by its extent and intent. The extent of a concept is a collection of all objects belonging to that concept and its intent is the set of all attributes common to all objects of the extent. FCA establishes the relationships between intents and extents and visualizes the generalization and specialization of concepts by means of concept lattices. Because of its strengths, FCA has been become a powerful tool for data mining (Mouakher and Yahia, 2019; [Bartl and Konecny, 2019](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r2)), social networks (Roth et al., 2008), software engineering (Tilley et al., 2005), cognition-based concept learning (Kuznetsov and Makhalova, 2018) and knowledge reduction (Konecny and Krajca, 2019; [Cornejo et al., 2018](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r7)).

In the study of knowledge representation and reasoning in FCA, (attribute) implication ([Ganter and Wille, 1999](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r11); Qu and Zhai, 2008; [Zhai et al., 2012](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r19); Ma et al., 2011; [Zhai and Li, 2019](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r18)) is proposed in the form of A→B, meaning that if all the attributes in A are satisfied, then all the attributes in B are satisfied. Duquenne etc. (V and J-L, 1986) constructed the so-called canonical basis, which turns out to be complete and non-redundant w.r.t. implication logic ([Ganter and Wille, 1999](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r11); Stumme, 1996), and minimal among all complete sets of implications. Starting from a canonical basis of a formal context, one can obtain all implications in this context by applying Armstrong rules ([Armstrong, 1974](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r1)).

A Brief Review of Decision Implication

Decision implication, revealing the dependency between conditions and decisions, is a knowledge representation and reasoning theory that has been developed within the case of decision-making ([Zhai et al., 2014](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r20), [2015b](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r22); Li et al., 2017; [Zhang et al., 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r23); [Zhai et al., 2015a](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r21)). Compared with other decision knowledge representations, such as concept rule ([Wu et al., 2009](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r16); Li et al., 2013a) and granule rule ([Wu et al., 2009](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r16)), decision implication has been demonstrated to possess a stronger representation ability ([Zhang et al., 2020](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r24)).

Zhai et al. ([Zhai et al., 2014](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r20), [2015b](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r22)) conducted decision implication logic, encompassing both semantic and syntactic aspects. In the semantic aspect, the research considers the soundness, completeness, and non-redundancy of decision implications, as well as decision implication basis (a most compact set of decision implications) ([Zhai et al., 2015b](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r22); Nanjia et al., 2019). In a given dataset, the soundness of decision implications means that the decision implications are valid within the data, the completeness of a decision implication set implies that all sound decision implications in data can be derived from this set, and the non-redundancy of a decision implication set means that no decision implication within the set can be deduced from other decision implications in the set. In the syntactic aspect, two inference rules, namely Augmentation and Combination, are proposed, which have been demonstrated to be sound and complete w.r.t. the semantic aspect. The logic research results of decision implication can be applied to data (i.e., decision context) research (Li et al., 2017; [Zhang et al., 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r23); [Zhai et al., 2015a](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r21)). Methods for generating decision implications basis in decision contexts can be found in (Li et al., 2017; [Zhang et al., 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r23)). Additionally, the inference strategies based on decision implications basis and inference rules are explored in ([Zhai et al., 2022](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r17), [2015a](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r21); Nanjia et al., 2019).

A Brief Review of Concept Rule and Granular Rule

Granular computing is a collective term referring to theories, methodologies, techniques, and tools for the analysis of information granules encountered in problem solving ([Wu et al., 2009](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r16); Li and Wu, 2017). Wu et al. ([Wu et al., 2009](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r16)) brought a perspective of granular computing into FCA based on the fact that concept lattice extracted from data is capable of representing all the information implied by data ([Ganter and Wille, 1999](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r11)). Thus, it is expected that decision implications extracted from concept lattices are also capable of representing all the information implied by decision implications. Wu et al. ([Wu et al., 2009](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r16)) considered such special decision implications, called concept rules, by requiring that the premises and consequences of decision implications must be intents from condition and decision concepts respectively1 . The relationships between concept rules and decision implications, however, remain untouched in ([Wu et al., 2009](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r16)).

Another important result of FCA is that any concept can be represented by the join of some object concepts ([Wille, 1982](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r15)). Inspired by the result, Wu et al. ([Wu et al., 2009](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r16)) considered the special concept rules between object concepts, called granular rules, and expected that all concept rules can be represented by granular rules. Li et al. (Li et al., 2013a) achieved the goal by defining the non-redundancy on concept rules and proving that the non-redundant concept rules can be represented by merging the corresponding granular rules. In addition, Li et al. (Li et al., 2011) proposed a method for deriving all non-redundant concept rules from a decision context and optimized this method with less time complexity in (Li et al., 2013a). Li et al. (Li et al., 2013a) also performed a comparison between the concept rule oriented knowledge reduction (Li et al., 2011) and the granular rule oriented knowledge reduction ([Wu et al., 2009](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r16)).

A Brief Review of Knowledge Acquisition Within Incomplete Formal Contexts

Incomplete data is a common occurrence in real-world scenarios. In FCA, incomplete formal context is employed to formally describe the situations where a complete description of individuals is unavailable. Burmeister and Holzer, among others, have pioneered the development of attribute exploration within incomplete formal contexts (P and R, 2000; [Holzer, 2001](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r12); [Carpineto and Romano, 2004a](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r4),b; [Ganter, 1999](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r9)). Additionally, ([Holzer, 2004a](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r13),b) proposed an algorithm for attribute exploration. Furthermore, attribute exploration delves into the ideas of interactive collaborative exploration, as extensively researched in ([Ganter and Wille, 1999](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r11)).

Despite the lack of information in information system, from a knowledge acquisition perspective, there is an expectation to represent the knowledge initially implied in incomplete information and discover automatic inference rules that require no prior knowledge ([Wasserkrug et al., 2008](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r14)). In fact, substantial work has already been undertaken in modeling incomplete knowledge within the domain of incomplete formal context(S, 2002; P and R, 2000). Differing from formal contexts where knowledge can be precisely described, implications in incomplete formal contexts are semantically assessed by evaluating their validity within completions, which represent the distinct formal contexts an incomplete formal context could be (P and R, 2000). This means that an implication is considered to be necessary when it holds in all completions of the incomplete formal context, while an acceptable implication is said to hold in at least one completion. Both acceptable implications and necessary implications are available for practical applications and can be effectively employed in decision-making and problem-solving in real-world scenarios. Sergei Obiedkov (S, 2002) proposed a deduction mechanism that allowed for the deduction of all necessary/acceptable implications from a basis.

Motivations and Contributions of The Chapter

Comparatively, decision implication is more general than concept rule and granular rule, and hence has a wider application ([Zhai et al., 2015b](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r22), [a](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r21), [2014](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r20)). Concept rule and granular rule, which reveal the dependencies between intents ([Wu et al., 2009](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r16)), are more special than decision implication, and are usually useful and easily obtainable when only concepts or object concepts are provided. This is the case for such practical applications as ontology, where knowledge is generally described by concepts, so considering the decision implications between concepts, i.e., concept rules or granular rules, may be more preferable. A decision implication, however, might not be a concept rule or a granular rule, since its premise or consequence might not be an intent or a granular intent. All the results are basic but insufficient to clarify the essential issue that whether concept rules and granular rules have the power of representing all the information in decision implications, because according to decision implication logic, a subset of decision implications may keep all the decision information of decision implications and thus may be complete w.r.t. a closed set of decision implications ([Zhai et al., 2014](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r20)).

From the perspective of incomplete formal contexts, attribute exploration relies on the prior knowledge of experts, and the correctness and completeness of the knowledge mastered by experts significantly affect the quality of knowledge reasoning. In this case, this paper intends to research the automatic inference rules to completely deduce knowledge applied in knowledge bases without prior knowledge. In the existing deduction mechanism, the basis of necessary implications and that of acceptable implications are inconvenient as it recursively involves the bases of all its completions, resulting in exponential time complexity in generation and inference processes. A concise knowledge basis presentation is needed to enable the streamlined generation of the basis and facilitate the reasoning process. Another critical issue we revealed in our paper is that while necessary and acceptable implications in incomplete formal contexts can be represented by the implications within (complete) formal contexts, the inference rules originally apply to formal contexts may yield invalid implications when they are applied to these two types of implications. In this case, a new form of knowledge representation and inference rules needs to be reformulated for the two types of implications.

Actually, although an incomplete formal context can potentially extend to a vast number of formal contexts, it can be effectively modeled by employing two completions: one for attributes that an object certainly possesses and another for attributes that an object may possibly possess ([Ganter, 1999](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r9)). In this sense, an intriguing concept arises wherein the modeling of an incomplete formal context is based on the two completions. Consequently, knowledge representation and reasoning within an incomplete formal context are transformed into those within two formal contexts. This transformation offers more general and efficient methods for knowledge acquisition. Furthermore, another issue cannot be ignored is that, even with small-scale data, there may exist extensive sets of implications, making them impractical for direct applications. Therefore, it becomes imperative to seek a compact representation for implications. These sets are expected to significantly reduce the number of implications comparing to the closure sets, while still retaining all the information implied in the closure sets.

This chapter conducts a comparative study of decision implication, concept rule and granular rule, and that of decision implication-based knowledge re-representation and reasoning within incomplete formal contexts. The originality and contributions are as follows:

*   (1) It is exemplified that both concept rules and granular rules are not complete w.r.t. decision implications, and that granular rules are not complete w.r.t. concept rules. This implies that there exists information loss when studying decision implications by using only concept rules or granular rules, or when studying concept rules by using only granular rules. With the help of decision implication logic, we uncover the information loss by identifying the decision implications that cannot be deduced from concept rules or granular rules, and the concept rules that cannot be deduced from granular rules.
*   (2) We advance the modeling of incomplete formal contexts by introducing specially constructed decision contexts that connect the minimal and maximal completions. This approach enables us to re-represent both necessary and acceptable implication based on decision implication within the specialized decision contexts.
*   (3) We extend the applicability of inference rules, specifically Augmentation and Combination, initially designed for decision implications, to necessary/acceptable implications. Furthermore, we introduce Reflexivity rule to jointly define the completeness and non-redundancy for sets of necessary and acceptable implications, working in conjunction with Augmentation and Combination to effectively eliminate redundancy within these sets. Necessary implication basis and an acceptable implication basis are established, with preserving all the information implied in necessity/acceptable implications while minimizing the number of implications.

The Arrangement of This Chapter

The remainder of this chapter is organized as follows. The basic notions and properties in FCA are reviewed in Section 2. Section 3 conducts a comparative study of decision implication, concept rule and granular rule. Section 4 conducts a comparative study of decision implication-based knowledge re- representation and reasoning within incomplete formal contexts. Conclusions and future work end the chapter in Section 5.

BASIC NOTIONS AND PROPERTIES IN FCA

This section gives a brief overview over some basic notions and properties in FCA.

Formal Context, Implication, And Concept Lattice

Definition 2.1 ([Wille, 1982](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r15)). A formal context is a triple K\=(G,M,I), where G and M are the object set and the attribute set respectively, and I⊆G×M is a set of incidence relations between objects and attributes. For g∈G and m∈M, (g,m)∈I expresses “the object g has the attribute m”.

A formal context is generally represented by a two-dimensional table, in which row headers are object names, column headers are attribute names and a “1” indicates the row object has the column attribute.

Example 2.1 A formal context K\=(G,M,I) is given in [Table 1](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#t1), where G\= {g[1],g[2],g[3],g[4],g[5],g[6]} and M\= {a[1],a[2],a[3],a[4],a[5]}.

Table 1. A formal context

|  || a[1] || a[2] || a[3] || a[4] || a[5] |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| g[1] || 1 || 1 || 0 || 0 || 1 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| g[2] || 0 || 0 || 1 || 0 || 0 |
| g[3] || 1 || 0 || 1 || 0 || 0 |
| g[4] || 0 || 1 || 0 || 0 || 1 |
| g[5] || 0 || 1 || 1 || 1 || 1 |
| g[6] || 0 || 0 || 1 || 0 || 1 |

Definition 2.3 ([Wille, 1982](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r15)). Let K\=(G,M,I) be a formal context. For A⊆G and B⊆M, C\=(A,B) is a formal concept of K if A^I\=B and B^I\=A. In this case, A is the extent of C and B is the intent of C.

Definition 2.4 ([Wille, 1982](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r15)). Let K\=(G,M,I) be a formal context and C[1]\= (A[1],B[1]), C[2]\= (A[2],B[2]) be two concepts of K. We define:

Then, all the formal concepts of K ordered in this way constitute a complete lattice, called the concept lattice of K and denoted by L(K).

Implication reveals the dependency relationship between attributes. An implication is an expression in the form of A→B, meaning that if all attributes in attribute set A are satisfied, then all attributes in attribute set B are also satisfied. Formal concept analysis can obtain implications from the formal context.

Definition 2.5 ([Wille, 1982](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r15)). Let K\=(G,M,I) be a formal context. For A⊆G and B⊆M, if A^I⊆B^I, then A→B is called an implication of K.
