Decision Implication Logic

Decision implication is an extension of implication in the case of decision, which studies the dependencies between attributes of different domains (such as causes and effects, conditions and behaviors). Similar to the study of implication ([Ganter and Wille, 1999](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r11)), decision implication can be studied from two perspectives, namely the logical way and the data-driven way ([Zhai et al., 2015b](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r22), [2014](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r20), [2015a](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r21)). By the logical way, we mean decision implication logic, which has the semantical and syntactical aspects. By the data-driven way, one extracts decision implications from a given decision context and discusses the completeness and non-redundancy of the decision implications w.r.t. the decision context. It has been shown in ([Zhai et al., 2014](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r20)) that the results obtained by the logical way can be directly applied to the data-driven way.

The definition of decision implication in decision implication logic is firstly given in Definition 2.6.

Definition 2.6 ([Zhai et al., 2014](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r20)). Let C be a set of condition attributes and D be a set of decision attributes such that ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m13](i/ch010.m13.png). If A⊆C and B⊆D, then A→B is called a decision implication, where A is the premise of A→B and B is the consequence.

In the semantic aspect of decision implication logic, the soundness of decision implications and the completeness and non-redundancy of decision implication sets are studied.

Definition 2.7 ([Zhai et al., 2014](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r20)). Let C be a set of condition attributes, D be a set of decision attributes, and L and L[1] be sets of decision implications. We define:

*   1\. For a set T⊆C∪D and a decision implication A→B, if ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m14](i/ch010.m14.png) or B⊆T∩D, then T respects A→B (or T is a model of A→B), denoted by T|= A→B. If for any A[1]→B[1]∈L, T|= A[1]→B[1] holds, then T respects L, denoted by T|=L.
*   2\. For a decision implication A→B, if for any T⊆C∪D, T|=L implies T|= A→B, then A→B can be semantically deduced from L, denoted by L⊢ A→B. If for any A[1]→B[1]∈L[1], L⊢A[1]→B[1] holds, then L[1] can be semantically deduced from L, denoted by L⊢L[1].
*   3\. If for any ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m15](i/ch010.m15.png) holds, then L is non-redundant.
*   4\. If any A→B that can be semantically deduced from L is contained in L, then L is closed.
*   5\. If L is closed, L[1]⊆L and L[1]⊢L, then L[1] is complete w.r.t. L.

Given a data set, the soundness of a decision implication means that the decision implication holds in the data. The completeness of a decision implication set is that all sound decision implications in data can be derived from this set. The non-redundancy of a decision implication set means that any decision implication in the set cannot be derived from other decision implication in the set.

From Definition 2.7, we can see that T|= A→B means that A→B holds in T, i.e., if A⊆T, then B⊆T, and that T|=L means that L holds in T, i.e., all the decision implications in L hold in T. Thus, L⊢ A→B means that if L holds, then A→B also holds, i.e., A→B is a decision information implied by L. In this sense, L is non-redundant if and only if any decision implication in L is indispensable, i.e., any decision implication is not implied by other decision implications in L. Furthermore, from Definition 2.7, it is easy to verify that, a decision implication A→B is redundant w.r.t. L if and only if A→B can be semantically deduced from other decision implications in L, i.e., L\\{ A→B }⊢ A→B.

The closure of a condition attribute set A w.r.t. a set of decision implications L is given in Definition 2.8, which is actually the maximal consequence deduced from L based on A, as verified in Proposition 2.3 .

Definition 2.8 ([Zhai et al., 2014](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r20)). Let C be a set of condition attributes, and L be a set of decision implications. A closure of A⊆C w.r.t. L is defined as

A^L = ∪{B[i]| A[i]→B[i]∈L, A[i]⊆A}

Proposition 2.3 ([Zhai et al., 2014](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r20)). Let L be a set of decision implications, and A→B be a decision implication. Then we have that L⊢ A→B if and only if B⊆A^L.

In the syntactical aspect, one studies the soundness, completeness, and redundancy of inference rules ([Zhai et al., 2014](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r20)). Zhai et al. ([Zhai et al., 2014](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r20)) proposed two inference rules, Augmentation and Combination, by which, one can obtain decision implications from the given decision implications:

*   Augmentation
    
    ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m16](i/ch010.m16.png) (1)
    
*   Combination
    
    ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m17](i/ch010.m17.png) (2)
    

Augmentation means that if T is a model of A→B, A[1]⊇A and B[1]⊆B, then T is also a model of A[1]→B[1]. Similarly, Combination means that if T is a model of A→B and A[1]→B[1], then T is also a model of A∪ A[1]→B∪B[1].

The following theorems show the soundness, completeness and non-redundancy of Augmentation and Combination.

Theorem 2.1 ([Zhai et al., 2014](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r20)). (Soundness)Augmentation and Combination are sound, i.e.,

*   1\. If A→B A[1]⊇A and B[1]⊆B, then A→B ⊢ A[1]→B[1].
*   2\. If A→B and A[1]→B[1], then ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m18](i/ch010.m18.png).

The soundness of the inference rules ensures the semantical compatibility of syntactical deduction, i.e., if a decision implication A→B can be obtained from L by iteratively applying the inference rules, then A→B can be semantically deduced from L.

Theorem 2.2 ([Zhai et al., 2014](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r20)). (Completeness)Augmentation and Combination are complete w.r.t. the semantical aspect, i.e., for any closed set L and complete set L[1]⊆L, we have A→B ∈L if and only if A→B can be obtained from L[1] by applying Augmentation and Combination.

The completeness of the inference rules ensures the completeness of syntactical deduction, i.e., if a decision implication A→B can be semantically deduced from L, then A→B can be obtained from L by iteratively applying the inference rules.

Theorem 2.3 ([Zhai et al., 2014](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r20)). (Non-redundancy)Augmentation and Combination are non-redundant, i.e., they cannot be replaced by each other in deduction process.

Decision Implications Within Decision Contexts

The section studies decision implication in the data-driven way, where the data is represented in the form of decision context.

Definition 2.9 ([Zhai et al., 2014](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r20)). A decision context is a triple ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m19](i/ch010.m19.png), where G is the object set, C is the condition attribute set, and D is the decision attribute set such that ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m20](i/ch010.m20.png). In this case, I[C]⊆G×C is the set of condition incidence relations and I[D]⊆G×D is the set of decision incidence relations. For g∈G and m∈C∪D, (g,m)∈I[C] or (g,m)∈I[D] denotes “the object g has the attribute m “.

A decision context can also be represented by a two-dimensional table, in which row headers are object names, column headers are attribute names, and a ” 1 ” indicates the row object has the column attribute.

Decision Implication Canonical Basis

Zhai et al. ([Zhai et al., 2015a](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r21)) proposed a most compact set of decision implications, i.e., decision implication canonical basis.

Definition 2.12 (Li et al., 2017; [Zhang et al., 2021](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r23); [Zhai et al., 2015a](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r21)). Let ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m33](i/ch010.m33.png) be a decision context. A set A⊆C is called a decision premise of K, if A is proper, i.e.,

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m34](i/ch010.m34.png)

The set

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m35](i/ch010.m35.png)

is called the decision implication canonical basis (DICB) of K.

A decision implication in DICB takes decision premise as its premises and the closure of the decision premise w.r.t. the decision context as its consequence. DICB is proven to be complete, non-redundant, and optimal, i.e., it is of minimal cardinality among all complete sets of decision implications, as shown in Theorem 2.4.

Theorem 2.4 ([Zhai et al., 2015a](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r21)). Let ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m36](i/ch010.m36.png) be a decision context. Then, ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m37](i/ch010.m37.png) is complete w.r.t. ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m38](i/ch010.m38.png), non-redundant and optimal.

Example 2.5 (Continuing Examples 2.3 and 2.4). The DICB of [Table 2](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#t2) is shown in [Table 3](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#t3).

Table 3. The DICB of [Table 2](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#t2)

| {a[1]} → {d[3]} || {a[4],a[5]} → {d[1],d[2]} |
| --- | --- |
| {a[2]} → {d[1]} || {a[3],a[4]} → {d[2]} |
| {a[1],a[4]} → {d[1],d[2],d[3]} || {a[3],a[5]} → {d[2],d[3]} |
| {a[2],a[3]} → {d[1],d[2],d[3]} ||  |

All decision implications in [Table 2](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#t2) can be deduced from that in [Table 3](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#t3), by applying Augmentation and Combination. Take the decision implication {a[2],a[4]}→{d[1]} for an example. It is derived from {a[2]} → {d[1]} in [Table 3](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#t3) by applying Augmentation.

Furthermore, it can be verified that the DICB in [Table 3](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#t3) is non-redundant, meaning that any decision implication in [Table 3](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#t3) cannot be derived from other ones in the DICB. Take {a[3],a[5]} → {d[2],d[3]} as an example. The derivation of {a[3],a[5]} → {d[2],d[3]} only requires the decision implications that contain the consequences {d[2]} and {d[3]}, namely {a[1],a[4]} → {d[1],d[2],d[3]} and {a[2],a[3]} → {d[1],d[2],d[3]} in [Table 3](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#t3). However, since Augmentation and Combination always expand (or maintain) the consequence of a given decision implication, when they are applied to {a[1],a[4]} → {d[1],d[2],d[3]} and {a[2],a[3]} → {d[1],d[2],d[3]}, they cannot produce a decision implication with the premise {a[3],a[5]}, and therefore, {a[3],a[5]} → {d[2],d[3]} cannot be obtained.

A COMPARATIVE STUDY OF DECISION IMPLICATION, CONCEPT RULE AND GRANULAR RULE

Concept Rule and Granular Rule

Decision implication, concept rule and granular rule are considered and extracted from consistent decision contexts ([Wu et al., 2009](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r16); Li et al., 2013; [Zhai et al., 2014](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r20)).

Definition 3.1 ([Wu et al., 2009](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r16)). Let ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m39](i/ch010.m39.png) be a decision context. K is consistent if for any ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m40](i/ch010.m40.png) holds.

Example 3.1 (Continuing Example 2.3). Consider the decision context K in Example 2.3. Since

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m41](i/ch010.m41.png)

by Definition 3.1, we know K is consistent.

In Section 3, we always assume that the decision contexts we are handling are consistent.

By using the operators in Definition 2.10, we can obtain the concept lattices of the condition and decision parts of K, denoted by L(G,C,I[C]) and L(G,D,I[D]), respectively. Wu et al. ([Wu et al., 2009](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r16)) proposed concept rules by requiring the premises and consequences of decision implications to be intents of L(G,C,I[C]) and L(G,D,I[D]), respectively, and expected that concept rules are capable of representing all the information implied by decision implications.

Definition 3.2 ([Wu et al., 2009](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r16)). Let ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m42](i/ch010.m42.png) be a decision context, (A^C,A)∈L(G,C,I[C]) and (B^D,B)∈L(G,D,I[D]^), where ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m43](i/ch010.m43.png). If ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m44](i/ch010.m44.png), then A→B is called a concept rule of K, where A is the premise of A→B and B is the consequence.

Since concept rules reveal the dependencies between intents, it is easy to see that concept rules are special decision implications, and are more easily obtainable than decision implications when providing concept lattices. We denote by ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m45](i/ch010.m45.png) the set of all concept rules of K.

Proposition 3.1 states that if A is an intent, then A→A^(CD) is a concept rule.

Proposition 3.1 Let ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m46](i/ch010.m46.png) be a decision context. Then, A→A^(CD) is a concept rule of K if and only if A is an intent.

Proof. It is straightforward.□

In addition, Wu et al. ([Wu et al., 2009](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r16)) also considered granular rules by requiring the premises and consequences of decision implications to be granular intents.

Definition 3.3 ([Ganter and Wille, 1999](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r11))([Wu et al., 2009](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r16)). Let ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m47](i/ch010.m47.png) be a decision context. Both condition attribute granule and decision attribute granule are called granular intents.

*   2\. If (A,B)∈L(G,D,I[D]), then ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m50](i/ch010.m50.png).

From Definition 3.4, it is easy to see that granular rules are special decision implications and special concept rules. We denote by ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m54](i/ch010.m54.png) the set of all granular rules of K.

Proposition 3.3 Let ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m55](i/ch010.m55.png) be a decision context. Then we have:

*   1\. For any g∈G, g^C→g^D is a granular rule.
*   2\. A→A^(CD) is a granular rule of K if and only if A is a condition attribute granule.

(2) ” ⇒ “. By Definition 3.4, it is straightforward.

“ ⇐ “. Because A is a condition attribute granule, then there exists g∈G such that A=g^C. Next, we will prove A^(CD)\=g^D, i.e., g^(CCD)\=g^D since A=g^C. Because ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m57](i/ch010.m57.png), we have ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m58](i/ch010.m58.png), i.e., ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m59](i/ch010.m59.png). Since concept lattices can retain all information in the data ([Wille, 1982](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r15)), the rules obtained from concept lattices, known as concept rules, are also expected to capture all the information in decision entailments ([Wu et al., 2009](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r16)). Furthermore, since all concepts can be represented by the join of some object concepts (see Proposition 3.2), it is also expected that granular rules are capable of representing all the information implied by concept rules and decision implications ([Wu et al., 2009](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r16); Li and Wu, 2017).

As mentioned previously, granular rules are special concept rules and concept rules are special decision implications; therefore, we have ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m64](i/ch010.m64.png). A decision implication, however, might not be a concept rule or a granular rule, since its premise or consequence might not be an intent or a granular intent. It is thus essential to clarify the relationships between decision implications, concept rules and granular rules.

Concept Rule and Decision Implication

We will discuss the relationships between concept rules and decision implications in this section. Although concept rules are just special decision implications, from the perspective of decision implication logic, concept rules may be complete w.r.t. decision implications (see Definition 2.7). The following example, however, shows that this is not always true.

Now, let us explain Proposition 3.4 and Theorem 3.1 from the perspective of information loss. From this perspective, any decision implication ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m125](i/ch010.m125.png) can be viewed as a decision information extracted from K, by which one can make all decisions in B when all conditions in A occur. From this perspective, Proposition 3.4 says that if A→B is an information loss of ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m131](i/ch010.m131.png) w.r.t. ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m132](i/ch010.m132.png), then there must exist another information loss A→d(d∈B) of ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m133](i/ch010.m133.png) w.r.t. ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m134](i/ch010.m134.png); in other words, the reason for A→B being an information loss is that there exists an information loss A→d(d∈B). Thus, any information loss A→B can be reduced to a special information loss A→d. In conclusion, any decision implication in ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m177](i/ch010.m177.png) carries the decision information {a[1],a[2],a[5]}→{d[3]}, but ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m178](i/ch010.m178.png) does not.

The following proposition connects the condition with Theorem 3.1.

Proposition 3.6 Let ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m188](i/ch010.m188.png) be a decision context and A→d be a decision implication of K. Then the following statements are equivalent:

Since A[i] is an intent, by Proposition 3.1, ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m201](i/ch010.m201.png) is a concept rule, a contradiction with the condition that none of the decision implications in ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m202](i/ch010.m202.png) is a concept rule.□

Proposition 3.6 says that if there exists ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m203](i/ch010.m203.png) such that A[i] is an intent, we can find a concept rule ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m204](i/ch010.m204.png) from ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m205](i/ch010.m205.png), i.e., a concept rule that carries the decision information A→d. If, conversely, none of the decision implications in ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m206](i/ch010.m206.png) satisfies the condition, the definition of concept rule has filtered out all the decision implications in ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m207](i/ch010.m207.png), and no decision information concerning A→d is kept in ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m208](i/ch010.m208.png). In other words, it is the restrictions on decision implications imposed by the definition of concept rule that lead to information loss of ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m209](i/ch010.m209.png) w.r.t. ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m210](i/ch010.m210.png) and cause the incompleteness of concept rules w.r.t. decision implications.

Although we have proven the existence of information loss of ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m230](i/ch010.m230.png) w.r.t. ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m231](i/ch010.m231.png) and explored the underlying reason behind the information loss, it is not clear if the information loss is significant for practical applications. Since for each ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m261](i/ch010.m261.png) holds, and then we obtain ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m262](i/ch010.m262.png), thus ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m263](i/ch010.m263.png) by Proposition 2.3.□

The literature ([Zhai et al., 2015b](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r22)) has shown that a decision premise A must also be a minimal generator (Stumme et al., 2000) of the intent A^(CC). Generally speaking, however, an intent may own a lot of minimal generators and hence may not be a minimal generator of itself, as verified experimentally in ([Zhai et al., 2015a](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r21)). Thus, if a decision premise is an intent, the decision premise has to be a minimal generator of itself. Therefore, it is reasonable to conclude that nearly all decision premises may not be intents, and by Proposition 3.7, ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m264](i/ch010.m264.png) loses nearly all the decision implications in ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m265](i/ch010.m265.png), each of which contains the most abundant decision information of ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m266](i/ch010.m266.png), as illustrated in Example 3.8.

Comparatively, {a[4],a[5]}→{d[1],d[2]} contains more decision information than {a[2],a[4],a[5],a[6]}→{d[1],d[2]}, since {a[2],a[4],a[5],a[6]}→{d[1],d[2]} can be obtained by applying Augmentation to {a[4],a[5]}→{d[1],d[2]}.

In fact, all the decision premises except {a[2]} in [Table 3](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#t3) are not intents, and all the decision implications except {a[2]}→{d[1]} in ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m269](i/ch010.m269.png) are lost in ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m270](i/ch010.m270.png).

Similar to Proposition 3.6, Proposition 3.9 says that if there exists ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m353](i/ch010.m353.png) such that A[i] is a condition attribute granule, we can find a granular rule ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m354](i/ch010.m354.png) from ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m355](i/ch010.m355.png), which carries the decision information A→d and will be kept in ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m356](i/ch010.m356.png). DECISION IMPLICATION-BASED KNOWLEDGE RE-REPRESENTATION AND REASONING WITHIN INCOMPLETE FORMAL CONTEXT

Implication in Incomplete Formal Contexts

When incidence relations loss occurs in a formal context, the context is referred to as incomplete formal context, with being formally defined by a three-values context (P and R, 2000).

Definition 4.1 (P and R, 2000). Let M be a set of attributes. An incomplete formal context on M is a quadruple =(G,M,{+,-,?},I), where G is a set of objects, M is a set of attributes, and ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m489](i/ch010.m489.png) is a ternary relation with the following interpretation:

*   • (g,m,+)∈I: g has attribute m;
*   • (g,m,\-)∈I: g does not have attribute m;
*   • (g,m,?)∈I: it is unknown whether g has attribute m.

Example 4.1. An incomplete formal context is given in [Table 10](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#t10).

Table 10. An incomplete formal context

|  || a[1] || a[2] || a[3] || a[4] || a[5] |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| g[1] || 1 || 1 || ? || 0 || 0 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| g[2] || 1 || 0 || ? || 1 || 1 |
| g[3] || 1 || 1 || 1 || ? || 0 |
| g[4] || ? || 1 || 1 || 1 || 0 |
| g[5] || 1 || 1 || 0 || 0 || 0 |
| g[6] || 1 || 1 || 1 || 1 || ? |

Within incomplete formal contexts, the values of some attributes may be unknown for some objects, and we have to consider two possibilities for an incomplete entry “?”: this entry should be filled with either “1” or “0”. In this sense, an incomplete formal context can be translated into a complete one by filling the items “?” with “1” or “0”.

Definition 4.2 (P and R,2000). Let =(G,M,{+,-,?},I) be an incomplete formal contexts. We call the formal context [i]\=(G,M,I[i]) a completion (formal context) of , if

*   • (g,m,+)∈I implies (g,m)∈I[i];
*   • (g,m,\-)∈I implies (g,m)∉I[i];
*   • (g,m,?)∈I implies (g,m)∈I[i] or (g,m)∉I[i].

An incomplete formal context corresponds to multiple completions. Definition 4.3 gives two important completions of incomplete formal contexts.

Definition 4.3. Let K\=(G,M,{+,-,?},I) be an incomplete formal context. The minimal completion and the maximal completion are respectively defined as:

*   • [min]\={G,M,I[min]} where I[min]\={(g,m)|(g,m,+)∈I};
*   • [max]\={G,M,I[max]} where I[max]\= {(g,m)|(g,m+)∈I or (g,m,?)∈I}.

By Definition 4.3, [min] collects all necessary attributes of objects by replacing all “?” with “0”, while [max] gathers all possible attributes that objects might possess by replacing all “?” with “1”.

Remark 4.1. Here are some symbol denotations to be used in this chapter:

*   • The operator (.)^I conducted in [min] and [max] is respectively denoted as ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m490](i/ch010.m490.png) and ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m491](i/ch010.m491.png).
*   • For a set ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m492](i/ch010.m492.png), we denote ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m493](i/ch010.m493.png) and ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m494](i/ch010.m494.png).

Example 4.2 (Continuing Example 4.1). Consider the incomplete formal context in Example 4.1. [min] and [max] are respectively shown in [Table 11](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#t11) and [12](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#t12).

Table 11. The minimal completion [min]

|  || a[1] || a[2] || a[3] || a[4] || a[5] |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| g[1] || 1 || 1 || 0 || 0 || 0 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| g[2] || 1 || 0 || 0 || 1 || 1 |
| g[3] || 1 || 1 || 1 || 0 || 0 |
| g[4] || 0 || 1 || 1 || 1 || 0 |
| g[5] || 1 || 1 || 0 || 0 || 0 |
| g[6] || 1 || 1 || 1 || 1 || 0 |

Table 12. The maximal completion [max]

|  || a[1] || a[2] || a[3] || a[4] || a[5] |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| g[1] || 1 || 1 || 1 || 0 || 0 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| g[2] || 1 || 0 || 1 || 1 || 1 |
| g[3] || 1 || 1 || 1 || 1 || 0 |
| g[4] || 1 || 1 || 1 || 1 || 0 |
| g[5] || 1 || 1 || 0 || 0 || 0 |
| g[6] || 1 || 1 || 1 || 1 || 1 |

Differing from formal contexts where implications can be determined as holding or not, in an incomplete formal context, the lack of incidence relations may lead to uncertainty regarding whether implications hold in the data.

Example 4.3 (Continuing Example 4.1). Consider the incomplete formal context in Example 4.1. It is confirmed that {a[2],a[4]}→{a[3]} always holds in , regardless of the values represented by the symbol “?”.

On the other hand, there exist implications that cannot be verified as either holding or not. Consider the implication {a[2],a[4]}→{a[1],a[3]}. By [Table 10](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#t10), it holds for objects g[1], g[2], g[3], g[5], and g[6], while cannot be determined whether it holds for g[4], and we can only confirm that {a[2],a[4]}→{a[1],a[3]} holds in when g[4] has a[1].

There are also exists invalid implications . For example, {a[2]}→{a[3],a[4]} does not hold in since it is invalid for g[1].

The literature (P and R, 2000) evaluates the availability of implications based on their validity within completions. In other words, an implication necessarily holds in an incomplete formal context when it holds in all completions of the incomplete formal context. Similarly, an implication is considered potentially valid when it holds in at least one completion.

Definition 4.4 (P and R, 2000). Let =(G,M,{+,-,?},I) be an incomplete formal contexts. For a given implication A→B, we define:

*   1\. A→B is necessary in if and only if A→B holds in all completions of .
*   2\. A→B is in if and only if A→B holds in at least one completion of .

Proposition 4.1 provides the equivalent conditions for determining acceptable implications and necessary implications.

Proposition 4.1(P and R,2000). Let =(G,M,{+,-,?},I) be an incomplete formal context. Then,

*   1\. ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m495](i/ch010.m495.png) is necessary in if and only if ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m496](i/ch010.m496.png).
*   2\. A→B is acceptable in if and only if ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m497](i/ch010.m497.png).

acceptable implications and necessary implications are two categories of knowledge, with possessing varying degrees of knowledge validity; overall, both are available for practical applications, and can be effectively applied to decision-making and problem-solving in real-world scenarios. In this chapter, we intend to seek a more general representation of incomplete formal contexts. This will allow us to transform the process of knowledge acquisition from incomplete formal contexts into a more manageable task by utilizing the general representation.

Decision Implication-Based Knowledge Representation and Inference Rules

A straightforward approach to deducing necessary or acceptable implications involves applying the inference rules within formal contexts to both types of implications.

Example 4.4 (Continuing Examples 4.1). Take the incomplete formal context in Example 4.1 as an example. Let us consider a significant inference rule:

*   Pseudo-transitive:
    
    ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m498](i/ch010.m498.png)
    

in Armstrong's Axiom System ([Armstrong, 1974](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r1)), which initially applies to formal contexts in FCA ([Wille, 1982](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r15)). Take the acceptable implications {a[4]}→{a[3]} and {a[3]}→{a[2]} in [Table 10](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#t10) as an example. The application of Pseudo-transitive to {a[4]}→{a[3]} and {a[3]}→{a[2]} yields {a[4]}→{a[2]}, seemingly a logical progression. However, as we verified in [Table 10](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#t10), it is discovered that{a[4]}→{a[2]} is invalid within .

Example 4.4 shows that, as we intend to apply the inference rules in formal contexts to acceptable implications, an unexpected outcome emerges, i.e., the inference rules may lead to invalid results. This phenomenon arises from the incompatibility of acceptable implications; in other words, when these implications do not simultaneously exist in any completion, they fail to collaboratively work in the inference process. This section embarks on an exploration of decision implication-based knowledge representation and reasoning rules. This innovative approach offers a smoother, more effective solution to the challenges outlined above.

By the definitions of [max] and [min] (see Definition 4.3), can be easily reconstructed by considering the incidence relations in both [max] and [min]. That is to say, the pair ([max], [min]) stores all details in . Motivated by this discovery, we design a unique formal context:

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m499](i/ch010.m499.png) (3)

by connecting [max] and [min], where ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m500](i/ch010.m500.png) represents the placement of the set M of decision attributes to the right of an identical set of condition attributes. Similarly, ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m501](i/ch010.m501.png) signifies the appending of the set I[min] to the right of I[max]. Likewise, another formal context can be constructed:

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m502](i/ch010.m502.png) (4)

Indeed, ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m503](i/ch010.m503.png) and ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m504](i/ch010.m504.png), each comprising two components: [max] and [min], can be considered as unique decision contexts. While strictly speaking, the condition attributes and decision attributes of a decision context are distinct, in the case of ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m505](i/ch010.m505.png) and ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m506](i/ch010.m506.png), the condition attributes align with their corresponding decision attributes. This alignment allows us to loosely interpret them as a form of decision context. Within this framework, both necessary and acceptable implication can be re-presented through decision implication within ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m507](i/ch010.m507.png) and ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m508](i/ch010.m508.png), respectively.

Theorem 4.1. Let =(G,M,{+,-,?},I) be an incomplete formal context. Then,

*   1\. ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m509](i/ch010.m509.png) is necessary in if and only if A→B is a decision implication in ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m510](i/ch010.m510.png).
*   2\. A→B is acceptable in if and only if A→B is a decision implication in ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m511](i/ch010.m511.png)[²](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#fn2).

Proof. By Proposition 4.1, they are straightforward.□

Zhai et al. ([Zhai et al., 2014](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#r20)) proposed two inference rules Augmentation and Combination for deduction decision implications (see Formulas (1) and (2)). Proposition 4.2 extends the soundness of Augmentation and Combination to both necessary and acceptable implications, thereby establishing an inference pathway to obtain the two types of implications.

Theorem 4.2. Let =(G,M,{+,-,?},I) be an incomplete formal context. Then,

*   1\. If A→B is necessary in , ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m518](i/ch010.m518.png) and ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m519](i/ch010.m519.png), then A[1]→B[1] is necessary in .
*   2\. If A→B is acceptable in , ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m520](i/ch010.m520.png) and ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m521](i/ch010.m521.png), then A[1]→B[1] is acceptable in .
*   3\. If A→B and A[1]→B[1] are necessary in , then ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m522](i/ch010.m522.png) is necessary in .
*   4\. If A→B and A[1]→B[1] are acceptable in , then ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m523](i/ch010.m523.png) is acceptable in .

By ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m529](i/ch010.m529.png), and ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m530](i/ch010.m530.png) holds, i.e., A[1]→B[1] holds in by Definition 2.5. In conclusion, for any completion of , A[1]→B[1] holds in , meaning that A[1]→B[1] is necessary in .

(2) Similar to the proof of (1).

(3) By Definition 4.4, for any completion of , A→B and A[1]→B[1] holds in , and then ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m531](i/ch010.m531.png) and ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m532](i/ch010.m532.png) by Definition 2.5. In this case, by Proposition 4.1, ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m542](i/ch010.m542.png) is acceptable in .□

Although in the previous discussions in the chapter, necessary implications were restricted by having an empty intersection between the condition and consequence (as in Theorems 4.1 and 4.1), it can be observed from Proposition 4.2 that Augmentation and Combination break through such limitation.

We will discuss the reasons behind the applicability of Augmentation and Combination to necessary implications and acceptable implications based on incomplete formal contexts.

Firstly, Augmentation and Combination are applicable to necessary implications. This is due to the fact that necessary implications hold in all completions of the given incomplete formal context. When Augmentation and Combination are applied to necessary implications, it is equivalent to applying the inference rules to the corresponding implications in each completion formal context[³](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#fn3). Consequently, the implications derived through this process hold in all completions, maintaining their necessity within incomplete formal contexts. Secondly, due to the fact that acceptable implications hold in some completions of the given incomplete formal context, when Augmentation is applied to such an implication, the derived implication remains valid in those complete formal contexts. As a result, these implications also maintain their possibility within incomplete formal contexts.

Next, we will explain the reason behind the applicability of Combination to acceptable implications. The following conclusion is firstly presented.

Proposition 4.2. Let =(G,M,{+,-,?},I) be an incomplete formal context, and A[1]→B[1] and A[2]→B[2] be two acceptable implications of . If

![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m543](i/ch010.m543.png)

then A[1]→B[1] and A[2]→B[2] hold in some completions of .

Necessary Implication Basis and Acceptable Implication Basis

Section 4.2 applies the inference rule Augmentation and Combination for reasoning about necessary implications and acceptable implications. From a perspective of knowledge completeness, the derived necessary/acceptable implications can be considered as redundant knowledge within the data and therefore can be eliminated. Thus, in this section, we address the completeness and non-redundancy of defining sets of necessary implications and acceptable implications based on Augmentation and Combination, thereby obtaining compact sets of necessary implications and acceptable implications, i.e., necessary implication basis and acceptable implication basis.

However, we find that using only Augmentation and Combination cannot thoroughly eliminate the redundancy in implications, especially the implications with disjoint sets for premise and consequence.

Example 4.6 (Continuing Example 4.1). Take the incomplete formal context in Example 4.1 as an example. L= {{a[1]}→{a[1]}, {a[3],a[5]}→{a[1]}, {a[3],a[5]}→{a[1],a[3]}} is a set of necessary implications of .

Consider the implication {a[1]}→{a[1]}. Clearly, this implication holds trivially. However, from a decision-making perspective, such an implication is meaningless and redundant, and thus should be removed. Nevertheless, it can be verified that {a[1]}→{a[1]} cannot be derived from other implications in L, i.e., {a[3],a[5]}→{a[1]} and {a[3],a[5]}→{a[1],a[3]}, using Augmentation and Combination, preventing the removal of {a[1]}→{a[1]} from L.

Then, Take the implications {a[3],a[5]}→{a[1]} and {a[3],a[5]}→{a[1],a[3]} as an example. For {a[3],a[5]}→{a[1],a[3]}, the decision attribute a[3] in the consequence is redundant (as a[3] is already given in the premise). Therefore, for the sake of calculation and analysis efficiency, {a[3],a[5]}→{a[1],a[3]} should be deleted while retaining {a[3],a[5]}→{a[1]}. However, we find that using only Augmentation and Combination cannot achieve this effect.

Similar issues arise when applying Augmentation and Combination to acceptable implications, which will not be illustrated. To overcome the shortcomings of Augmentation and Combination, we introduce another inference rule[⁴](#979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.xhtml#fn4):

*   Reflexivity:
    
    ![Mathtype979-8-3693-1582-8.ch010.m572](i/ch010.m572.png)
    

Example 4.7 (Continuing Example 4.6). Consider the set of necessary implications L= {{a[1]}→{a[1]}, {a[3],a[5]}→{a[1]}, {a[3],a[5]}→{a[1],a[3]}} from Example 4.6. First, applying Reflexivity yields {a[1]}→{a[1]}, which can then be removed from L. Second, similarly applying Reflexivity leads to {a[3]}→{a[3]}, and by further utilizing Combination to {a[3],a[5]}→{a[1]} and {a[3]}→{a[3]}, we obtain {a[3],a[5]}→{a[1],a[3]}, leading to the removal of {a[3],a[5]}→{a[1],a[3]} from L. In summary, after eliminating the redundant implications, the set L simplifies to {a[3],a[5]}→{a[1]}.

Consequently, we define the completeness and non-redundancy of sets of necessary/acceptable implications based on the Augmentation, Combination and Reflexivity.

Definition 4.5. Let =(G,M,{+,-,?},I) be an incomplete formal context, and L be a set of necessary implications of . We define:

*   1\. L is said to be complete, if for any necessary implication A→B of , A→B can be deduced from L by applying Reflexivity, Augmentation and Combination.
*   2\. L: is said to be non-redundant, if for any A→B∈L, A→B cannot be deduced from L\\{A→B} by applying Reflexivity, Augmentation and Combination, and A→B is called non-redundant in L.

The completeness and non-redundancy of acceptable implication sets are defined in a similar way, and will not be repeated. We then research to construct a set of necessary/acceptable implications with completeness and non-redundancy.

CONCLUSION AND FUTURE WORK

This chapter conducts a comparative study of decision implication, concept rule and granular rule, and that of decision implication-based knowledge re-representation and reasoning within incomplete formal contexts, which includes the following contribution:

*   (1) It is exemplified that both concept rules and granular rules are not complete w.r.t. decision implications, and that granular rules are not complete w.r.t. concept rules. This implies that there exists information loss when studying decision implications by using only concept rules or granular rules, or when studying concept rules by using only granular rules.With the help of decision implication logic, we uncover the information loss by identifying the decision implications that cannot be deduced from concept rules or granular rules, and the concept rules that cannot be deduced from granular rules, and explore the underlying reason behind the information loss in concept rules and granular rules.
*   (2) We have identified that, while both necessary and acceptable implications can be represented within formal contexts, the inference rules, initial apply to implications within formal contexts, may yield invalid results when they are applied to acceptable implications.To address this issue, we have introduced specially constructed decision contexts that connect the minimal and maximal completions of incomplete formal contexts. These contexts facilitate the re-representation of both necessary and acceptable implications based on decision implications.
*   (3) We have extended the applicability of inference rules such as Augmentation and Combination, originally designed for decision implications, to necessary and acceptable implications. Additionally, we have introduced the Reflexivity inference rule to enhance redundancy elimination capabilities. The completeness and non-redundancy for sets of necessary/acceptable implications have been defined based on Augmentation, Combination, and Reflexivity. We constructed necessary implication basis and acceptable implication basis, which respectively serve as a compact representation of preserving all information implied in necessary/acceptable implications with minimal implications.

Future work will center on several meaningful aspects:

*   (1) Potential values exploration of concept rules and granular rules. We will judge whether concept rules or granular rules are complete w.r.t. decision implications in such applications is also a meaningful issue. In fact, it is also worthwhile to study and conduct some experiments to verify if the information loss in concept rules or granular rules exists or is important in some applications, and if it is the case, to find the approximate and optimal representation types of decision information.
*   (2) Inference system construction for concept rules and granular rules. It refers to the study concept rules logic, which includes semantical and syntactical aspects. The semantical aspect defines the soundness of a concept rule, and concept rule basis. The syntactical aspect accounts for the inference rules of concept rules, which are sound, complete and non-redundant w.r.t. the semantical aspect.
*   (3) Uncertain knowledge description in a logic perspective. The semantic investigates the soundness, completeness, and redundancy of implications and the construction of canonical basis. The syntactic focuses on inference rule with semantic consistency, taking cues from the inference rules applied in this chapter.
*   (4) Incompatible knowledge representation and reasoning. While it has been demonstrated that incompatible knowledge can yield acceptable implications (such as via Combination), there may still be incompatibilities between the derived knowledge. Our plan is to explore subsystems of compatible knowledge reasoning and select the appropriate subsystem based on practical scenarios.

|Chao Zhang - Editor|Chao Zhang, professor of Institute of Intelligent Information Processing, Shanxi University, his main study interests include data mining, granular computing and intelligent decision making. In recent years, he has published more than 80 papers including IEEE Transactions on Computational Social Systems, IEEE Transactions on Fuzzy Systems, IEEE Transactions on Consumer Electronics, Information Sciences, Information Fusion, International Journal of Approximate Reasoning, ACM Transactions on Asian and Low-Resource Language Information Processing, Computers in Industry, Applied Mathematical Modelling. Among them, one paper has been selected as “ESI highly cited paper”. He has published 3 academic monographs in national publishers. He has published 2 national invention patents. He has been awarded the first prize of Outstanding Achievements in Scientific Research in Institutions of Higher Learning in Shanxi Province, the second prize of Outstanding Achievement Award in Social Sciences in Shanxi Province, two Excellent Academic Paper Awards in Taiyuan City, ACM Excellent Doctoral Dissertation Award in Taiyuan Chapter, the best student paper award in CGCKD.0d401635-9d7c-4d50-a6d8-fb034415e45f

|Wentao Li - Editor| Wentao Li received the Ph.D. degree from the Department of Mathematics, Harbin Institute of Technology, Harbin, China, in 2019, and the M.Sc. degree from the School of Mathematics and Statistics, Chongqing University of Technology, Chongqing, China, in 2015. From 2016 to 2018, he was a joint Ph. D student with the University of Alberta, Edmonton, AB, Canada. He is currently an Associate Professor with the College of Artificial Intelligence, Southwest University, Chongqing, China. His current research interests include artificial intelligence, feature selection, and granular computing. In recent years, He has published over 30 articles including IEEE Transactions on Fuzzy Systems, IEEE Transactions on Neural Networks and Learning Systems, IEEE Transactions on Cybernetics, IEEE Transactions on Emerging Topics in Computational Intelligence, Fuzzy Sets and Systems, Information Sciences, Artificial Intelligence Review, and many others. Among them, 3 papers have been selected as “ESI hot paper” and “ESI highly cited paper”. He serves on editorial board of several international journals, and successfully held several Special Issues in journals of International Journal of Fuzzy Systems, Wireless Communications and Mobile Computing, Intelligent Automation & Soft Computing, and Frontiers in Neurorobotics. He also served as the reviewer of many journals, such as IEEE TFS, IEEE TNNLS, IEEE TCYB, IEEE TKDE, INS, KBS, AIRE, and granted the certificate of “Outstanding Reviewer” for Knowledge-Based Systems in 2018. aed6ac2a-1d54-4a57-8644-687be5380726

|Chunmao Jiang - Contributing Author|Chunmao Jiang is currently serving as a professor at the School of Computer Science and Mathematics at Fujian University of Technology, where he also mentors graduate students. With a Master of Science degree from Harbin Institute of Technology (2004) and a Doctor of Philosophy degree in Computer Science from Harbin Engineering University, Professor Jiang has developed a keen interest and expertise in granular computing, three-way decision theory, and cloud computing. As an active member of the academic community, he holds positions as an executive committee member of the CCF Committee on Open Source Development and a member of the CAAI Committee on Granular Computing and Knowledge Discovery. His research interests include intelligent information processing and decision-making, multi-granularity cloud computing, and the development of large-scale Chinese-Russian models. In recent years, Professor Jiang has led significant educational and research initiatives. He has been the principal investigator of one key provincial-level educational reform project and one general project. In the realm of scientific research, he has led and participated in more than ten projects funded by the National Natural Science Foundation of China, the National Social Science Fund of China, the China Postdoctoral Science Foundation, and various provincial grants. Professor Jiang's scholarly contributions are extensive, with over eighty publications in prestigious journals such as TFS, INS, KBS, and the Journal of Electronics, including more than thirty SCI-indexed articles. His dedication to advancing knowledge in his fields of interest underscores his commitment to both teaching and research excellence. 2b4c7b84-b430-4b2f-b3e7-b41ea069050c
